\label{alg:system}$$ The constant $\delta_0>0$ is fixed sufficiently small independently of $N$; the floor lemma below specifies the choice. The penalty $C_N$ is then chosen polynomially large. It is fixed before the truncation radius.

There are two parts of the continuation. First replace $K$ by $sK$, $0\leq s\leq1$, everywhere in $F,V,\mathcal T$, leaving the displayed source otherwise unchanged. At $s=0$ the trace equation implies $f=0$. In the second part retain $K=f=0$ and replace $\Xi$ by $\lambda\Xi$, $0\leq\lambda\leq1$. At $\lambda=0$ the sole solution is $Z=0$. We will prove uniform a priori estimates on the part of this family where $\min t\geq-\varepsilon$; the floor lemma excludes equality.

<a id="stmt-alg-trace-height"></a>
<div id="alg:trace-height" class="lemma">

<a id="alg:height-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Height and outer boundary gradient). *Every smooth solution of the trace equation in the first homotopy part satisfies, with constants independent of $N,R,s$, $$|h|\leq C_h,\qquad
 |h|\leq C\bigl(r^{-b_1}-R^{-b_1}\bigr)
 \quad(r_0\leq r\leq R),\qquad
 |\mathrm dh|\leq CR^{-b_1-1}\quad(r=R),
 \label{alg:height-bounds}$$ provided $R\geq2r_0$, where $r_0$ is fixed and $K=0$ on $r\geq r_0$. For $R\geq R_{\min}(N)$ these estimates imply $t>-\varepsilon$ on the outer sphere, without a prior assumption on the interior floor. The same assertions hold in the second homotopy part, where $h=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-trace-height"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-trace-height</summary>

<a id="alg:height-equation"></a>
*Proof.* In terms of $h$ the trace equation reads $$\frac{A_\chi^{ij}\nabla_i\nabla_jh}
 {\sqrt{(\eta/l)^2+|\mathrm dh|^2}}+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{A_\chi}(sK)=h.
 \label{alg:height-equation}$$ At a positive maximum, the first term is nonpositive and the second is bounded by $n\sup|K|$; a negative minimum is treated with the opposite sign. The supremum of $|K|$ on the filling is uniform in $N$. Taking $C_h>n\sup|K|$ proves the first assertion. The same argument with $K=0$ proves $h=0$ on the last homotopy part.

Let $B_R=C(r^{-b_1}-R^{-b_1})$. At a contact with $h-B_R$, the axis of $A_\chi$ is the radial-gradient direction, so that $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{A_\chi}\nabla^2B_R
 =Cb_1r^{-b_1-2}
   \bigl((b_1+1)\chi-k+O(r^{2-n})\bigr)<0.$$ The error is uniform for $0<\chi\leq1$; choose $r_0$ large enough using $b_1<k-1$. Choose $C$ so that $B_R\geq C_h$ on $r=r_0$ for every $R\geq2r_0$. At a positive interior maximum of $h-B_R$, the Hessian term in <a href="#alg:height-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:height-equation">[alg:height-equation]</a> would be negative while $h>B_R\geq0$, a contradiction. The lower barrier is $-B_R$. Their one-sided boundary derivatives give the last estimate in <a href="#alg:height-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:height-bounds">[alg:height-bounds]</a>; tangential derivatives there vanish.

On $r=R$, $Z=0$ and $l\leq e$. Hence $$t=-\frac{1}{2k}\log(1+l^2|\mathrm df|^2)
 \geq-\frac{1}{2k}\log\bigl(1+C\eta^{-2}R^{-2b_1-2}\bigr).$$ For example, in addition to $R\geq2r_0$, require $C\eta^{-2}R^{-2b_1-2}<e^{k\varepsilon}-1$. This gives $t>-\varepsilon/2$ on the outer sphere and defines a permissible $R_{\min}(N)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Exclusion of the floor

Define, for a symmetric tensor $B$, $$\mathcal S(B)=\tfrac12\bigl((\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{A_d}B)^2
                         -|B|_{A_d\otimes A_d}^2\bigr).$$

<a id="stmt-alg-floor-algebra"></a>
<div id="alg:floor-algebra" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (The stationary algebraic comparison). *There are constants $c_1>0$ and $C_k>0$, independent of $N$, such that at any point where $\mathrm dt=0$, <a id="alg:floor-quadratic"></a>$$\mathcal T-\mathcal S(H^f)
 \geq c_1\mathcal T
        -C_k(1+N)^2\bigl(vF^2+|K|^2\bigr).
 \label{alg:floor-quadratic}$$ The same constants apply with $K$ replaced by $sK$, $0\leq s\leq1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-floor-algebra"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-floor-algebra</summary>

<a id="alg:floor-subtraction"></a>
*Proof.* First put $B=H^f+K$. Its blocks give $$\mathcal S(B)=\frac{k-1}{2k}(F-\chi j)^2
 -\tfrac12|T^0|^2+dj(F-\chi j)-d|M|^2.$$ Subtracting this expression from <a href="#alg:remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:remainder">[alg:remainder]</a> at $\mathrm dt=0$ yields $$\begin{split}
 \mathcal T-\mathcal S(B)={}&|T^0|^2+(1+d)|M|^2+F^2/k\\
 &+\left(\frac{k-2}{k}\chi-d\right)Fj
 +\left(\chi(1+d)-\frac{k-1}{k}\chi^2\right)j^2.
\end{split}
\label{alg:floor-subtraction}$$ The last coefficient is at least $\chi$, while the absolute value of the mixed coefficient is at most $d$. Fix a small dimension-dependent $\delta_k>0$. If $(1+p)v\geq\delta_k$, Young’s inequality in the variables $F,\sqrt\chi j$, together with $$\frac{d^2}{\chi}=\frac{d(k+pv)}k\leq1+N/k,
 \qquad v^{-1}\leq(1+N)/\delta_k,$$ gives $\mathcal T-\mathcal S(B)\geq\tfrac12E_0
 -C_k(1+N)^2vF^2$. If $(1+p)v<\delta_k$, both $d$ and $\chi$ differ from one by at most $C_k\delta_k$. The quadratic form in <a href="#alg:floor-subtraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:floor-subtraction">[alg:floor-subtraction]</a> then differs, by at most $C_k\delta_k(|M|^2+F^2+j^2)$, from $$|T^0|^2+2|M|^2+(F-j)^2/k+j^2.$$ The least eigenvalue of the last $F,j$ form is at least $1/(k+2)$. Choose $\delta_k$ small enough to preserve half this lower bound. Combining the cases proves a fixed positive $E_0$ lower bound with the stated error.

Finally, polarization of $\mathcal S$ and $d^2/\chi\leq1+N/k$ give $$|\mathcal S(B)-\mathcal S(H^f)|
 \leq C_k\sqrt{1+N}\sqrt{E_0}|K|+C_k|K|^2.$$ Apply Young’s inequality, using half the positive $E_0$ lower bound, and then <a href="#alg:stationary-coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:stationary-coercivity">[alg:stationary-coercivity]</a>. This proves the result. All steps are unchanged on replacing $K$ by $sK$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-alg-floor"></a>
<div id="alg:floor" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Floor exclusion). *There is a choice $0<\delta_0<\min\{c_1,1\}/2$ and a polynomial $C_N$ such that, for $R\geq R_{\min}(N)$, no smooth solution in either part of the homotopy has $\min t=-\varepsilon$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-floor"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-floor</summary>

<a id="alg:floor-divergence"></a>
*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#alg:trace-height" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:trace-height">23</a> excludes a boundary minimum at this value. At an interior minimum $\mathrm dt=0$, $\nabla^2t\geq0$. View $\bar g$ as the metric of the graph $z=f(x)$ in the Riemannian warped product $g+l^2\mathrm dz^2$. At this point its second form is $H^f$. The ambient scalar curvature is $R_g-2p\Delta t$. With vertical unit vector $e_z=l^{-1}\partial_z$ the graph unit normal is $\sqrt d\,e_z-a_we$, and the ambient normal Ricci curvature equals $$v\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}_g(e,e)-vp\nabla^2t(e,e)-dp\Delta t.$$ The Gauss and conformal formulas therefore give the exact equality <a id="alg:floor-gauss"></a>$$\tfrac12 e^{2t}R_{\widehat g}
 =\tfrac12R_g-v\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}_g(e,e)+\mathcal S(H^f)
 -vp\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{e^\perp}\nabla^2t-k\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{A_d}\nabla^2t.
 \label{alg:floor-gauss}$$ The last two terms are nonpositive. Compare this upper bound with <a href="#alg:scalar-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:scalar-identity">[alg:scalar-identity]</a>, use $F=h$ and $w(F)=\eta a_w|\nabla f|$, and apply Lemma <a href="#alg:floor-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:floor-algebra">24</a>. It follows that $$u^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}V
 \geq c_1\mathcal T+\eta a_w|\nabla f|
                  -P_N(\rho_0+v\rho_1)
 \label{alg:floor-divergence}$$ for some polynomial $P_N$ independent of $R$ and the homotopy parameter. Here are all the error estimates used in this conclusion. The difference $\mu-R_g/2=(\tau^2-|K|^2)/2$, $J(w)$, and $F\tau$ is uniformly bounded on the enlarged core and vanishes on the end, so is bounded by $C\rho_0$. On the end $|\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}_g|=O(r^{-n})\leq C\rho_1$ because $2b_1<n$. The height barrier gives $vF^2\leq Cv\rho_1$ there; on the core its bound is absorbed by $C\rho_0$. The same bounds hold for $sK$, including its momentum density.

At $t=-\varepsilon$, $m_0=1$. Choose $\delta_0$ as in the statement, and then take, for instance, $C_N\geq P_N+c_*+1$. Subtracting the right side of <a href="#alg:system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:system">[alg:system]</a> from <a href="#alg:floor-divergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:floor-divergence">[alg:floor-divergence]</a> gives a strictly positive quantity, contradicting that equation. This choice is polynomial in $N$.

In the last homotopy part, the height comparison gives $K=f=0$, $t=Z$, $w=0$, and $\mathcal T=ks_p|\mathrm dt|^2$. At a floor minimum the left side is $k\Delta t\geq0$, whereas for $\lambda>0$ the right side is $\lambda(\rho-C_N\rho_0)<0$. For $\lambda=0$ the equation is $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}(ku(t)\nabla t)=0$ with zero boundary data. Testing by $t$ gives $\nabla t=0$, hence $t=0$. This finishes the homotopy check. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### High levels and graph Sobolev estimates

<a id="stmt-alg-distortion-proposition"></a>
<div id="alg:distortion-proposition" class="proposition">

<a id="alg:distortion"></a>
**Proposition 26** (Distortion bound). *With the choices above, every smooth homotopy solution on $\mathcal M_{N,R}$ with $\min t> -\varepsilon$ satisfies $$-\varepsilon<t\leq Z\leq\mathcal P_N,\qquad
 \ell\leq l\leq e,\qquad
 |f|+|\nabla f|\leq\exp(\mathcal P_N).
 \label{alg:distortion}$$ The constants are independent of $R\geq R_{\min}(N)$ and of the homotopy parameter.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-alg-distortion-proposition"><summary>Proof of stmt-alg-distortion-proposition</summary>

<a id="alg:highlevel-factors"></a>
<a id="alg:highlevel-sign"></a>
*Proof.* We give the full high-level argument for the first homotopy part and write $K$ for $sK$ throughout it. Put $q_D=\sqrt D$ and $a(t)=e^{(k-1)t}l$, so that $$u=a(t)q_D=l e^{kZ-t},\qquad
 \eta a_w|\nabla f|=\frac\eta l(q_D-q_D^{-1}),\qquad
 q_D=e^{k(Z-t)}.
 \label{alg:highlevel-factors}$$ The penalty can be nonzero only on $-\varepsilon<t<-\varepsilon+1/N$; its absolute size is at most $\mathcal P_N$. Equations <a href="#alg:profiles" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:profiles">[alg:profiles]</a> and <a href="#alg:highlevel-factors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:highlevel-factors">[alg:highlevel-factors]</a> show that a positive threshold $Z_0=\mathcal P_N$ can be chosen such that on $Z>Z_0$ $$\Xi\geq\delta_0\mathcal T+\rho
                +\tfrac12\delta_0\eta a_w|\nabla f|.
 \label{alg:highlevel-sign}$$ For example, on the penalty band it suffices to make $(\eta/e)(e^{kZ}-1)$ larger than $2C_N\sup(\rho_0+\rho_1)/\delta_0$; the logarithm of this requirement is bounded by $C(1+N)+C\log(1+C_N)$. Off the penalty band the assertion is immediate.

<a id="alg:highlevel-integral"></a>
Let $\zeta$ be smooth, nondecreasing, zero up to $Z_0$, and one above $Z_0+1$, with bounded derivative. Testing the divergence equation by $\zeta(Z)$ is legitimate since $Z=0$ at the outer boundary. Writing $A=A_\chi$ and $B_K=K(w,\cdot)$, we obtain $$\int\zeta(Z)u\Xi\,\mathrm dV_g
 =-\int\zeta'(Z)u
       \bigl(k|\mathrm dZ|_A^2+\langle\mathrm dZ,B_K\rangle_A\bigr)\,\mathrm dV_g
 \leq C\int_{\{Z_0<Z<Z_0+1\}\cap\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}K}u|K|^2\,\mathrm dV_g.$$ On the last set $u\leq e\exp(k(Z_0+1)+\varepsilon)$. The support has volume $O(1+N)$. Using <a href="#alg:highlevel-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:highlevel-sign">[alg:highlevel-sign]</a> proves $$\int_{\{Z>Z_0+1\}}u
    \bigl(\rho+\eta a_w|\nabla f|+\mathcal T\bigr)\,\mathrm dV_g
 \leq\exp(\mathcal P_N).
 \label{alg:highlevel-integral}$$

<a id="alg:seed-integral"></a>
Let $\mathcal K_N$ be the enlarged core and a fixed additional annulus containing $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}K$ in its interior. On this set $\rho\geq c>0$, uniformly. Since $l\geq\ell\geq\eta$ and $q_D\geq1$, $$u\bigl(\rho+\eta a_w|\nabla f|\bigr)
 =q_De^{(k-1)t}\bigl(l\rho+\eta(q_D-q_D^{-1})\bigr)
 \geq c'\eta e^{(k-1)t}q_D^2.$$ For any fixed $0<\alpha\leq k-1$, $t\geq-\varepsilon$ implies $e^{\alpha Z}q_D=e^{\alpha t}q_D^{1+\alpha/k}
 \leq C e^{(k-1)t}q_D^2$. On the complementary level set $Z\leq Z_0+1$, $q_D\leq\exp(k(Z_0+1+\varepsilon))$. Thus <a href="#alg:highlevel-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:highlevel-integral">[alg:highlevel-integral]</a> and the core volume bound give, in particular, the fixed-exponent seed $$\int_{\mathcal K_N}e^Z\sqrt D\,\mathrm dV_g
 \leq\exp(\mathcal P_N).
 \label{alg:seed-integral}$$ We chose $\alpha=1$; this exponent does not deteriorate as $N$ grows.

We next prove the local Sobolev inequality needed to turn <a href="#alg:seed-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:seed-integral">[alg:seed-integral]</a> into a supremum estimate. Use the graph of $f$ in the ordinary product $(\mathcal M_N\times\mathbb R,g+\mathrm dz^2)$. Over a base patch its measure and inverse metric are $$\mathrm dV_\Gamma=S_f\,\mathrm dV_g,\qquad
 S_f=\sqrt{1+|\nabla f|^2},\qquad
 A_\Gamma=g^{-1}-(1-S_f^{-2})e\otimes e.$$ Both $\mathrm dV_\Gamma$ and $A_\Gamma$ are comparable to $\mathrm d\mu_D=\sqrt D\,\mathrm dV_g$ and $A_\chi$, respectively, with $\exp(\mathcal P_N)$ factors. In fact $$\min\{1,l\}\leq q_D/S_f\leq\max\{1,l\},\qquad
 \frac{S_f^{-2}}\chi
 =\frac{1+l^2|\nabla f|^2}{1+|\nabla f|^2}\frac{k+pv}{k}.$$ The second ratio lies between $\ell^2$ and $e^2(1+N/k)$.

<a id="alg:graph-mean-curvature"></a>
The scalar mean curvature of this graph in the product is $$H_\Gamma=\frac{q_D}{lS_f}
  \left(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(T-K|_{e^\perp})+S_f^{-2}(j-K(e,e))\right).$$ The coefficients satisfy $$\frac{q_D}{lS_f}\leq\max\{1,l^{-1}\},\qquad
 \frac{q_D}{lS_f}\frac{S_f^{-2}}{\sqrt d}
 =\frac{q_D^2}{lS_f^3}\leq\max\{l,l^{-1}\}.$$ Consequently <a href="#alg:coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:coercivity">[alg:coercivity]</a> gives $$|H_\Gamma|\leq\exp(\mathcal P_N)(1+\sqrt{\mathcal T}).
 \label{alg:graph-mean-curvature}$$ Notice that this uses only $\sqrt d\,j$ and the transverse Hessian block, not an unweighted bound for the whole Hessian.

For clarity concerning the ambient geometry in this step, each of the finitely many fixed base models used in Lemma <a href="#alg:filling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:filling">20</a> can be extended beyond the patch to a closed smooth manifold. Choose a smooth Euclidean isometric embedding of that fixed manifold (Nash 1956) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Nash1956](#ref-Nash1956)], and take its product with the line. The bound on its second fundamental form is fixed; it is the same on all translated copies along a cylinder. Its contribution to the Euclidean mean curvature vector of the graph is at most $n$ times that bound, because the horizontal projection of a graph unit vector has length at most one. Thus <a href="#alg:graph-mean-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:graph-mean-curvature">[alg:graph-mean-curvature]</a> holds also for the Euclidean mean curvature vector. A compactly supported base test lifts to a compactly supported graph test for each individual smooth $f$.

<a id="alg:graph-sobolev"></a>
The Michael–Simon inequality (Michael and Simon 1973) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[MichaelSimon1973](#ref-MichaelSimon1973)], applied to $|\varphi|^{2(n-1)/(n-2)}$ and followed by Hölder’s inequality, therefore gives $$\|\varphi\|_{L^{2n/(n-2)}(\mathrm d\mu_D)}^2
 \leq\exp(\mathcal P_N)
 \int\bigl(|\mathrm d\varphi|_{A_\chi}^2
                    +(1+\mathcal T)\varphi^2\bigr)\,\mathrm d\mu_D
 \label{alg:graph-sobolev}$$ for compactly supported tests in each patch. The Euclidean theorem has no slope or graph-area hypothesis. The comparisons just established check precisely the metric and mean-curvature terms used here.

<a id="alg:weighted-energy"></a>
It remains to supply a compatible energy estimate. Globally $\Xi\geq\delta_0\mathcal T-\mathcal P_N$. Also $$\mathrm d\log a=(k-1+p)\mathrm dt,\qquad
 |\mathrm d\log a|_{A_\chi}\leq\mathcal P_N\sqrt{\mathcal T}$$ by <a href="#alg:coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:coercivity">[alg:coercivity]</a>. Test the divergence equation by $\psi^2e^{2sZ}/a$, where $\psi$ is a compactly supported base cutoff. The weight $a$ cancels from $u=a\sqrt D$. After moving the positive principal term to the left, this gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int\psi^2e^{2sZ}
       (\delta_0\mathcal T+2ks|\mathrm dZ|_A^2)\,\mathrm d\mu_D\\
 &\quad\leq\mathcal P_N\int\psi^2e^{2sZ}\,\mathrm d\mu_D
 +\int e^{2sZ}\psi^2\langle\mathrm d\log a,k\mathrm dZ+B_K\rangle_A\,\mathrm d\mu_D\\
 &\qquad-2s\int e^{2sZ}\psi^2\langle\mathrm dZ,B_K\rangle_A\,\mathrm d\mu_D
 -2\int e^{2sZ}\psi\langle\mathrm d\psi,k\mathrm dZ+B_K\rangle_A\,\mathrm d\mu_D.
\end{aligned}$$ The logarithmic-weight terms cost at most $\delta_0\psi^2\mathcal T/2+
 \mathcal P_N\psi^2(|\mathrm dZ|_A^2+|K|^2)$ by Young’s inequality. The term multiplied by $s$ costs a fixed fraction of $ks\psi^2|\mathrm dZ|_A^2$ plus $Cs\psi^2|K|^2$. The cutoff terms cost another such fraction plus $C(1+s^{-1})|\mathrm d\psi|_g^2+C\psi^2|K|^2$. It follows, on choosing a polynomial $s_*(N)$ sufficiently large, that for every $s\geq s_*(N)$, $$\int\psi^2e^{2sZ}
      (\mathcal T+s|\mathrm dZ|_{A_\chi}^2)\,\mathrm d\mu_D
 \leq\mathcal P_N(1+s)
       \int e^{2sZ}(\psi^2+|\mathrm d\psi|_g^2)\,\mathrm d\mu_D.
 \label{alg:weighted-energy}$$ All absorption thresholds are polynomial in $N$. No derivative of $A_\chi$ is estimated in this test.

Set $W=e^Z$, $\kappa_*=n/(n-2)$, and $p_0=2s_*(N)>1$. Insert $\varphi=\psi e^{sZ}$ into <a href="#alg:graph-sobolev" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:graph-sobolev">[alg:graph-sobolev]</a> and use <a href="#alg:weighted-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:weighted-energy">[alg:weighted-energy]</a>. For two nested base patches whose gap is $b\in(0,1]$, the resulting estimate, with $p=2s\geq p_0$, is <a id="alg:moser-step"></a>$$\|W\|_{L^{p\kappa_*}(\mathrm{inner},\mathrm d\mu_D)}
 \leq\bigl[\exp(\mathcal P_N)(1+p)^3b^{-2}\bigr]^{1/p}
          \|W\|_{L^p(\mathrm{outer},\mathrm d\mu_D)}.
 \label{alg:moser-step}$$ Iteration with $p_i=p_0\kappa_*^i$ and gaps proportional to $b2^{-i}$ is legitimate: both $\sum_i1/p_i$ and $\sum_i i/p_i$ converge. It gives <a id="alg:moser-sup"></a>$$\sup_{\mathrm{inner}}W
 \leq A_Nb^{-C_n}\|W\|_{L^{p_0}(\mathrm{outer},\mathrm d\mu_D)},
 \qquad A_N\leq\exp(\mathcal P_N).
 \label{alg:moser-sup}$$

We record explicitly why starting at the polynomially large $p_0$ does not spoil the bound obtained from <a href="#alg:seed-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:seed-integral">[alg:seed-integral]</a>. Let $J_*\geq1$ bound that integral and take increasing patches with successive gaps $b_j=b_*2^{-j-1}$, all inside a fixed larger patch. If $m_j=\log\max\{1,\sup W\}$ on patch $j$, interpolation gives $$\|W\|_{p_0}\leq(\sup W)^{1-1/p_0}J_*^{1/p_0},\qquad
 m_j\leq a+cj+(1-1/p_0)m_{j+1},$$ where $a=\log A_N+(\log J_*)/p_0+C_n\log(2/b_*)$ and $c=C_n\log2$ can be increased to be positive. The supremum on the largest compact patch is finite for each individual smooth solution. Iterating the recurrence and letting its length tend to infinity thus gives $$m_0\leq p_0a+c(p_0^2-p_0)\leq\mathcal P_N.$$ This last estimate is polynomial even though $1-1/p_0$ tends to one. The patches covering $\mathcal K_N$ have uniform sizes and geometry, so the conclusion is uniform on that whole set.

Outside $\mathcal K_N$ one has $K=0$ on a neighborhood. A larger interior maximum of $Z$ above $Z_0$ would have $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}V=ku\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}_{A_\chi}\nabla^2Z\leq0$, contrary to <a href="#alg:highlevel-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:highlevel-sign">[alg:highlevel-sign]</a>; the outer boundary has $Z=0$. This proves the global upper bound for $Z$ in the first homotopy part. In the last part $K=f=0$, $t=Z$, and the same high-maximum argument applies whenever $\lambda>0$. At $\lambda=0$ the solution is zero, as already proved.

Finally $t\leq Z$ follows from the implicit relation, $q_D=e^{k(Z-t)}\leq\exp(\mathcal P_N)$ follows from the floor, and $|\nabla f|\leq q_D/l\leq q_D/\ell$. Lemma <a href="#alg:trace-height" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:trace-height">23</a> gives $|f|\leq C_h/\eta$. These are the remaining assertions of <a href="#alg:distortion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:distortion">[alg:distortion]</a>. ◻

</details>

The estimates in this section are statements about every smooth solution, uniformly in the outer truncation, and are therefore available before continuation or exhaustion. Their parameter order is essential: $\delta_0$ is fixed first, $C_N$ is a polynomial penalty, and the distortion bounds are exponential in a polynomial. The subsequent existence proof may use unrestricted constants at a fixed $N$, while the later mass estimate will use only the polynomial losses proved here.

<a id="pde:section"></a>
### The coupled elliptic construction and its limiting geometry

We now solve the system of Section <a href="#alg:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:section">4</a>. Its preceding estimates control the unknowns and their first derivatives, but the trace operator has a coefficient whose modulus of continuity is not initially controlled. We prove the particular gradient estimate needed for that operator. This estimate, a scalar potential argument for the second equation, and continuation give smooth solutions. We then establish the end behavior, mass cost, and height barriers that keep the region requiring charged curvature control on the original exterior. No charged solvability theorem is used.

The prepared data, dimension, and $\varepsilon>0$ are fixed first, and $N$ is subsequently chosen sufficiently large. We use <a href="#alg:definitions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:definitions">[alg:definitions]</a> and <a href="#alg:system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:system">[alg:system]</a>, including $$k=n-1,\qquad \ell=e^{-N\varepsilon},\qquad
 \eta=\ell^{3/2},\qquad h=\eta f.$$ The notation $\mathcal P_N$ denotes a possibly changing polynomial in $1+N$, with coefficients depending on the fixed data, dimension, and $\varepsilon$. Local elliptic constants may depend arbitrarily on fixed $N$. They are not used as uniform constants in the limit $N\to\infty$.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### A gradient estimate without a coefficient modulus

<a id="stmt-pde-special-gradient"></a>
<div id="pde:special-gradient" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27** (The special scalar gradient estimate). *Let $g$ be a smooth uniformly positive metric on a coordinate ball, or on a smooth coordinate half-ball with a distinguished boundary face, with bounded smooth coordinate geometry. Suppose an individually smooth function $z$ satisfies <a id="pde:special-equation"></a>$$\left(g^{-1}-(1-\chi)e_z\otimes e_z\right):\nabla^2 z=G,
 \qquad e_z=\frac{\nabla z}{|\nabla z|_g},
 \qquad c\le\chi\le1,
 \label{pde:special-equation}$$ where $c>0$, $|\nabla z|_g\le M$, and $|G|\le C$. The axis at a gradient zero may be chosen arbitrarily. The coefficient $\chi$ may be measurable, with no assumed modulus of continuity. In the half-ball case assume a constant value of $z$ on the distinguished face.*

<a id="pde:hessian-morrey"></a>
*There are $\alpha\in(0,1)$ and uniform local $C^\alpha$ bounds for $\nabla z$, in the interior or up to that face. On smaller balls or half-balls one also has $$\int_{B_r}|\nabla^2z|_g^2\,\mathrm dV_g
 \le C_1r^{n-2+2\alpha},\qquad 0<r<r_1.
 \label{pde:hessian-morrey}$$ Constants depend on $n,c,M,C$, the coordinate geometry and localization, but not on the modulus of $\chi$ or higher derivatives of the individual solution.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pde-special-gradient"><summary>Proof of stmt-pde-special-gradient</summary>

*Proof.* We give the boundary reduction and both improvement arguments.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### A local Hessian estimate.

<a id="pde:cordes-local"></a>
Normalize the metric to the identity at a ball center and restrict to a sufficiently small ball. The coordinate principal matrix $a$ then satisfies $$|I-a|_{\mathrm F}\le1-c/2,$$ where the norm is Frobenius; connection terms are lower-order terms. The full-space operator $D^2\Delta^{-1}$, with the Frobenius Hessian norm, has $L^2$ norm one by Fourier transformation. Its boundedness on nearby $L^s$ spaces and interpolation give $s_0>2$ for which its norm times $1-c/2$ is less than one. Absorbing the perturbation from the Laplacian and localizing therefore gives, for $s=2,s_0$, $$\|D^2Y\|_{L^s(B_r)}
 \le C_s\left(\|a:D^2Y\|_{L^s(B_{2r})}
                 +r^{-2}\|Y\|_{L^s(B_{2r})}\right).
 \label{pde:cordes-local}$$ The cutoff commutator contains $r^{-1}DY$ and $r^{-2}Y$. The interpolation estimate $\|DY\|_s\le\delta r\|D^2Y\|_s+C_\delta r^{-1}\|Y\|_s$, and absorption on nested balls, remove the former. This argument uses only measurable principal coefficients. The Laplacian estimates are the classical singular-integral bounds (Calderón and Zygmund 1952; Gilbarg and Trudinger 2001) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[CalderonZygmund1952](#ref-CalderonZygmund1952), [GilbargTrudinger2001](#ref-GilbargTrudinger2001)].

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### Reflection and its flux correction.

Subtract the constant face value. In Gaussian coordinates write $$g=\mathrm dt^2+h_{ab}(y,t)\,\mathrm dy^a\mathrm dy^b,\qquad t\ge0.$$ Extend $z$ oddly, $h$ and $\chi$ evenly, and $G$ oddly across $t=0$. For $R=\operatorname{diag}(I,-1)$, the reflected gradient and Hessian are $-R\nabla z$ and $-R^T(\nabla^2z)R$, while the principal matrix is $RaR$. Thus the equation holds almost everywhere. Tangential derivatives of the face value vanish, so the extension is individually $C^1$ and $W^{2,s}$ for finite $s$. The reflected metric is Lipschitz and smooth on each side, sufficient for <a href="#pde:cordes-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:cordes-local">[pde:cordes-local]</a>.

There is one boundary contribution when differentiating the gradient norm. Put $$p_z=\nabla z,\quad U_z=|p_z|_g^2/2,\quad
 A=g^{-1}-(1-\chi)e_z\otimes e_z,\quad
 \mathcal F=(\nabla^2z-(\Delta_gz)g)\nabla z.$$ The equation and the contracted Hessian identity give <a id="pde:gradient-flux"></a><a id="pde:bochner-flux"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 A\,\mathrm dU_z-Gp_z&=\mathcal F,
 \label{pde:gradient-flux}\\
 \operatorname{div}_g\mathcal F
 &=|\nabla^2z|_g^2-(\Delta_gz)^2+\operatorname{Ric}_g(p_z,p_z)
 \label{pde:bochner-flux}
\end{aligned}$$ on each smooth side. Neither formula differentiates $\chi$. Let $q=z_t(y,0)$, $\mathrm{II}_{ab}=\tfrac12\partial_t h_{ab}(y,0)$, $H=\operatorname{tr}_h\mathrm{II}$, and $J=\sqrt{\det h}$. Constant face data give $(\nabla^2z)_{ab}=q\mathrm{II}_{ab}$. Thus the normal traces of $\mathcal F$ are $-Hq^2$ and $+Hq^2$. The coordinate divergence of $J\mathcal F$ has the plane density $-2J(y,0)Hq^2\delta_{\{t=0\}}$. It is canceled by <a id="pde:face-correction"></a>$$\mathcal C
 =2J(y,0)H(y)q(y)^2\,\mathbf1_{\{t>0\}}\partial_t.
 \label{pde:face-correction}$$ This vector has only a normal component. Its divergence differentiates only in $t$, and requires no tangential derivative of $q$. For each localized ball, extend the face density $2J(y,0)H(y)q(y)^2$ by zero in the tangential variables outside the portion of the face lying in that ball. Since $\mathcal C$ has only a normal component, this localization creates no additional distributional divergence and its bound uses only the gradient bound on that face portion.

<a id="pde:deficit-inequality"></a>
Normalize $|p_z|_g\le1$ and set $s=1-|p_z|_g^2$. The corrected flux is $$JA\,Ds+B=-2(J\mathcal F+\mathcal C),\qquad
 B=2JGp_z-4J(y,0)Hq^2\mathbf1_{\{t>0\}}\partial_t.$$ Convexity gives $$(\Delta_gz)^2
 =\big((1-\chi)\nabla^2z(e_z,e_z)+G\big)^2
 \le(1-\chi)|\nabla^2z|_g^2+G^2/\chi.$$ Consequently, in distributions, $$\operatorname{div}(JA\,Ds+B)
 \le-2cJ|\nabla^2z|_g^2
       +2J\big(G^2/c+|\operatorname{Ric}_g||p_z|_g^2\big).
 \label{pde:deficit-inequality}$$ In an interior chart take $\mathcal C=0$. Radius-$r$ rescaling multiplies face curvature by $r$, smooth-side curvature by $r^2$, and the forcing by $r/M_1$ if gradients are divided by $M_1$. Thus the additional bounded flux is small under the stated small-error rescaling. The construction also applies to balls centered near the reflected face.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Gradient drop or affine entry.

Fix $r_*\in(0,1/32)$ and $b_0>0$. There exist $k_*\in(r_*,1)$ and a small error threshold such that a normalized solution on $B_1$, with $|\nabla z|_g\le1$, satisfies either <a id="pde:gradient-drop"></a><a id="pde:affine-entry"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &\sup_{B_{r_*}}|\nabla z|_g\le k_*,
 \label{pde:gradient-drop}\\
 &\|z-L\|_{L^\infty(B_{r_*})}\le b_0r_*,
 \qquad \tfrac12\le|DL|\le2
 \label{pde:affine-entry}
\end{aligned}$$ for some affine $L$. Small errors comprise forcing, metric and connection errors, smooth-side curvature, and reflected-face curvature. Both $k_*$ and the threshold may depend on $b_0$.

Otherwise choose errors tending to zero, with the gradient supremum on $B_{r_*}$ tending to one and without affine entry. Normalize $z_j(0)=0$. Equation <a href="#pde:cordes-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:cordes-local">[pde:cordes-local]</a> gives uniform local $W^{2,2}$ bounds and hence uniform local $L^2$ bounds on $Ds_j$, where $s_j=1-|\nabla z_j|_{g_j}^2$. Equation <a href="#pde:deficit-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:deficit-inequality">[pde:deficit-inequality]</a> reads $$\operatorname{div}(a_jDs_j+B_j)
 \le-\kappa|D^2z_j|^2+\epsilon_j,\qquad
 \|B_j\|_\infty+\epsilon_j\longrightarrow0$$ with uniform ellipticity and $\kappa>0$. Weak Harnack, after discarding the Hessian term, gives $s_j\to0$ in measure on $B_{1/2}$, since $\inf_{B_{r_*}}s_j\to0$.

For completeness, convergence of $Ds_j$ needs a separate test. With a cutoff $\zeta$ supported in $B_{1/2}$, test the inequality by $\zeta^2(b-s_j)_+$, $0<b<1$. Ellipticity and Young give <a id="pde:lower-truncation"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{\{s_j<b\}}\zeta^2|Ds_j|^2
 \le C\bigg(&b^2\int|D\zeta|^2+\int\zeta^2|B_j|^2
 \nonumber\\
 &+b\int\zeta|D\zeta||B_j|
   +\epsilon_j b\int\zeta^2\bigg).
 \label{pde:lower-truncation}
\end{aligned}$$ Its limsup on a smaller compact ball is at most $Cb^2$. On $\{s_j\ge b\}$, measure convergence and the uniform $L^2$ gradient bound make the $L^1$ norm of $Ds_j$ tend to zero. On $\{s_j<b\}$, the limsup is at most $Cb$. Sending $b$ to zero gives local $L^1$ convergence of $Ds_j$. A fixed cutoff in the full deficit inequality now yields $D^2z_j\to0$ in $L^2(B_{1/3})$. Poincaré makes the gradients converge to a constant vector, of length one because $s_j\to0$. Uniform Lipschitz control gives uniform convergence on smaller balls to the corresponding affine function, a contradiction. Here and below we use the usual inhomogeneous weak Harnack and scalar divergence estimates (Gilbarg and Trudinger 2001) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[GilbargTrudinger2001](#ref-GilbargTrudinger2001)].

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### Improvement near a nonzero affine function.

<a id="pde:affine-improvement"></a>
There are $\alpha_2>0$, $\lambda\in(0,1/8)$, and sufficiently small $b_0,\delta'>0$ with this property: if $$\|z-L\|_{L^\infty(B_1)}\le b\le b_0,\qquad
 \tfrac14\le|DL|\le4,$$ the gradient is uniformly bounded, and metric, connection and forcing errors are at most $b\delta'$, then an affine $L'$ satisfies $$\|z-L'\|_{L^\infty(B_\lambda)}
 \le b\lambda^{1+\alpha_2},\qquad |DL'-DL|\le Cb.
 \label{pde:affine-improvement}$$ Indeed $Y=(z-L)/b$ has bounded supremum and bounded local $W^{2,s_0}$ norm by <a href="#pde:cordes-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:cordes-local">[pde:cordes-local]</a>. The normalized connection term is small because it uses the original gradient bound before division by $b$. As $b_0,\delta'\to0$, $Dz-DL=bDY\to0$ locally in measure. Freeze only the axis at $e_0=DL/|DL|$, leaving $\chi$ measurable. Bounded coefficient differences converging in measure, and the $L^{s_0}$ Hessian bound, give <a id="pde:axis-residual"></a>$$\|\Delta_{e_0^\perp}Y+\chi\partial_{e_0}^2Y\|_{L^2(B_{3/4})}
 \longrightarrow0.
 \label{pde:axis-residual}$$ The Dirichlet Laplace inverse on the convex ball $B_{3/4}$ has $\|D^2v\|_2\le\|\Delta v\|_2$, by integration by parts. Thus the frozen operator has a zero-Dirichlet inverse in $W^{2,2}$ by a contraction of norm at most $1-c$. Subtract the resulting small correction to obtain $Y_0$, uniformly bounded in $W^{2,2}$, with $$\Delta_{e_0^\perp}Y_0+\chi\partial_{e_0}^2Y_0=0.$$

The derivative $\zeta_0=\partial_{e_0}Y_0\in W^{1,2}$ satisfies <a id="pde:frozen-derivative"></a>$$\operatorname{div}\left[
 (I-e_0\otimes e_0+\chi e_0\otimes e_0)D\zeta_0\right]=0
 \label{pde:frozen-derivative}$$ in distributions. Differentiation acts on the $L^2$ flux $\chi\partial_{e_0}\zeta_0$; no classical derivative of $\chi$ is being asserted. Uniform scalar divergence estimates give $\zeta_0\in C^{\alpha_1}$ for some $\alpha_1\in(0,1)$. Caccioppoli after subtraction of a local value gives $$\int_{B_r}|D\zeta_0|^2\le Cr^{n-2+2\alpha_1}.$$ The source in $\Delta Y_0=(1-\chi)\partial_{e_0}\zeta_0$ therefore has $L^1$ mass at most $Cr^{n-1+\alpha_1}$. The gradient of its localized Newton potential is $C^{\alpha_1}$: for points separated by $a$, near annuli of radius $R\le a$ contribute $CR^{\alpha_1}$, and the far-field gradient difference contributes $CaR^{\alpha_1-1}$ for $R\ge a$. Both dyadic sums converge. Subtracting this potential leaves a harmonic function on a smaller ball. All first derivatives of $Y_0$ thus have a uniform Hölder bound.

The unfrozen $Y$ has a uniform interior Hölder modulus by the scalar Krylov–Safonov estimate with bounded forcing (Gilbarg and Trudinger 2001, sec. 9.8) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[GilbargTrudinger2001](#ref-GilbargTrudinger2001)]; see also (Mooney 2019) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Mooney2019](#ref-Mooney2019)] for the homogeneous mechanism. The $L^2$-small correction $Y-Y_0$ is consequently small uniformly on a smaller ball, using the uniform moduli of both terms. Taylor approximation of $Y_0$, first choosing $0<\alpha_2<\alpha_1$ and small $\lambda$, and then $b_0,\delta'$, proves <a href="#pde:affine-improvement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:affine-improvement">[pde:affine-improvement]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### Iteration and Hessian growth.

At each center begin with a fixed gradient normalization and sufficiently small radius for both error thresholds. On each gradient drop reduce the radius by $r_*$ and the normalization by $k_*$. Since $r_*/k_*<1$, normalized forcing stays small; geometric errors also decrease. If affine entry never occurs, the approximation by a constant has order $r^{1+\alpha_d}$, where $\alpha_d=\log k_*/\log r_*>0$.

After affine entry rescale its smaller ball to unit size and keep the same gradient normalization. Iterate <a href="#pde:affine-improvement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:affine-improvement">[pde:affine-improvement]</a> at radii $\lambda^i$, with errors $b_i=b_0\lambda^{i\alpha_2}$. Only constants, not affine functions, are subtracted when rescaling the structural equation, so its axis remains that of the actual gradient. Forcing and geometric errors decrease like $\lambda^i$, faster than $b_i$. The slope increments $Cb_i$ are summable; small $b_0$ keeps slopes in $[1/4,4]$. Combining the two regimes gives uniform affine approximation of order $r^{1+\alpha}$, also across the entry scale, for $\alpha\le\min(\alpha_d,\alpha_2)$. The affine Campanato criterion gives the gradient Hölder estimate. The same iteration applies in corrected reflected charts.

Finally subtract the first affine jet in <a href="#pde:cordes-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:cordes-local">[pde:cordes-local]</a>. Its remainder is $O(r^{1+\alpha})$, and the forcing and connection terms are bounded. The squared $s=2$ estimate gives $$\int_{B_r}|D^2z|^2\le C(r^n+r^{n-2+2\alpha}),$$ which implies <a href="#pde:hessian-morrey" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:hessian-morrey">[pde:hessian-morrey]</a>, with equivalent covariant norms and the same argument on half-balls. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### The second equation and coupled regularity

<a id="stmt-pde-natural-growth"></a>
<div id="pde:natural-growth" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28** (A bounded solution with natural gradient growth). *On a ball suppose a bounded, individually smooth function $Z$ solves $$-\operatorname{div}(A\,DZ)=\operatorname{div}B+F,$$ where $A$ is symmetric, bounded and uniformly elliptic, $B$ is bounded, and $$|F|\le a_0|DZ|^2+\mu,\qquad
 \mu(B_r(x))\le C_0 r^{n-2+\alpha_0}$$ for some $\alpha_0\in(0,1)$, uniformly at the relevant centers. Here $\mu$ can be a nonnegative measure; in the application its density is individually bounded. Then $Z$ has a uniform local Hölder modulus. The same conclusion holds up to a constant Dirichlet face if the equation can be reflected with bounded coefficients, bounded divergence flux, and the same source growth.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pde-natural-growth"><summary>Proof of stmt-pde-natural-growth</summary>

<a id="pde:exponential-subsolutions"></a>
*Proof.* Choose a fixed large $L$ and set $Q_\pm=e^{\pm LZ}$. The distributional chain rule gives $$\begin{aligned}
 -\operatorname{div}(A\,DQ_\pm)
 ={}&\operatorname{div}(\pm Le^{\pm LZ}B)\\
 &+e^{\pm LZ}\big(\pm LF-L^2 A\,DZ\cdot DZ-L^2B\cdot DZ\big).
\end{aligned}$$ If the lower ellipticity bound is $\lambda_0$, choose $L>2a_0/\lambda_0$. Young’s inequality absorbs the bounded-flux cross term into the remaining negative gradient square. The boundedness of $Z$ gives $$-\operatorname{div}(A\,DQ_\pm)
 \le\operatorname{div}B_\pm+C(\mathrm dx+\mu),
 \qquad \|B_\pm\|_\infty\le C.
 \label{pde:exponential-subsolutions}$$ The matrix on the left is the same for both signs.

On $B_r$, let $w_\pm$ be the zero-Dirichlet potential of the right side of <a href="#pde:exponential-subsolutions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:exponential-subsolutions">[pde:exponential-subsolutions]</a>. Scalar Dirichlet Green estimates for bounded measurable uniformly elliptic coefficients give <a id="pde:green-potential"></a>$$\|w_\pm\|_\infty\le C(r+r^{\alpha_0}).
 \label{pde:green-potential}$$ Here is the dependence on the source hypotheses. The bound $\mathcal G(x,y)\le C|x-y|^{2-n}$ and dyadic annuli give $Cr^{\alpha_0}$ for the measure source. The exterior-pole gradient energy bound $\int_{\{|x-y|>\rho\}}|D_y\mathcal G(x,y)|^2
 \le C\rho^{2-n}$ gives $L^1$ gradient mass $C\rho$ on an annulus of scale $\rho$. Summing gives $Cr$ for bounded divergence data. These scalar Green bounds hold in every $n\ge3$; see (Kang and Kim 2010, Corollary 4.1 and Theorem 3.6) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[KangKim2010](#ref-KangKim2010)]. One can first use the individually bounded densities here and then pass to their uniform growth bounds.

Put $\epsilon_r=C(r+r^{\alpha_0})$, and define $$U_\pm=\sup_{B_r}Q_\pm+\epsilon_r-Q_\pm+w_\pm.$$ They are nonnegative supersolutions. Let $M=\sup_{B_r}Z$ and $m=\inf_{B_r}Z$. On at least half of an intermediate ball either $Z\le(M+m)/2$ or $Z\ge(M+m)/2$. In the first case the uncorrected plus deficit is bounded below by a fixed multiple of $M-m$; in the second case the minus deficit is. The multiplicative constant is positive because $Z$ is bounded. Weak Harnack for the corresponding $U_\pm$ therefore lowers the supremum, or raises the infimum, on $B_{r/4}$, up to $C\epsilon_r$. Thus $$\operatorname{osc}_{B_{r/4}}Z
 \le(1-c_0)\operatorname{osc}_{B_r}Z+C(r+r^{\alpha_0}).$$ Iteration gives a Hölder modulus. The same proof applies after the stated reflection. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pde-coupled-estimates"></a>
<div id="pde:coupled-estimates" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29** (Estimates for smooth fixed points above the floor). *Fix $N$ and a sufficiently distant truncation of $\mathcal M_N$. Every smooth solution of the system <a href="#alg:system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:system">[alg:system]</a>, or of its specified homotopy, satisfying $t>-\varepsilon$, has local smooth bounds depending on fixed $N$ and the background geometry. The bounds hold up to the outer zero-Dirichlet face and are uniform as that face tends to infinity, on fixed compact sets and uniformly sized end patches.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pde-coupled-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-pde-coupled-estimates</summary>

<a id="pde:normalized-trace"></a>
*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#alg:floor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:floor">25</a> and Proposition <a href="#alg:distortion-proposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:distortion-proposition">26</a> give $$-\varepsilon<t\le Z\le\mathcal P_N,\qquad
 \ell\le l\le e,\qquad |f|+|Df|\le e^{\mathcal P_N}.$$ At fixed $N$, the eigenvalue $\chi=kd/(k+pv)$ is consequently bounded away from zero. The normalized trace equation is $$A_\chi:\nabla^2f
 =\frac{\sqrt D}{l}
       \big(\eta f-\operatorname{tr}_{A_\chi}K\big).
 \label{pde:normalized-trace}$$ Its right side is bounded. Lemma <a href="#pde:special-gradient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:special-gradient">27</a> therefore bounds $Df$ in $C^\alpha$, including at the outer face, and gives <a href="#pde:hessian-morrey" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:hessian-morrey">[pde:hessian-morrey]</a> for $f$.

Write the other equation in the form of Lemma <a href="#pde:natural-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:natural-growth">28</a>, with $$A=kuA_\chi,\qquad B=uA_\chi K(w,\cdot).$$ The sign of the scalar source is immaterial for its absolute bound. The implicit relation for $t$ gives $$|Dt|\le C_N(1+|DZ|+|D^2f|).$$ The explicit quadratic formula for $\mathcal T$ then bounds the absolute scalar source by $$C_N(1+|DZ|^2)+C_N|D^2f|^2.$$ All lower-order fluxes are bounded. The Hessian estimate supplies $$\int_{B_r}|D^2f|^2\le C_Nr^{n-2+2\alpha};$$ we may decrease the exponent when applying Lemma <a href="#pde:natural-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:natural-growth">28</a>. This proves a uniform Hölder modulus for $Z$.

At the outer face extend $Z$ oddly and the divergence matrix by $A^-=RA^+R$. Extend the total flux by $-R$, and the scalar source oddly. The normal flux is continuous, so its distributional divergence has no plane mass. Boundedness and the source-growth estimates are preserved. This justifies the use of the boundary case of Lemma <a href="#pde:natural-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:natural-growth">28</a>.

The coefficients and right side of <a href="#pde:normalized-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:normalized-trace">[pde:normalized-trace]</a> are now Hölder, since they are smooth functions of $Z,Df$ on the bounded parameter range. Interior and Dirichlet Schauder estimates give $f$ through second derivatives. Expanding the divergence equation for $Z$, its principal matrix is Hölder and its remaining source is bounded by $C_N(1+|DZ|^2)$. For finite $s>n$, use the local interpolation inequality <a id="pde:quadratic-interpolation"></a>$$\|DZ\|_{L^{2s}(Q)}^2
 \le C\,\operatorname{osc}_{Q'}Z\,\|D^2Z\|_{L^s(Q')}
       +C_{Q,Q'}(\operatorname{osc}_{Q'}Z)^2
 \label{pde:quadratic-interpolation}$$ on concentric ball or half-ball patches $Q\Subset Q'$. It follows by scaled derivative interpolation after subtraction of a constant. The already proved Hölder estimate makes its Hessian coefficient uniformly small on sufficiently small fixed patches. Local nondivergence $W^{2,s}$ estimates therefore absorb the quadratic term. The leading estimate constants stay bounded as patches shrink, since the principal matrices have a uniform Hölder modulus. Lower-order constants may depend on the selected scale. Use bounded overlap of patch enlargements and the supremum of their local Hessian norms to carry out the absorption; these norms are individually finite on every truncation. This proves $W^{2,s}$ bounds for $Z$.

Schauder estimates and differentiation now bootstrap both functions. At each differentiation the $f$ equation is solved first with the known regularity of $Z$, and then the expanded $Z$ equation gains the next derivative. These are the usual local and Dirichlet elliptic estimates (Gilbarg and Trudinger 2001) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[GilbargTrudinger2001](#ref-GilbargTrudinger2001)]. All charts, including uniformly sized end charts and outer boundary charts, have controlled geometry. Hence the constants on fixed compact sets do not depend on the truncation radius. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Solving the trace equation and the coupled system

<a id="stmt-pde-trial-trace"></a>
<div id="pde:trial-trace" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30** (The trace equation for an arbitrary trial scalar). *On a fixed smooth truncation, fix $Z\in C^{1,\alpha}$ with zero outer trace. The trace equation $F=\eta f$, with zero outer Dirichlet value for $f$, has a unique solution in $C^{3,\alpha}$. The solution depends continuously on the trial $Z$ and on the tensor homotopy. On bounded sets of trials these solutions have bounded $C^{3,\alpha}$ norms. No floor condition on the trial is needed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pde-trial-trace"><summary>Proof of stmt-pde-trial-trace</summary>

*Proof.* For any prescribed $Z,Df$, the defining relation for $t$ is strictly increasing in $t$, so the coefficients are smooth functions of these variables. Continue the normalized equation <a href="#pde:normalized-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:normalized-trace">[pde:normalized-trace]</a> from $\Delta f=f$, by straight interpolation of the two operators. Its height coefficient is strictly negative when all terms are placed on the left. At a positive maximum the gradient vanishes, $A_\chi=I$, and the maximum test gives $f\le\|\operatorname{tr}K\|_\infty/\eta$; the analogous bound holds at a minimum. The same height bound works throughout the interpolation.

<a id="pde:trial-growth"></a>
We supply the gradient bound, since ellipticity must not be assumed before it. Put $\sigma=|Df|$. For bounded trial $Z$, as $\sigma\to\infty$, the implicit relation has $t<0$ and hence $l=e^{Nt}$, so $$t=-\frac{\log\sigma}{k+N}+O(1),\qquad
 d\asymp\sigma^{-2k/(k+N)}.$$ For $N>k$ this gives $$\chi\ge\frac{c_N}{1+\sigma},\qquad
 \frac{\sqrt D}{l}\le C_N(1+\sigma).
 \label{pde:trial-growth}$$ The constants are uniform on a bounded set of trials. Compact $\sigma$ ranges are included by positivity and continuity. The normalized right side, using the height bound, is at most $C_N(1+\sigma)$ in absolute value. Straight operator interpolation has the same properties: its axial eigenvalue is at least $\chi$.

Choose a short distance interval $[0,s_0]$ and a modulus $$\psi(0)=0,\qquad \psi''=-K_0(\psi')^2,\qquad \psi'>0.$$ Choose $K_0$ large first, then $s_0$ sufficiently small for the distance geometry and so that $\psi'\ge1$ can hold throughout. Finally choose the initial slope sufficiently large that $\psi(s_0)$ exceeds twice the height bound. This is possible from $$\psi(s)=K_0^{-1}\log(1+K_0\psi'(0)s).$$ On a boundary collar, $\pm\psi(\operatorname{dist}(\cdot,\partial))$ are upper and lower barriers. The negative axial term $\chi\psi''$, by <a href="#pde:trial-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:trial-growth">[pde:trial-growth]</a>, has size at least a fixed multiple of $K_0\psi'$, and dominates the linear-growth right side and the bounded second derivatives of the distance.

For the interior estimate consider a positive maximum of $$f(x)-f(y)-\psi(\operatorname{dist}(x,y))$$ with distance less than $s_0$, using the smooth ambient metric extension across the outer boundary. Contacts at a boundary endpoint are excluded by the preceding barriers; contacts at distance $s_0$ are excluded by the height bound. At an interior contact the two gradients are parallel along the short minimizing geodesic and have length $\sigma=\psi'$. Parallel transverse endpoint variations bound the difference of the transverse Hessian traces by $C\operatorname{dist}(x,y)\sigma$. The separate axial variations give $$\nabla^2f_x(e,e)\le\psi'',\qquad
 -\nabla^2f_y(e,e)\le\psi''.$$ Thus the difference of the two operators is at most $$C s_0\sigma+(\chi_x+\chi_y)\psi''
 \le C s_0\sigma-c_NK_0\sigma.$$ For large $K_0$ this contradicts the absolute linear-growth bound for the difference of their right sides. The same argument with $x,y$ exchanged proves the Lipschitz bound. It is valid throughout the operator interpolation.

The gradient bound gives uniform ellipticity. Apply Lemma <a href="#pde:special-gradient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:special-gradient">27</a>, then Schauder to obtain $C^{2,\beta}$ for some $\beta>0$. These bounds make $Df$ Lipschitz, so the original $C^{1,\alpha}$ trial and the smooth coefficient functions give $C^\alpha$ coefficients; Schauder then yields $C^{2,\alpha}$ and, by one differentiation, $C^{3,\alpha}$. The Dirichlet linearization has a strictly negative zero-order term. The maximum principle and the usual elliptic Fredholm alternative therefore make it invertible. The same maximum principle, applied to the linear equation for the difference, gives uniqueness. Continuation is open by this invertibility and closed by the uniform estimates. The implicit-function theorem and uniqueness also give continuous dependence in the stated spaces. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pde-filled-solve"></a>
<div id="pde:filled-solve" class="proposition">

<a id="pde:end-decay"></a>
**Proposition 31** (A filled solution). *For fixed prepared data and $\varepsilon>0$, all sufficiently large $N$ admit a smooth solution of <a href="#alg:system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:system">[alg:system]</a> on the entire one-ended filled manifold $\mathcal M_N$, with $$t>-\varepsilon,\qquad
 t\le Z\le\mathcal P_N,\qquad
 \ell\le l\le e,\qquad
 |f|+|\nabla f|\le e^{\mathcal P_N}.$$ The functions $f$ and all their derivatives decay exponentially on the end, with constants depending on fixed $N$. Moreover $$t,Z=O_j(r^{2-n})\quad\hbox{for every }j,\qquad
 \Delta_gt=O(r^{-n-\beta}).
 \label{pde:end-decay}$$ The uniform height bound and the radial height estimate of Lemma <a href="#alg:trace-height" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:trace-height">23</a> hold on the limiting solution.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pde-filled-solve"><summary>Proof of stmt-pde-filled-solve</summary>

*Proof.* First truncate at a coordinate sphere of radius $R$, with $R\ge R_{\min}(N)$ as in the floor estimate. For a trial $Z\in C^{1,\alpha}$ with zero outer trace solve the trace equation by Lemma <a href="#pde:trial-trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:trial-trace">30</a>. Freeze this pair in the divergence equation, except for the gradient of the new $Z$ in its principal flux. The resulting linear Dirichlet solve defines a map $T$ into $C^{2,\alpha}$. Indeed its principal coefficients are $C^{1,\alpha}$, its drift flux is $C^{1,\alpha}$, and its scalar source is $C^\alpha$. Thus $T$ is a continuous compact map on $C^{1,\alpha}$. Fixed points bootstrap to smoothness.

Use the homotopy described with <a href="#alg:system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:system">[alg:system]</a>, in reverse order: first take $K=0$ and scale the full scalar source from zero to one, then scale $K$ from zero to its specified value with the full source. When $K=0$, uniqueness of the trace equation gives $f=0$ for every trial. At zero scalar scaling the map is identically zero.

For a homotopy parameter $\lambda$, write $f_\lambda[Z]$ for the trace solve. Apply degree on the relatively open moving domain $$\left\{(\lambda,Z):
   \min t[Z,Df_\lambda[Z]]>-\varepsilon,\quad
   \|Z\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}<M_{N,R}\right\}.$$ The dependence is continuous by Lemma <a href="#pde:trial-trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:trial-trace">30</a>. Choose $M_{N,R}$ larger than the fixed-point bound supplied by Lemma <a href="#pde:coupled-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:coupled-estimates">29</a>. There is no norm-boundary fixed point. There is no floor-boundary fixed point by the floor exclusion, including its outer-sphere estimate and final $K=f=0$ stage. The a priori estimates also hold with a non-strict floor inequality when taking a closure, before that exclusion is applied. The map has relatively compact image on the bounded trial closure. Leray–Schauder degree for a bounded relatively open subset of the product with the parameter interval therefore applies (Leray and Schauder 1934, secs. 12–13) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[LeraySchauder1934](#ref-LeraySchauder1934)]. Its degree at the initial zero map is one, by excision around $Z=0$; it stays one throughout the homotopy. This gives a smooth solution above the floor on each sufficiently large truncation.

Lemma <a href="#pde:coupled-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:coupled-estimates">29</a> gives smooth local compactness uniform in $R$. To retain the boundary condition at infinity, we establish uniform end estimates before exhausting. On the end $K=0$, and <a href="#pde:normalized-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:normalized-trace">[pde:normalized-trace]</a> becomes $$A_\chi:\nabla^2f=\frac{\eta\sqrt D}{l}f,\qquad
 \frac{\eta\sqrt D}{l}\ge\eta/e.$$ For fixed $N$, sufficiently small $\kappa_N>0$, and a sufficiently large fixed inner radius, the functions $C_Ne^{-\kappa_N(r-r_0)}$ are barriers for both signs of $f$. The positive zeroth-order coefficient dominates their radial second derivatives; enlarge $r_0$ to control the radial Hessian error. They dominate at the inner sphere and at the outer zero boundary. Consequently $f$ decays exponentially, uniformly in $R$. Local and outer-face estimates give the same decay for every derivative. The implicit relation then gives exponentially small $t-Z$, with all derivatives.

<a id="pde:end-equation"></a>
Up to exponentially decaying errors, the end equation is $$k\Delta_gZ+
 k\big[(k-1)+p(Z)-\delta_0s_{p(Z)}\big]|dZ|_g^2
 =\rho-\vartheta(N(Z+\varepsilon))C_N\rho_0,
 \qquad s_p=p+(k-1)/2.
 \label{pde:end-equation}$$ Set $a_N(z)=(k-1)+p(z)-\delta_0s_{p(z)}$, and choose $\Phi_N(0)=0$, $\Phi_N'(0)=1$, with $\Phi_N''/\Phi_N'=a_N$. On the bounded range of $Z$, $\Phi_N'$ has positive lower and upper bounds depending on fixed $N$. Thus <a href="#pde:end-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:end-equation">[pde:end-equation]</a> implies $$\Delta_g\Phi_N(Z)=O_N(r^{-n-\beta})+O_N(e^{-\kappa_Nr}).$$ Comparison with multiples of $r^{2-n}(1-r^{-\beta/2})$, on each truncation, gives $\Phi_N(Z)=O_N(r^{2-n})$, hence $Z=O_N(r^{2-n})$. These barriers have the required signed Laplacian for large $r$; their $r^{-n-\beta/2}$ term dominates both the source and the asymptotically flat metric errors.

Scaled Poisson $W^{2,s}$ estimates first give weighted first-derivative Hölder bounds; the source is a smooth function of $Z$, the radius weights and exponentially small terms. Scaled Schauder and differentiation then give $Z=O_j(r^{2-n})$ for every $j$. The same follows for $t$. Its Laplacian has the improved bound in <a href="#pde:end-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:end-decay">[pde:end-decay]</a>, since $|dZ|^2=O(r^{2-2n})$ and $\beta<1$. Exhaustion now supplies the asserted solution with zero limit at infinity. If the limit touched $-\varepsilon$, that point would be an interior minimum, and the identical floor calculation of Lemma <a href="#alg:floor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:floor">25</a> would exclude it. All height bounds pass to the limit. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Mass, height separation, and the charged region

<a id="stmt-pde-mass-cost"></a>
<div id="pde:mass-cost" class="proposition">

<a id="pde:mass-flux"></a>
<a id="pde:mass-error"></a>
<a id="pde:base-mass"></a>
**Proposition 32** (The mass cost of the filled construction). *Let $\widehat g=e^{2t}(g+l^2df^2)$ be obtained from Proposition <a href="#pde:filled-solve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:filled-solve">31</a>, and let $E_*$ be the ADM energy of the prepared metric $g$. Then $$\mathfrak F
 :=\lim_{r\to\infty}\int_{S_r}g(V,\nu)\,\mathrm dA_g
 =\int_{\mathcal M_N}u\Xi\,\mathrm dV_g,\qquad
 \mathcal E_{\mathrm{ADM}}(\widehat g)
 =E_*-\frac{\mathfrak F}{k\omega}.
 \label{pde:mass-flux}$$ For fixed $\varepsilon$, $$\mathfrak F\ge
 -\mathcal P_N(\ell+\ell^2/\eta)
 =-\mathcal P_N(\ell+\ell^{1/2})=-o_N(1).
 \label{pde:mass-error}$$ The metric $g_b=e^{2\varepsilon}\widehat g$, written in its dilated asymptotic coordinates, consequently has $$\mathcal E_{\mathrm{ADM}}(g_b)
 \le e^{(n-2)\varepsilon}(E_*+o_N(1)).
 \label{pde:base-mass}$$ It has all-order metric decay $O_j(r^{2-n})$, a finite ADM energy, and scalar curvature $O(r^{-n-\beta})$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pde-mass-cost"><summary>Proof of stmt-pde-mass-cost</summary>

*Proof.* All terms in $u\Xi$ are integrable. On the end this follows from the exponential decay of $f$, $\mathcal T=O(r^{2-2n})$, and the integrable radius weight $\rho_0=r^{-n-\beta}$; on the filled compact part it is ordinary smooth integrability. The divergence theorem on the manifold without an inner boundary proves the flux limit in <a href="#pde:mass-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:mass-flux">[pde:mass-flux]</a>. At infinity $V=k\nabla t+o(r^{1-n})$ in the flux sense, since $u=1+O_N(r^{2-n})$ and all graph terms decay exponentially. The metric perturbation $2t\delta_{ij}$ contributes $-2k\,\partial_i t$ to the Euclidean ADM integrand. The normalization $1/(2k\omega)$ therefore gives the second identity in <a href="#pde:mass-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:mass-flux">[pde:mass-flux]</a>; all mixed error fluxes vanish.

<a id="pde:penalty-band"></a>
Only the penalty band can contribute negatively to the integral. There $m_0\ne0$ implies $-\varepsilon<t<-\varepsilon+1/N$, and hence $\ell\le l\le e\ell$ for sufficiently large $N$. Writing $\sigma=|\nabla f|_g$, the definitions give $$u\le C(\ell+\ell^2\sigma),\qquad
 uv\le C\ell^2\sigma,\qquad
 ua_w\sigma\ge c\ell^2\sigma^2.
 \label{pde:penalty-band}$$ For example the last expression equals $e^{(k-1)t}l^2\sigma^2$ exactly. The negative penalty is bounded by $$C\mathcal P_N\ell\rho_0
 +C\mathcal P_N\ell^2\sigma(\rho_0+\rho_1).$$ Absorb its second term into the positive $\delta_0\eta ua_w\sigma$, using <a href="#pde:penalty-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:penalty-band">[pde:penalty-band]</a>. The remaining negative part is bounded by $$C\mathcal P_N\ell\rho_0
 +C\mathcal P_N\frac{\ell^2}{\eta}(\rho_0+\rho_1)^2.$$ The integrals of $\rho_0,\rho_0^2,\rho_1^2$ grow at most polynomially in $N$: the core volume is $O(1+N)$, and on the end $4b_1>n$. This proves <a href="#pde:mass-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:mass-error">[pde:mass-error]</a>. Notice that no fixed-$N$ Schauder constant enters this estimate.

A constant metric dilation by $e^{2\varepsilon}$, followed by the corresponding coordinate dilation, multiplies ADM energy by $e^{(n-2)\varepsilon}$, proving <a href="#pde:base-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:base-mass">[pde:base-mass]</a>. All-order metric decay follows from Proposition <a href="#pde:filled-solve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:filled-solve">31</a> and the prepared end. The conformal scalar law, the prepared scalar tail, and $\Delta_gt=O(r^{-n-\beta})$ give the stated scalar decay. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pde-height-separation"></a>
<div id="pde:height-separation" class="proposition">

**Proposition 33** (Separation of the original boundary by height). *There are $b_2>0$ and $N_2$, depending on the fixed prepared data and $\varepsilon$, such that the solutions of Proposition <a href="#pde:filled-solve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:filled-solve">31</a> satisfy, for $N\ge N_2$, $$h<-b_2\quad\hbox{on every plus component of }S,\qquad
 h>b_2\quad\hbox{on every minus component of }S.$$ The constants are independent of $N$. For every fixed $b>0$, the portion of the original exterior on which $|h|\ge b$ is contained in a fixed compact set, independent of $N$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pde-height-separation"><summary>Proof of stmt-pde-height-separation</summary>

*Proof.* The last assertion follows immediately from the uniform height tail in Lemma <a href="#alg:trace-height" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:trace-height">23</a>. We prove the first assertion by a trace supersolution on each long product and its prescribed collars. Only the trace equation is used in this comparison, so no sign condition in the distant cap is needed.

Treat a plus component. Choose a small fixed $\delta>0$ below the strict negative leaf-expansion margin on the prescribed collars. On the middle of its long product use height $-\delta$. There $K=-L_0g$, with $L_0$ the fixed large number used when constructing the filling. Towards the original boundary let the slope increase from zero to a small positive fixed value $a$. It can be chosen as a smoothly truncated exponential: start with size of order $\eta$, increase exponentially, and stop smoothly at $a$, with $$|(\hbox{slope})'|\le C(\hbox{slope}+\eta).$$ Its length is $O(1+|\log\eta|)=O(1+N)$, and its total height increase is as small as desired by choosing $a$ small. The initial smoothing from slope zero has the same displayed bound.

For a height test $H$, the Hessian multiplier in the trace equation is $$\frac{1}{\sqrt{(\eta/l)^2+|dH|^2}}.$$ Thus the axial Hessian contribution along this ramp is uniformly bounded, since $l\le e$ and $$\frac{\hbox{slope}+\eta}
      {\sqrt{(\eta/l)^2+\hbox{slope}^2}}\le C.$$ The transverse contribution of the product metric is zero. The trace of $-L_0g$ is at most $-kL_0$. Taking $L_0$ large compared with the fixed ramp bound makes the ramp a strict supersolution while its height is at least $-\delta$. This choice is part of the fixed filling geometry, before $N$.

Continue at small positive slope through the fixed transitions to $S$, keeping the height there below $-\delta/2$. Then, on a fixed strict trapped collar on the original side, increase the slope smoothly until the height exceeds the uniform coarse height bound. All these slopes are bounded below by $a>0$; the slopes and their derivatives on this fixed portion are fixed finite numbers. At a contact with the solution, $\eta/l\le\ell^{1/2}$, and the axial eigenvalue satisfies $$\chi\le d
 =\frac{(\eta/l)^2}{(\eta/l)^2+|dH|^2}.$$ Consequently the trace operator on the test converges uniformly, on that fixed portion, to the expansion of its coordinate leaf, $H_{\mathrm{leaf}}+\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{leaf}}K$. It is strictly negative by construction. The error from the axial second derivative tends to zero as $N\to\infty$. With the original choice of $\delta$, this trace is below the test height, including while the height increases to dominate the coarse bound.

On the side of the long product facing the cap, mirror the ramp and continue farther into product geometry at fixed slope, with height increasing until it also dominates the coarse bound. This adds only a fixed amount of length after the $O(N)$ ramp. It closes the comparison region without assuming anything about the values of $h$ in the cap, even when opposite-sign products join there.

The test therefore dominates $h$ on both ends of the comparison region and is a strict supersolution within it. At a positive interior maximum of $h-H$, the two gradients agree. The implicit $t$, $l$, and $A_\chi$ consequently agree when evaluating the test with the actual $Z$, while the Hessian of $h-H$ is nonpositive. The trace equation would give $h\le F(H)<H$, a contradiction. Thus $h\le H<-\delta/2$ on $S$. For a minus component apply the same argument to $-h,-K$: $D,t,l,\chi$ are unchanged under the reversal, and the trace equation reverses sign. Taking the minimum over finitely many components gives $b_2$. These barriers concern the final system; the homotopy stages need not satisfy this comparison. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pde-charged-region"></a>
<div id="pde:charged-region" class="proposition">

<a id="pde:retained-band"></a>
**Proposition 34** (The charged low-height region). *Choose $b_3>0$ sufficiently small relative to $b_2$ and the strict prepared matter margin. For every sufficiently large $N$, put $$\bar g=g+l^2df^2,\qquad
 g_b=e^{2(t+\varepsilon)}\bar g.$$ On the original exterior $g_b\ge g$ as quadratic forms, and $$|dh|_{\bar g}\le\eta/l\le\ell^{1/2}.$$ If $\alpha=\iota_EdV_g$ is the retained electric flux form, then on the original region $\{|h|\le b_3\}$, <a id="pde:charged-curvature"></a>$$\frac{R_{g_b}}2\ge c_n^2|\alpha|_{g_b}^2.
 \label{pde:charged-curvature}$$ There is $c_*>0$, independent of $N$, such that on the original band $b_3/4\le|h|\le b_3$, $$e^{2(t+\varepsilon)}\frac{R_{g_b}}2
 \ge I_g(w)+c_*+(1-\delta_0)\mathcal T.
 \label{pde:retained-band}$$ The band lies in a fixed compact portion of the original exterior. Here $I_g(w)$ is the electromagnetic expression defined with the prepared data; it also permits the compactly supported magnetic form used in the strict numerical extension.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pde-charged-region"><summary>Proof of stmt-pde-charged-region</summary>

*Proof.* The strict margin and the choice of $\rho$ in <a href="#alg:system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:system">[alg:system]</a> give $\mu_m-|J_m|-\rho>0$. Since $\tau$ is compactly supported, we may choose $b_3<b_2$, independent of $N$, so that $$\mu_m-|J_m|-\rho-b_3|\tau|
 \ge\tfrac12(\mu_m-|J_m|-\rho)>0$$ everywhere on the original exterior. The scalar identity <a href="#alg:scalar-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:scalar-identity">[alg:scalar-identity]</a>, the two equations, and $w(F)=\eta a_w|\nabla f|$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 e^{2t}\frac{R_{\widehat g}}2
 ={}&\mu+J(w)-\rho-h\tau+(1-\delta_0)\mathcal T\\
 &+(1-\delta_0)\eta a_w|\nabla f|
     +m_0C_N(\rho_0+v\rho_1).
\end{aligned}$$ The DEC in the retained electromagnetic variables implies $\mu+J(w)\ge I_g(w)+\mu_m-|J_m|$. All displayed terms on the second line are nonnegative. This proves a positive matter remainder and the retained $(1-\delta_0)\mathcal T$ throughout the low region. On the stated band its lower bound is uniform: by Proposition <a href="#pde:height-separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:height-separation">33</a> the band is contained in a fixed compact set. This proves <a href="#pde:retained-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:retained-band">[pde:retained-band]</a>, since $R_{g_b}=e^{-2\varepsilon}R_{\widehat g}$.

The inverse of $\bar g$ is $A_d$. Hence $$|dh|_{\bar g}^2
 =\eta^2d|\nabla f|_g^2
 =(\eta/l)^2v\le(\eta/l)^2.$$ For the $(n-1)$-form $\alpha=\iota_EdV_g$, the volume factor $\sqrt D$ gives the exact norm identity <a id="pde:flux-form-norm"></a>$$|\alpha|_{\bar g}^2
 =d|E|_g^2+\langle w,E\rangle_g^2
 =|E|_g^2-|w\wedge E|_g^2.
 \label{pde:flux-form-norm}$$ The electromagnetic square completion gives $I_g(w)\ge c_n^2|\alpha|_{\bar g}^2$. Set $z=t+\varepsilon>0$. Scalar curvature scales by $e^{-2z}$ in the preceding inequality, whereas the squared norm of an $(n-1)$-form scales by $e^{-2kz}$. Since $k\ge1$, $$\frac{R_{g_b}}2
 \ge c_n^2e^{-2z}|\alpha|_{\bar g}^2
 \ge c_n^2e^{-2kz}|\alpha|_{\bar g}^2
 =c_n^2|\alpha|_{g_b}^2.$$ Finally $e^{2z}\ge1$ and $\bar g\ge g$, proving metric dominance. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:flux-conversion"></a>
### Conversion of electric flux into an energy decrease

This section completes the numerical argument. The filled scalar construction provides a metric which dominates the original metric and has the required charged scalar-curvature inequality on a region separated from the original boundary. A second scalar equation converts the signed total electric flux into a controlled decrease of ADM energy. A lower bound for its conformal factor retains a definite fraction of every full-cut area. We then construct a smooth future-trapped boundary and apply only the neutral numerical input, Theorem <a href="#thm:neutral-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:neutral-input">4</a>.

<a id="flux:setup"></a>
#### Data supplied by the filled construction

Throughout this section, $n\geq4$, $k=n-1$, $\omega=\mathop{\mathrm{Area}}(S^k)$, and $c_n^2=k(k-1)/2$. Initially the exterior data have been prepared as in Proposition <a href="#prep:rest-preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prep:rest-preparation">17</a> and Lemma <a href="#prep:strictification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prep:strictification">15</a>. We write $g$ for their metric, $\mathcal E_*$ for their ADM energy, and <a id="flux:prepared-area"></a>$$a_* = \inf_{\Gamma}\mathop{\mathrm{Area}}_g(\Gamma),
 \qquad X_* = (a_*/\omega)^{(k-1)/k}.
 \label{flux:prepared-area}$$ The infimum is over the entire full cuts of this prepared exterior. In particular, $a_*>0$ by Lemma <a href="#prep:positive-area" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prep:positive-area">10</a>. The prepared tensor $K$ has compact support, the metric has differentiated decay of order $2-n$ to every fixed order, and the charged dominant-energy and prescribed componentwise trapping inequalities are strict. The optional compact magnetic form is allowed in this paragraph; it will be specified again in Corollary <a href="#flux:magnetic-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:magnetic-extension">41</a>.

<a id="flux:height-gradient"></a>
Fix $\varepsilon>0$. For every sufficiently large approximation integer $N$, let $\mathcal M_N$ be the filled one-ended manifold of Proposition <a href="#pde:filled-solve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:filled-solve">31</a>. We also denote its background metric by $g$. It agrees with the prepared metric on the original exterior. Put <a id="flux:background-metrics"></a>$$\ell=e^{-N\varepsilon},\qquad \eta=\ell^{3/2},\qquad
 h=\eta f,\qquad z=t+\varepsilon,\qquad
 \bar g=g+l^2\,\mathrm df^2,\qquad g_b=e^{2z}\bar g.
 \label{flux:background-metrics}$$ The scalar-construction variables satisfy $$z\geq0,\qquad \ell\leq l\leq e,\qquad
 |\mathrm dh|_{\bar g}\leq\eta/l\leq\ell^{1/2}.
 \label{flux:height-gradient}$$ For fixed prepared data and $\varepsilon$, the bounds for $t$, $Z$, and $\nabla f$ imply that the metric coefficients and inverse coefficients needed below have bounds of the form $\exp(\mathcal P_N)$, where $\mathcal P_N$ denotes a polynomial in $1+N$ whose coefficients may change from occurrence to occurrence. No uniform bound as $N\to\infty$ is claimed for the second scalar solution.

<a id="flux:height-separation"></a>
<a id="flux:low-region"></a>
We use the following further conclusions of Propositions <a href="#pde:mass-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:mass-cost">32</a>, <a href="#pde:height-separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:height-separation">33</a>, and <a href="#pde:charged-region" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pde:charged-region">34</a>. There are constants $b_2>b_3>0$, independent of $N$, such that $$|h|>b_2\quad\hbox{on }S,\qquad h\longrightarrow0
 \quad\hbox{at infinity},
 \label{flux:height-separation}$$ and the set $\{|h|\geq b_3/4\}$ on the original exterior is contained in a fixed compact set. We take $b_3<b_2/2$. On $$\mathcal U_N=\{x\hbox{ in the original exterior}: |h(x)|\leq b_3\},
 \label{flux:low-region}$$ the retained curvature estimate is the charged one, with an additional positive margin on $b_3/4\leq|h|\leq b_3$. More precisely, the scalar identity supplies there a constant $c_*>0$, independent of $N$, and the nonnegative term $(1-\delta_0)\mathcal T$, with $\delta_0<1/2$. The energy estimate, in coordinates normalized for $g_b$ at infinity, is <a id="flux:background-energy"></a>$$\mathcal E(g_b)\leq
 e^{(k-1)\varepsilon}(\mathcal E_*+o_N(1)),
 \qquad o_N(1)\longrightarrow0
 \quad(N\longrightarrow\infty).
 \label{flux:background-energy}$$ The fixed-$N$ metric $g_b$ has all-order differentiated asymptotic decay of order $2-n$ after the constant coordinate dilation, and $R_{g_b}=O(r^{-n-\beta})$ for a $\beta>0$.

<a id="flux:extended-fields"></a>
Let $\alpha=i_E\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g$ on the original exterior. Multiply it by $\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}Q$ when $Q\neq0$, and use either sign when $Q=0$. Extend this signed form smoothly into the filling, cutting it off on fixed collars on the inside of $S$; call the extension $\widetilde\alpha$. It is closed on the original exterior and need not be closed in the filling. Define $$i_{E_b}\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_{g_b}=\widetilde\alpha,
 \qquad i_{E_{\mathrm{ext}}}\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g=\widetilde\alpha.
 \label{flux:extended-fields}$$ Thus the flux of $E_b$ across a large sphere is $\omega|Q|$. Its divergence vanishes on the original exterior. The vector $E_{\mathrm{ext}}$ is uniformly bounded on the filling and is $O(r^{-k})$ on the end. Its support in the filling can be kept in the fixed collars; even an extension over the whole core would have only polynomially growing $L^p$ norms, because that core has volume $O(1+N)$.

<a id="flux:graph-form-norm"></a>
<a id="flux:conformal-field-cost"></a>
Matrix formulas below use a $g$-orthonormal frame, or equivalently the $g$-identification of vectors and covectors. For clarity, the form norm underlying the charged estimate is $$|\alpha|_{\bar g}^2
   =(1-|w|_g^2)|E|_g^2+g(E,w)^2
   =|E|_g^2-|w\wedge E|_g^2.
 \label{flux:graph-form-norm}$$ Indeed $\bar g^{-1}=A_d=g^{-1}-w\otimes w$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_{\bar g}=\sqrt D\,\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g$. Completing the square in the magnetic two-form, when it is present, gives $I_g(w)\geq c_n^2|\alpha|_{\bar g}^2$. Since $z\geq0$, $$e^{2z}|E_b|_{g_b}^2
 =e^{-2(k-1)z}|\alpha|_{\bar g}^2
 \leq |\alpha|_{\bar g}^2.
 \label{flux:conformal-field-cost}$$ Consequently the scalar-construction estimates imply $R_{g_b}/2\geq c_n^2|E_b|_{g_b}^2$ throughout $\mathcal U_N$.

<a id="stmt-flux-retained-gradient"></a>
<div id="flux:retained-gradient" class="lemma">

<a id="flux:T-gradient"></a>
<a id="flux:background-band-curvature"></a>
**Lemma 35** (A retained gradient term). *The quadratic expression $\mathcal T$ in the filled scalar construction satisfies $$\mathcal T\geq\frac{k^2-1}{4}|\mathrm dt|_{\bar g}^2.
 \label{flux:T-gradient}$$ In particular, on the original band $b_3/4\leq|h|\leq b_3$, $$e^{2z}R_{g_b}/2\geq
 c_n^2 e^{2z}|E_b|_{g_b}^2+c_*+
 \frac{k^2-1}{8}|\mathrm dt|_{\bar g}^2.
 \label{flux:background-band-curvature}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-retained-gradient"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-retained-gradient</summary>

*Proof.* The inverse graph metric is $A_d$. Hence <a href="#flux:T-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:T-gradient">[flux:T-gradient]</a> is exactly <a href="#alg:gradient-coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:gradient-coercivity">[alg:gradient-coercivity]</a> in the Coercivity Lemma (Lemma <a href="#alg:coercivity-lemma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:coercivity-lemma">22</a>). Combining that estimate with $1-\delta_0\geq1/2$ in <a href="#pde:retained-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pde:retained-band">[pde:retained-band]</a>, the electromagnetic square completion above, and <a href="#flux:conformal-field-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:conformal-field-cost">[flux:conformal-field-cost]</a> gives <a href="#flux:background-band-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:background-band-curvature">[flux:background-band-curvature]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Smooth profiles with the sharp limiting coefficient

<a id="stmt-flux-profiles"></a>
<div id="flux:profiles" class="lemma">

<a id="flux:floor"></a>
<a id="flux:profile-discriminant"></a>
<a id="flux:profile-value"></a>
**Lemma 36** (Flux profiles). *Fix $0<s<1$ and set $$y_0=\frac{\log(1-s^2)}{k-1}<0.
 \label{flux:floor}$$ For every $\delta>0$ there exist smooth functions $L>0$ and $H_0$ on $\mathbb R$ with <a id="flux:profile-properties"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 L(0)=1,\qquad L'\geq0,\qquad
 H_0=0\text{ on }(-\infty,y_0],\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}H_0'\Subset(y_0,0),\qquad H_0'\geq0,
 \label{flux:profile-properties}\\
 |H_0'(y)|\leq
 k\sqrt{(k-1)\bigl(k-1+2L'(y)/L(y)\bigr)}
 e^{(k-1)y}L(y),
 \label{flux:profile-discriminant}\\
 ks-\delta<H_0(0)\leq ks.
 \label{flux:profile-value}
\end{gathered}$$ In particular $H_0$ is bounded. The function defined by <a id="flux:B-definition"></a>$$B_0(0)=0,\qquad B_0'(y)=e^{(k-1)y}L(y)
 \label{flux:B-definition}$$ is strictly increasing, and its range on $[y_0,\infty)$ is $[B_0(y_0),\infty)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-profiles"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-profiles</summary>

<a id="flux:optimal-derivative"></a>
*Proof.* Let $a_0=k-1$ and $c=1-s^2$. On $(y_0,\infty)$ consider <a id="flux:optimal-profile"></a>$$L_{\mathrm{opt}}(y)^2=
 \frac{1-c e^{-a_0y}}{s^2}.
 \label{flux:optimal-profile}$$ This is positive and increasing, and $L_{\mathrm{opt}}(0)=1$. Direct differentiation gives $$k\sqrt{a_0(a_0+2L_{\mathrm{opt}}'/L_{\mathrm{opt}})}
 e^{a_0y}L_{\mathrm{opt}}=
 \frac{ka_0}{s}e^{a_0y}.
 \label{flux:optimal-derivative}$$ Its integral from $y_0$ to zero is $ks$.

Here is a positive smooth replacement at the lower endpoint. Take a small $\sigma>0$ and a smooth nondecreasing cutoff $\chi_\sigma$ which is zero below $y_0+\sigma/3$ and one above $y_0+2\sigma/3$. For $y\leq b_\sigma:=y_0+\sigma$ define <a id="flux:profile-smoothing"></a>$$L(y)=L_{\mathrm{opt}}(b_\sigma)
 -\int_y^{b_\sigma}
   \chi_\sigma(v)L_{\mathrm{opt}}'(v)\,\mathrm dv,
 \label{flux:profile-smoothing}$$ where the integrand is zero below $y_0+\sigma/3$. For $y\geq b_\sigma$ use $L=L_{\mathrm{opt}}$. The definitions agree with all derivatives near $b_\sigma$. The lower constant value is positive, since it is at least $L_{\mathrm{opt}}(y_0+\sigma/3)$. Thus $L$ is smooth, positive and nondecreasing on the whole line, and still has $L(0)=1$.

<a id="flux:B-growth"></a>
Choose $0\leq\zeta_\sigma\leq1$ smooth with compact support in $(y_0+\sigma,-\sigma)$, tending pointwise to one on $(y_0,0)$ as $\sigma\downarrow0$, and put <a id="flux:H-construction"></a>$$H_0'(y)=\zeta_\sigma(y)\frac{ka_0}{s}e^{a_0y},
 \qquad H_0(y)=\int_{-\infty}^y H_0'(v)\,\mathrm dv.
 \label{flux:H-construction}$$ On the support of $H_0'$ the function $L$ is exactly $L_{\mathrm{opt}}$, so <a href="#flux:profile-discriminant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:profile-discriminant">[flux:profile-discriminant]</a> follows from <a href="#flux:optimal-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:optimal-derivative">[flux:optimal-derivative]</a>. Elsewhere its left side is zero. Dominated convergence gives $H_0(0)\to ks$ from below, proving <a href="#flux:profile-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:profile-value">[flux:profile-value]</a> after decreasing $\sigma$.
