Finally, $B_0'>0$ and, for $y\geq0$, $L(y)\geq1$, so $$B_0(y)\geq\frac{e^{(k-1)y}-1}{k-1}.
 \label{flux:B-growth}$$ This proves the range assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### The bounded-flux equation on the filled manifold

<a id="stmt-flux-conversion-solve"></a>
<div id="flux:conversion-solve" class="proposition">

<a id="flux:conversion-equation"></a>
<a id="flux:y-flux"></a>
**Proposition 37** (Filled flux conversion). *Fix the prepared data, $\varepsilon$, $s$, and one smooth profile from Lemma <a href="#flux:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:profiles">36</a>. For each sufficiently large fixed $N$ there is a smooth function $y$ on $\mathcal M_N$ such that $$k\Delta_{g_b}B_0(y)=\operatorname{div}_{g_b}(H_0(y)E_b),
 \qquad y\longrightarrow0\quad\hbox{at infinity}.
 \label{flux:conversion-equation}$$ It satisfies <a id="flux:y-bounds"></a>$$y\geq y_0,\qquad y\leq\mathcal P_N,
 \qquad y=O_j(r^{2-n})\quad\hbox{for every fixed }j,
 \label{flux:y-bounds}$$ where constants in the last estimates may depend on $N$ and on the fixed profile. Moreover $B_0(y)$ is harmonic sufficiently far out, and $$\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{S_R}\partial_{\nu_b}y\,\mathrm dA_{g_b}
 =\frac{H_0(0)}{k}\,\omega|Q|.
 \label{flux:y-flux}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-conversion-solve"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-conversion-solve</summary>

*Proof.* We give the estimates on expanding smooth coordinate-sphere truncations of $\mathcal M_N$, with zero outer Dirichlet value. There is no inner boundary.

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
###### A background Sobolev inequality.

<a id="flux:background-Sobolev"></a>
The background filled metric has uniformly controlled local geometry. Its core is covered by at most $C(1+N)$ coordinate patches of fixed size and bounded overlap; the product necks account for the number of patches. The overlap graph is connected to a fixed annulus in the unchanged asymptotic end by chains of length at most $C(1+N)$. For a compactly supported smooth function $v$, radial integration from infinity and Cauchy–Schwarz give, on this fixed annulus $A$, <a id="flux:anchor-annulus"></a>$$\|v\|_{L^2(A,g)}^2\leq
 C\int_{\mathrm{end}}|\mathrm dv|_g^2\,\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g.
 \label{flux:anchor-annulus}$$ For example, the radial weight in this estimate is finite because $\int_R^\infty r^{1-n}\,\mathrm dr<\infty$. Local Poincaré inequalities control the oscillation about each patch mean. On overlapping patches the difference of the two means is controlled by the gradient energy on their union. Summing along the chains to $A$, then summing over the patches, therefore gives <a id="flux:core-poincare"></a>$$\|v\|_{L^2(\mathrm{core},g)}^2
 \leq C(1+N)^C\int_{\mathcal M_N}|\mathrm dv|_g^2\,\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g.
 \label{flux:core-poincare}$$ Combine this with the local Sobolev inequalities on the patches and the ordinary Euclidean Sobolev inequality on the end, using a cutoff across $A$. With $p_*=2n/(n-2)$, this proves $$\|v\|_{L^{p_*}(g)}^2\leq
 S_N\int_{\mathcal M_N}|\mathrm dv|_g^2\,\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g,
 \qquad S_N\leq\exp(\mathcal P_N).
 \label{flux:background-Sobolev}$$ The polynomial bound implicit above would suffice. The weaker displayed bound is convenient for combining constants. Functions with zero trace on a truncation can be extended by zero, so the same constant works for all outer radii. The local Sobolev and Poincaré inequalities used here are the usual uniformly elliptic coordinate versions; see (Gilbarg and Trudinger 2001, chaps. 7–8) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[GilbargTrudinger2001](#ref-GilbargTrudinger2001)].

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
###### The exact divergence matrix.

<a id="flux:background-equation"></a>
Set $Y=B_0(y)$ and $Y_0=B_0(y_0)<0$. Define <a id="flux:bounded-coefficient"></a>$$\mathfrak h(Y)=H_0(B_0^{-1}(Y))\quad(Y\geq Y_0),\qquad
 \mathfrak h(Y)=0\quad(Y<Y_0).
 \label{flux:bounded-coefficient}$$ This is smooth and bounded on the whole real line. It is identically zero near and below $Y_0$, and constant for all sufficiently large $Y$; all its nonzero derivatives have compact support. The ratio of volume forms and the inverse metric are <a id="flux:volume-and-inverse"></a>$$J_b=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_{g_b}}{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g}=e^{nz}\sqrt D,
 \qquad g_b^{-1}=e^{-2z}A_d.
 \label{flux:volume-and-inverse}$$ Consequently the equation in the fixed background measure is exactly $$k\operatorname{div}_g(A\nabla_gY)
   =\operatorname{div}_g(\mathfrak h(Y)E_{\mathrm{ext}}),
 \qquad A=e^{(n-2)z}\sqrt D\,A_d.
 \label{flux:background-equation}$$ Here $J_bE_b=E_{\mathrm{ext}}$ follows directly from <a href="#flux:extended-fields" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:extended-fields">[flux:extended-fields]</a>. In particular no graph-volume factor remains in the right-hand flux.

The transverse eigenvalues of $A$ are $e^{(n-2)z}\sqrt D$ and its axial eigenvalue is $e^{(n-2)z}/\sqrt D$. The filled-solve estimates therefore give <a id="flux:ellipticity"></a>$$\lambda_N\,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\leq A\leq\Lambda_N\,\mathop{\mathrm{id}},
 \qquad \lambda_N^{-1}+\Lambda_N\leq\exp(\mathcal P_N).
 \label{flux:ellipticity}$$ If $F_Y=\mathfrak h(Y)E_{\mathrm{ext}}$, its $L^2(g)$ and $L^{2n}(g)$ norms have polynomial bounds in $1+N$, uniformly in $Y$ and in the truncation radius. Indeed $\mathfrak h$ is bounded, the core extension was just described, and the end integral of $r^{-pk}$ is finite for $p=2,2n$.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
###### Energy and the level estimate.

<a id="flux:energy-bound"></a>
The same estimates apply to the homotopy which multiplies the right-hand side of <a href="#flux:background-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:background-equation">[flux:background-equation]</a> by a number in $[0,1]$. Testing by $Y$ gives $$k\lambda_N\|\mathrm dY\|_2^2
 \leq\|F_Y\|_2\|\mathrm dY\|_2,
 \qquad \|Y\|_{p_*}\leq\exp(\mathcal P_N).
 \label{flux:energy-bound}$$ For $s_1>0$, put $u_{s_1}=(Y-s_1)_+$ and $A_{s_1}=\{Y>s_1\}$. Testing by $u_{s_1}$, applying Hölder with exponent $2n$, and then using <a href="#flux:background-Sobolev" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:background-Sobolev">[flux:background-Sobolev]</a>, yields <a id="flux:level-norm"></a>$$\|u_{s_1}\|_{p_*}
 \leq C_N |A_{s_1}|^{1/2-1/(2n)},
 \qquad C_N\leq\exp(\mathcal P_N).
 \label{flux:level-norm}$$ Thus, for $t_1>s_1$, <a id="flux:level-recursion"></a>$$|A_{t_1}|\leq
 \left(\frac{C_N}{t_1-s_1}\right)^{p_*}
 |A_{s_1}|^{\vartheta},
 \qquad \vartheta=\frac{n-1}{n-2}>1.
 \label{flux:level-recursion}$$ This also holds for the negative levels of $Y$.

<a id="flux:Y-supremum"></a>
For completeness, start at level one, whose superlevel measure is at most $\exp(\mathcal P_N)$ by <a href="#flux:energy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:energy-bound">[flux:energy-bound]</a>, and set $s_j=1+d_*(1-2^{-j})$. The recursion has the form $M_{j+1}\leq C_N^{p_*}d_*^{-p_*}2^{p_*(j+1)}M_j^\vartheta$. Choosing $d_*$ to be a fixed sufficiently large multiple of $C_N M_0^{(\vartheta-1)/p_*}$ makes $M_j$ tend to zero geometrically after a further fixed exponential rescaling in $j$. All the constants and the starting measure are bounded by $\exp(\mathcal P_N)$, so this choice has the same bound. Therefore $$\|Y\|_\infty\leq\exp(\mathcal P_N)
 \label{flux:Y-supremum}$$ uniformly in the radius and in the homotopy parameter.

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
###### Existence on a truncation and exhaustion.

Fix $N$ and an outer radius. For a continuous trial function $Y$ of zero boundary value, freeze $\mathfrak h(Y)E_{\mathrm{ext}}$ and solve the linear Dirichlet equation <a href="#flux:background-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:background-equation">[flux:background-equation]</a>. On this fixed smooth domain its coefficients are smooth and uniformly elliptic. Linear divergence-form $W^{1,p}$ estimates with any fixed $p>n$ show that this solution operator is continuous and compact into continuous functions of zero boundary value. Fixed points of its homotopy with the zero map satisfy <a href="#flux:Y-supremum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:Y-supremum">[flux:Y-supremum]</a>. Leray–Schauder degree on a larger ball therefore gives a fixed point, since the degree at the zero map is one; see (Leray and Schauder 1934, secs. 12–17) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[LeraySchauder1934](#ref-LeraySchauder1934)].

For such a solution the bounded divergence data first give local Hölder estimates. Then $\mathfrak h(Y)E_{\mathrm{ext}}$ is Hölder continuous, divergence Schauder estimates give $C^{1,\alpha}$ regularity, and successive differentiation gives smoothness. These estimates, with the supremum bound, are uniform on each fixed compact subset as the outer radius tends to infinity. The standard interior and Dirichlet estimates used at this step apply to smooth uniformly elliptic scalar operators on fixed charts; see (Gilbarg and Trudinger 2001, chap. 6 and 8) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[GilbargTrudinger2001](#ref-GilbargTrudinger2001)]. A diagonal subsequence thus gives a smooth solution on $\mathcal M_N$, preserving the energy and supremum bounds.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
###### The floor and inversion.

On a truncation, test <a href="#flux:background-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:background-equation">[flux:background-equation]</a> by $(Y_0-Y)_+$. Its support is a region on which $\mathfrak h(Y)=0$. Ellipticity gives zero gradient for this test function, and its outer boundary trace is zero because $Y_0<0$. Hence $Y\geq Y_0$. This passes to the limit. Lemma <a href="#flux:profiles" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:profiles">36</a> now defines $y=B_0^{-1}(Y)$ everywhere. The inverse is smooth at $Y_0$ as well, since $B_0'(y_0)>0$. Inequality <a href="#flux:B-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:B-growth">[flux:B-growth]</a> and <a href="#flux:Y-supremum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:Y-supremum">[flux:Y-supremum]</a> give $y\leq\mathcal P_N$, while the floor is $y\geq y_0$.

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
###### The end and the total flux.

<a id="flux:exterior-drift"></a>
On the end $\operatorname{div}_{g_b}E_b=0$, so the equation for $Y$ is $$k\Delta_{g_b}Y=\mathfrak h'(Y)E_b(Y).
 \label{flux:exterior-drift}$$ The drift coefficient is $O(r^{-k})$, with fixed-$N$ bounds. Choose $0<\beta'<\min(1,n-2)$. After increasing the fixed inner radius, positive multiples of $r^{2-n}(1-r^{-\beta'})$ are supersolutions of the absolute drift comparison problem: their leading negative Laplacian term has order $r^{-n-\beta'}$, and dominates the metric-error and first-order terms. Comparison with both signs, using the uniform bound on the fixed inner sphere and zero data at the truncation sphere, gives $Y=O(r^{2-n})$ uniformly during exhaustion. Scaled elliptic estimates for <a href="#flux:exterior-drift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:exterior-drift">[flux:exterior-drift]</a> then give the first two differentiated orders, and the same is true for $y$.

Since $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}H_0'\Subset(y_0,0)$ and $y\to0$, one has $H_0(y)=H_0(0)$ identically sufficiently far out. There $Y$ is harmonic for $g_b$. The all-order metric estimates for $g_b$ and scaled harmonic estimates yield $Y,y=O_j(r^{2-n})$ for every fixed $j$. Integrating <a href="#flux:conversion-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:conversion-equation">[flux:conversion-equation]</a> on the whole filled truncation now gives, for every sufficiently large radius, <a id="flux:Y-flux"></a>$$k\int_{S_R}\partial_{\nu_b}Y\,\mathrm dA_{g_b}
 =H_0(0)\int_{S_R}g_b(E_b,\nu_b)\,\mathrm dA_{g_b}
 =H_0(0)\omega|Q|.
 \label{flux:Y-flux}$$ There is no omitted inner flux. Because $B_0'(0)=1$ and $B_0'(y)-1=O(y)$, replacing $\mathrm dY$ by $\mathrm dy$ in this integral has an error $O(R^{2-n})$. This proves <a href="#flux:y-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:y-flux">[flux:y-flux]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### Curvature, mass, and a smooth trapped boundary

<a id="stmt-flux-uncharged-metric"></a>
<div id="flux:uncharged-metric" class="lemma">

<a id="flux:metric-dominance"></a>
<a id="flux:mass-decrease"></a>
<a id="flux:gamma-band-curvature"></a>
**Lemma 38** (The uncharged metric). *Let $y$ be supplied by Proposition <a href="#flux:conversion-solve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:conversion-solve">37</a> and set <a id="flux:gamma"></a>$$\gamma=e^{2y}g_b=e^{2(z+y)}\bar g.
 \label{flux:gamma}$$ Then $R_\gamma\geq0$ on $\mathcal U_N$ and $$\gamma\geq e^{2y_0}g.
 \label{flux:metric-dominance}$$ Its ADM energy is $$\mathcal E(\gamma)=\mathcal E(g_b)-\frac{H_0(0)}{k}|Q|.
 \label{flux:mass-decrease}$$ On the band $b_3/4\leq|h|\leq b_3$ in the original exterior, $$e^{2(z+y)}R_\gamma/2\geq
 c_*+\frac{k^2-1}{8}|\mathrm dt|_{\bar g}^2+
 \mathbf 1_{\{y\geq0\}}\frac{k(k-1)}2|\mathrm dy|_{\bar g}^2.
 \label{flux:gamma-band-curvature}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-uncharged-metric"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-uncharged-metric</summary>

<a id="flux:expanded-y-equation"></a>
*Proof.* Where the extended flux form is closed, expand the equation as $$k\Delta_{g_b}y=
 \frac{H_0'}{B_0'}E_b(y)
 -k\left(k-1+\frac{L'}L\right)|\mathrm dy|_{g_b}^2.
 \label{flux:expanded-y-equation}$$ The conformal scalar formula gives <a id="flux:curvature-quadratic"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 e^{2y}R_\gamma/2
 &=R_{g_b}/2-k\Delta_{g_b}y
       -\frac{k(k-1)}2|\mathrm dy|_{g_b}^2\notag\\
 &\geq c_n^2|E_b|_{g_b}^2
 -\frac{H_0'}{e^{(k-1)y}L}E_b(y)
 +k\left(\frac{k-1}{2}+\frac{L'}L\right)|\mathrm dy|_{g_b}^2.
 \label{flux:curvature-quadratic}
\end{aligned}$$ The last expression is nonnegative: the square of its mixed coefficient is at most <a id="flux:discriminant-check"></a>$$4\frac{k(k-1)}2\,
 k\left(\frac{k-1}{2}+\frac{L'}L\right)
 =k^2(k-1)\left(k-1+2\frac{L'}L\right),
 \label{flux:discriminant-check}$$ exactly by <a href="#flux:profile-discriminant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:profile-discriminant">[flux:profile-discriminant]</a>. Completing the square in $E_b$ proves the assertion on $\mathcal U_N$. The positive terms beyond the electric term in <a href="#flux:background-band-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:background-band-curvature">[flux:background-band-curvature]</a> have not been used in this completion. If $y\geq0$, then $H_0'=0$ and $L'\geq0$, so the entire additional term $k(k-1)|\mathrm dy|_{g_b}^2/2$ can also be retained. Multiplication by $e^{2z}$ proves <a href="#flux:gamma-band-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:gamma-band-curvature">[flux:gamma-band-curvature]</a>. The metric comparison follows from $z\geq0$, $y\geq y_0$, and $\bar g\geq g$.

In coordinates normalized for $g_b$, the conformal ADM calculation is <a id="flux:conformal-energy-formula"></a>$$\mathcal E(e^{2y}g_b)-\mathcal E(g_b)
 =-\frac1\omega\lim_{R\to\infty}
       \int_{S_R}\partial_{\nu_b}y\,\mathrm dA_{g_b}.
 \label{flux:conformal-energy-formula}$$ To check the normalization, the leading perturbation is $2y\delta_{ij}$; its Euclidean ADM integrand is $-2k\partial_i y$. The normalizing factor is $1/(2k\omega)$. The difference between geometric and Euclidean surface fluxes, and every quadratic error, is $O(R^{2-n})$. Equation <a href="#flux:y-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:y-flux">[flux:y-flux]</a> now proves <a href="#flux:mass-decrease" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:mass-decrease">[flux:mass-decrease]</a>. The end scalar curvature remains integrable: outside a compact set $Y$ is harmonic, so <a href="#flux:expanded-y-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:expanded-y-equation">[flux:expanded-y-equation]</a> has no mixed term and $\Delta_{g_b}y=O(r^{2-2n})$, while $R_{g_b}=O(r^{-n-\beta})$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-flux-trace-tensor"></a>
<div id="flux:trace-tensor" class="lemma">

**Lemma 39** (A freely chosen late plateau). *For all sufficiently large $N$ one can choose a smooth nonnegative function $\mathcal H$ of $|h|$, zero on $[0,b_3/4]$ and equal to any prescribed finite nonnegative constant on $[b_3/2,b_3]$, so that the tensor <a id="flux:new-tensor"></a>$$K_{\mathrm{new}}=-\mathfrak a\,\gamma,
 \qquad
 \mathfrak a=e^{-(z+b(y))}\mathcal H(|h|)
 \label{flux:new-tensor}$$ satisfies the neutral dominant energy condition on $\mathcal U_N$. Here $b$ is a fixed smooth function with <a id="flux:b-cutoff"></a>$$b=0\text{ on }(-\infty,0],\qquad 0\leq b'\leq1,
 \qquad y-b(y)\text{ bounded on }[y_0,\infty).
 \label{flux:b-cutoff}$$ The lower bound on $N$ is independent of the prescribed plateau height.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-trace-tensor"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-trace-tensor</summary>

<a id="flux:trace-momentum-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Choose $b'$ smooth, zero up to zero and equal to one eventually, and integrate from zero. Then <a id="flux:C-bounds"></a>$$0<C_-\leq C(y):=e^{y-b(y)}\leq C_+<\infty
 \quad(y\geq y_0),
 \label{flux:C-bounds}$$ with constants independent of $N$. For a pure-trace tensor $-\mathfrak a\gamma$ the neutral densities are <a id="flux:trace-constraints"></a>$$\mu_{\mathrm{new}}=R_\gamma/2+\frac{nk}{2}\mathfrak a^2,
 \qquad J_{\mathrm{new}}=k\,\mathrm d\mathfrak a.
 \label{flux:trace-constraints}$$ On the band, multiply the required inequality by $e^{2(z+y)}$. The new quadratic energy is $nk C^2\mathcal H^2/2$, while <a href="#flux:height-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:height-gradient">[flux:height-gradient]</a> bounds the momentum by $$kC\left(\mathcal H|\mathrm dt+b'\mathrm dy|_{\bar g}
              +(\eta/l)|\mathcal H'|\right).
 \label{flux:trace-momentum-bound}$$ Set $A_t=(k^2-1)/8$ and $B_y=k(k-1)/2$. Young’s inequality absorbs the first term of <a href="#flux:trace-momentum-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:trace-momentum-bound">[flux:trace-momentum-bound]</a> into the two gradient terms in <a href="#flux:gamma-band-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:gamma-band-curvature">[flux:gamma-band-curvature]</a>, at a cost at most $C^2\mathcal H^2\chi_k$, where <a id="flux:dimension-slack"></a>$$\chi_k=\frac{k^2}{4}\left(\frac1{A_t}+\frac1{B_y}\right)
 =\frac{2k^2}{k^2-1}+\frac{k}{2(k-1)}\leq3
 \quad(k\geq3).
 \label{flux:dimension-slack}$$ There is no use of the $y$-gradient term where $y<0$, because $b'=0$ there. Where $y\geq0$, that term was retained in full in the preceding lemma. Hence no curvature term is used twice. As $nk/2\geq6$, the remaining coefficient <a id="flux:positive-slack"></a>$$d_k=\frac{nk}{2}-\chi_k\geq3
 \label{flux:positive-slack}$$ is positive in every dimension under consideration.

<a id="flux:transition-condition"></a>
<a id="flux:ramp-uniformity"></a>
It is now enough that $$(\eta/l)|\mathcal H'|\leq c'(1+\mathcal H^2),
 \qquad
 0<c'\leq
 \min\left\{\frac{c_*}{kC_+},\frac{d_kC_-}{k}\right\}.
 \label{flux:transition-condition}$$ Indeed this makes the second term in <a href="#flux:trace-momentum-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:trace-momentum-bound">[flux:trace-momentum-bound]</a> at most $c_*+d_kC^2\mathcal H^2$. Choose a smooth cutoff $\chi_h$ which is zero on $[0,b_3/4]$ and one on $[b_3/2,b_3]$. For the desired plateau $M<\infty$, define <a id="flux:arctan-ramp"></a>$$\mathcal H(s_1)=\tan\bigl((\arctan M)\chi_h(s_1)\bigr).
 \label{flux:arctan-ramp}$$ Then $$\frac{|\mathcal H'|}{1+\mathcal H^2}
 \leq\frac\pi2\|\chi_h'\|_\infty,
 \label{flux:ramp-uniformity}$$ independently of $M$. Since $\eta/l\leq\ell^{1/2}\to0$, one sufficiently large $N$ ensures <a href="#flux:transition-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:transition-condition">[flux:transition-condition]</a> for every finite plateau. Off the band the tensor vanishes on the required exterior and $R_\gamma\geq0$. Although $|h|$ need not be smooth at its zero set, its composition with $\mathcal H$ is smooth because $\mathcal H$ vanishes on a neighborhood of zero. This proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

Figure <a href="#flux:fig-height-bands" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:fig-height-bands">1</a> records the height bands and the order of the remaining choices.

<figure id="flux:fig-height-bands">

<figcaption>Height bands and the late neutral cut. The bars refer only to the original exterior and encode height, not distance or the topology of level sets. The end component $𝒟_{N}$ stays outside the filling; every component of its boundary is retained. After the two scalar solves and the regular choice of $c_{h}$, the finite plateau $M$ is chosen to make that boundary strictly future trapped, without changing $N$ or either solution. See <a href="#flux:height-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:height-separation">[flux:height-separation]</a>–<a href="#flux:low-region" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:low-region">[flux:low-region]</a>, Lemma <a href="#flux:trace-tensor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:trace-tensor">39</a>, and <a href="#flux:strict-new-boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:strict-new-boundary">[flux:strict-new-boundary]</a>.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="stmt-flux-prepared-bound"></a>
<div id="flux:prepared-bound" class="proposition">

<a id="flux:prepared-inequality"></a>
**Proposition 40** (Prepared numerical bound). *For the prepared data, for every $0<s<1$, $$\mathcal E_*\geq s|Q|+\frac{1-s^2}{2}X_*.
 \label{flux:prepared-inequality}$$ The conclusion also holds for prepared data carrying a smooth compactly supported closed magnetic two-form, with the charged energy convention used in the filled scalar construction.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-prepared-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-prepared-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Keep $s$, a smooth profile, and $\varepsilon$ fixed. Take $N$ sufficiently large for all preceding constructions. Sard’s theorem gives a regular value $c_h\in(b_3/2,b_3)$ of $|h|$. Let $\mathcal D_N$ be the closure of the component of $\{|h|<c_h\}$ containing the entire far end. By <a href="#flux:height-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:height-separation">[flux:height-separation]</a>, this component cannot cross $S$ into the filling. Its entire boundary is a compact smooth embedded two-sided hypersurface in the original interior. It is nonempty: a path in the original connected exterior from the far end to $S$ must cross the chosen level. All boundary components are retained.

<a id="flux:strict-new-boundary"></a>
The mean curvature of this fixed compact boundary in $\gamma$ is finite. We may now choose the plateau $M$ in Lemma <a href="#flux:trace-tensor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:trace-tensor">39</a> so large that, with normal toward the end, $$H_{\partial\mathcal D_N}
       +\operatorname{tr}_{\partial\mathcal D_N}K_{\mathrm{new}}
 =H_{\partial\mathcal D_N}-k\mathfrak a<0
 \quad\hbox{on every component}.
 \label{flux:strict-new-boundary}$$ This late choice does not require repeating either scalar solve or increasing $N$, by <a href="#flux:ramp-uniformity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:ramp-uniformity">[flux:ramp-uniformity]</a>. Restricted to $\mathcal D_N$, the new tensor has compact support, since $h\to0$ and $\mathcal H$ vanishes near zero.

We check the neutral input’s hypotheses explicitly. The exterior $\mathcal D_N$ is connected and oriented, smooth up to its nonempty compact boundary, and has exactly the original coordinate end with compact complement. It is complete with boundary included: it is closed in the original complete exterior, and <a href="#flux:metric-dominance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:metric-dominance">[flux:metric-dominance]</a> bounds its metric below by a positive constant times $g$. The metric is smooth and has all-order differentiated decay of order $2-n$ in normalized end coordinates. Thus the metric and tensor satisfy the stronger falloffs in Theorem <a href="#thm:neutral-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:neutral-input">4</a>, with any exponent strictly between $(n-2)/2$ and $n-2$. The neutral DEC follows from Lemma <a href="#flux:trace-tensor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:trace-tensor">39</a>; its matter densities are integrable because the tensor is compactly supported and the scalar curvature is integrable at infinity. The ADM momentum is zero. The ADM energy exists and is given by <a href="#flux:mass-decrease" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:mass-decrease">[flux:mass-decrease]</a>. Finally the new boundary is strictly future outer trapped by <a href="#flux:strict-new-boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:strict-new-boundary">[flux:strict-new-boundary]</a>.

Every full cut of $\mathcal D_N$, regarded as a subset of the original exterior, is also a full cut there. This includes any portions coincident with $\partial\mathcal D_N$, and counts every component. Therefore its full-cut infimum $a_N$ satisfies <a id="flux:area-retention"></a>$$a_N\geq e^{ky_0}a_*>0.
 \label{flux:area-retention}$$ Indeed the metric comparison acts on all $k$ tangent directions, and the class of available cuts has only been restricted. No minimizing boundary is assumed to be smooth or to exist.

<a id="flux:prelimit-bound"></a>
It remains to verify the timelike energy hypothesis of the neutral input for these auxiliary data. Write $e_N=\mathcal E(\gamma)$. If $e_N>0$, their zero momentum makes that hypothesis immediate. If $e_N\leq0$, apply the conformal end increase of Lemma <a href="#prep:end-increase" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prep:end-increase">12</a>, taking its positive function $\phi$ constant on a core containing the boundary and the new tensor’s support. For $U=1+\lambda\phi$, transform the data by <a id="flux:neutral-end-increase"></a>$$\gamma_\lambda=U^{4/(n-2)}\gamma,
 \qquad K_\lambda=U^{2/(n-2)}K_{\mathrm{new}}.
 \label{flux:neutral-end-increase}$$ The neutral DEC and trapping signs persist, the stronger asymptotic derivatives persist, momentum remains zero, and full-cut areas do not decrease. Its energy is $e_N+2\lambda$. For every arbitrarily small $e>0$ choose $\lambda=(e-e_N)/2\geq0$. These data satisfy all hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:neutral-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:neutral-input">4</a>, which would give <a id="flux:nonpositive-contradiction"></a>$$e\geq\frac12(a_N/\omega)^{(k-1)/k}
 \geq\frac12e^{(k-1)y_0}X_*>0.
 \label{flux:nonpositive-contradiction}$$ Letting $e\downarrow0$ is a contradiction. Thus $e_N>0$, and the same neutral input applied directly gives $$e^{(k-1)\varepsilon}(\mathcal E_*+o_N(1))
 -\frac{H_0(0)}k|Q|
 \geq\frac12 e^{(k-1)y_0}X_*
 =\frac{1-s^2}{2}X_*.
 \label{flux:prelimit-bound}$$

The parameter order is as follows. For the fixed prepared data, fix $s\in(0,1)$, a profile tolerance, and $\varepsilon>0$ first. Complete the two scalar solves and the regular cut for each sufficiently large $N$; the tensor plateau is chosen last for that $N$. Now let $N\to\infty$ in <a href="#flux:prelimit-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:prelimit-bound">[flux:prelimit-bound]</a>, then let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, and then let the profile tolerance decrease to zero. Equations <a href="#flux:profile-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:profile-value">[flux:profile-value]</a> and <a href="#flux:floor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:floor">[flux:floor]</a> give <a href="#flux:prepared-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:prepared-inequality">[flux:prepared-inequality]</a>. Only fixed-parameter estimates were required in the second scalar solve. The electromagnetic input used in this proof was precisely <a href="#flux:graph-form-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:graph-form-norm">[flux:graph-form-norm]</a> and its lower bound by $I_g(w)$, which also holds for the indicated compact magnetic form. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-48"></a>
#### Return to the original data and optimize

<a id="stmt-flux-magnetic-extension"></a>
<div id="flux:magnetic-extension" class="corollary">

<a id="flux:magnetic-s-family"></a>
**Corollary 41** (Strict compact-magnetic numerical extension). *Let the original exterior satisfy the numerical geometric and asymptotic hypotheses, and let $D_m$ be an arbitrary smooth compactly supported closed two-form. With $\alpha=i_E\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g$ closed, use the electromagnetic convention <a id="flux:magnetic-convention"></a>$$I_g(v)=c_n^2\bigl(|E|_g^2+|D_m|_g^2+2D_m(v,E)\bigr),
 \qquad
 \mu_m+J_m(v)=\mu+J(v)-I_g(v).
 \label{flux:magnetic-convention}$$ Assume also that $\mu_m,|J_m|_g$ are integrable and that the ADM and charge flux limits exist with the stated normalizations. Assume the ADM vector is future timelike, the fixed componentwise trapping inequalities are strict, and for some $c>0$ and $0<\beta<\min(1,q)$, <a id="flux:strict-magnetic-margin"></a>$$\mu_m-|J_m|_g\geq c r^{-n-\beta}.
 \label{flux:strict-magnetic-margin}$$ Then, with the original full-cut infimum $a$ and the original normalized electric charge $Q$, $$m\geq s|Q|+\frac{1-s^2}{2}X_A\quad(0<s<1).
 \label{flux:magnetic-s-family}$$ Equivalently, $m\geq|Q|$, and when $X_A>|Q|$ one also has $m\geq(X_A+Q^2/X_A)/2$. No magnetic equality classification or non-timelike extension is asserted here.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-magnetic-extension"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-magnetic-extension</summary>

*Proof.* Apply the rest preparation and strict preparation in Proposition <a href="#prep:rest-preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prep:rest-preparation">17</a> and Lemma <a href="#prep:strictification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prep:strictification">15</a>, retaining the two closed forms. The magnetic support stays in the compact original region. There the strictly positive margin absorbs the arbitrarily small rescaling errors. The gluing and bending annuli can be taken outside that support, so on those annuli and on the end the electric estimates are unchanged. This yields prepared data with charge $Q$, energies $\mathcal E_{*,j}\to m$, and full-cut infima $a_{*,j}\geq(1-o_j(1))a$. For each fixed $s$, apply Proposition <a href="#flux:prepared-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:prepared-bound">40</a> and then pass to this limit. The right side is increasing in the area variable, so the lower comparison of infima is sufficient. This proves <a href="#flux:magnetic-s-family" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:magnetic-s-family">[flux:magnetic-s-family]</a>. The elementary optimization is given in Proposition <a href="#prep:prepared-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prep:prepared-reduction">19</a>. The argument does not require the component electric fluxes to have one sign. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-magnetic-extension-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-magnetic-extension</summary>

*Completion of Theorem <a href="#thm:numerical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:numerical">5</a>.* Proposition <a href="#flux:prepared-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:prepared-bound">40</a> establishes <a href="#prep:prepared-bound-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prep:prepared-bound-hypothesis">[prep:prepared-bound-hypothesis]</a> for every prepared data set with positive energy and zero momentum, and every fixed $s\in(0,1)$. The Reduction of the Numerical Theorem (Proposition <a href="#prep:prepared-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prep:prepared-reduction">19</a>) therefore proves Theorem <a href="#thm:numerical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:numerical">5</a>, including positivity of the full enclosing area, strict future timelikeness of the original ADM vector, and the stated branch distinction. The endpoint limits in $s$ are taken after the fixed-$s$ inequality is established, so no profile estimate uniform at $s=0$ or $s=1$ is required. ◻

</details>

<a id="var:section"></a>
### The original-data variational identity

We now assume the connected-horizon hypotheses of Definition <a href="#def:rigidity-class" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:rigidity-class">6</a> and equality in Theorem <a href="#thm:numerical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:numerical">5</a>, with $m>|Q|$. All objects in this section belong to the original exterior $(\Omega,g,K,E)$. In particular, no equality statement about one of the auxiliary metrics is used. We first differentiate the full enclosing-area infimum, then separate the active constraint inequalities from a direction that would improve equality. The resulting multipliers are initially measures. Their regularity and the elimination of their possible singular area sheets are separate steps. Decrease the asymptotic exponent, if necessary, so that $(n-2)/2<q<n-2$; this retains every original hypothesis.

Write $A=\mathop{\mathrm{Area}}_g(S)$, $k=n-1$, and <a id="var:constants"></a>$$r_h=(A/\omega)^{1/k},\qquad
 b^0=\frac{\mathcal E_{\mathrm{ADM}}}{m},\qquad
 b^i=-\frac{(\mathbf P_{\mathrm{ADM}})_i}{m},\qquad
 c=\frac{k-1}{r_h}\left(1-\frac{Q^2}{r_h^{2(k-1)}}\right)>0.
 \label{var:constants}$$ The vector $b$ is fixed when taking variations. Set $\mathcal B=b^0\mathcal E_{\mathrm{ADM}}+\sum_i b^i
(\mathbf P_{\mathrm{ADM}})_i$. Thus $m'=\mathcal B'$ at the original data. On the outer, nonextremal area branch, differentiation at fixed $Q$ gives <a id="var:area-coefficient"></a>$$\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dA}\frac12
 \left(r_h^{k-1}+Q^2r_h^{-(k-1)}\right)
 =\frac{c}{2k\omega}.
 \label{var:area-coefficient}$$ The strictly positive coefficient in this formula will be essential.

Throughout the variations, an electric field is represented by the closed flux form $\alpha=i_E\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g$. We also allow a closed, compactly supported two-form $D_m$, initially zero, representing the spatial Faraday form divided by $c_n$. For these extended data put <a id="var:magnetic-constraints"></a>$$\begin{split}
 \mu_m&=\frac12(R_g+\tau^2-|K|^2)
              -c_n^2(|E|^2+|D_m|^2),\\
 J_m&=\operatorname{div}(K-\tau g)-2c_n^2D_m(\,\cdot\,,E).
 \end{split}
 \label{var:magnetic-constraints}$$ Only the strictly feasible numerical extension of Corollary <a href="#flux:magnetic-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:magnetic-extension">41</a> will be applied to data with $D_m\ne0$. No magnetic equality theorem is assumed. Compactly supported closed variations of either form, including tests supported up to $S$, do not change $Q$, by Stokes’ theorem on a truncation.

<a id="pm-h-50"></a>
#### The full area envelope

Choose a compact smooth filling $O$ behind $S$, with a smooth extension of the metric, and denote the resulting boundary-free manifold by $\mathcal M$. The filling is used only to define perimeter competitors; no constraint inequality is required there. A filled competitor is a bounded finite-perimeter set $F\subset\mathcal M$ containing $O$ up to null sets. Its perimeter counts the part coinciding with $\partial O=S$.

<a id="stmt-var-area-envelope"></a>
<div id="var:area-envelope" class="proposition">

**Proposition 42** (Area envelope and regular patches). *The least perimeter of filled competitors equals the full smooth-cut infimum $a$. The family $\mathcal F_*$ of minimizing filled sets, considered modulo null sets, is compact in local $L^1$, and all its members are supported in a common compact set. Their exterior end components have the full-boundary convention of Definition <a href="#def:full-cut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:full-cut">3</a>.*

*Each minimizing frontier is smooth and minimal at its free regular points. Its possible singular set has Hausdorff dimension at most $n-8$. All contact points with $S$ are regular of class $C^{1,\gamma}$ for some $\gamma>0$, and their graph representations belong to $W^{2,p}$ for every finite $p$. At an interior regular point of one frontier, any other minimizing frontier through the point coincides with it locally. On a smaller neighborhood the minimizing frontiers that meet that neighborhood are disjoint single graphs, with uniform smooth interior estimates.*

<a id="var:envelope-derivative"></a>
*For a differentiable metric path with $g'_0=h$, the right derivative of its enclosing-area infimum is $$a'_+(h)=\min_{F\in\mathcal F_*} a'_F(h),\qquad
 a'_F(h)=\frac12\int_{\partial^*F}
                       \operatorname{tr}_{T\partial^*F}h\,\mathrm dA_g.
 \label{var:envelope-derivative}$$ Here and below $\partial^*F$ is used for integration, while “frontier” means the support of its perimeter measure.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-area-envelope"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-area-envelope</summary>

*Proof.* Large coordinate spheres have positive mean curvature, uniformly for the finite-dimensional metric paths considered below. On the region outside one such sphere, the outward unit normal to their foliation has positive divergence. Applying the divergence theorem to the portion of a filled competitor outside that sphere shows that clipping off that portion cannot increase perimeter, and strictly decreases it if the discarded portion has positive volume. The argument holds for almost every cutting radius by finite-perimeter slicing. Choose two fixed radii, both outside the compact core. Clipping at the smaller radius gives a common compact truncation, strictly inside the larger one, for minimization and for all sufficiently small parameters of each path. BV compactness and lower semicontinuity now give a minimizer; see (Ambrosio, Fusco, and Pallara 2000) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[AmbrosioFuscoPallara2000](#ref-AmbrosioFuscoPallara2000)].

A smooth full cut bounds a filled competitor with exactly the same area, including its contact portions. Conversely, first push $O$ and an arbitrary filled competitor slightly outward by a smooth ambient diffeomorphism supported in a collar of $S$. The pushed competitor contains a neighborhood of the original $O$, and its perimeter tends to the original perimeter. Strict approximation of finite-perimeter sets by smooth domains can therefore be carried out while retaining that neighborhood. It is supported inside a fixed compact truncation. Retain the exterior component containing the end, filling any bounded exterior components of the smoothed set. This only removes boundary components. Its entire smooth intrinsic boundary is a full cut. Passing through these two approximations proves equality of the infima. The same removal observation shows that a minimizing frontier cannot leave a removable bounded complementary open component: a nonempty such component has positive perimeter, whose removal would improve the minimum. The usual indecomposable-component decomposition gives the same conclusion for finite-perimeter representatives (Ambrosio, Fusco, and Pallara 2000) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[AmbrosioFuscoPallara2000](#ref-AmbrosioFuscoPallara2000)].

<a id="var:obstacle-submodularity"></a>
<a id="var:quasiminimizing"></a>
Here is the local estimate needed at the obstacle. For a local competitor $G$, replace it by $G\cup O$. Submodularity gives $$P(G\cup O)\le P(G)+P(O)-P(G\cap O).
 \label{var:obstacle-submodularity}$$ Extend the smooth outward normal of $O$ to a smooth vector field of norm at most one near its boundary. Its divergence is bounded. The calibration inequality for this field gives $P(O)-P(G\cap O)\le C|O\setminus G|$ for changes in a sufficiently small coordinate neighborhood. Since $F$ minimizes among sets containing $O$, it follows that $$P(F;B)\le P(G;B)+C|F\mathbin{\triangle}G|
 \label{var:quasiminimizing}$$ whenever $F\mathbin{\triangle}G$ is compactly contained in the small ball $B$. This is an unconstrained perimeter almost-minimizing estimate. In coordinates the Riemannian area integrand is the ellipsoidal integrand associated with $\mathcal A=(\det g)g^{-1}$, which is smooth and uniformly elliptic. The error in <a href="#var:quasiminimizing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:quasiminimizing">[var:quasiminimizing]</a> is $O(r^n)$ and therefore is bounded by $C_\gamma r^{n-1+\gamma}$ for any fixed $0<\gamma<1$ on sufficiently small balls. The almost-minimizer results in (Simmons 2022, Propositions 4.5–4.7 and Theorems 1.1, 9.5, 10.2–10.3) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Simmons2022](#ref-Simmons2022)] apply. They give density and perimeter bounds, local compactness, regularity off a set of dimension at most $n-8$, and convergence as single $C^1$ graphs near free regular limit points. On free patches, the uniform local $C^{1,\gamma/4}$ graph bounds and the smooth minimal-graph equation upgrade this to smooth convergence on smaller patches by interior elliptic estimates. Tangent sets are perimeter-minimizing cones. The minimal-boundary formulation and dimension reduction are also described in (Simon 2014, chap. 7, Section 5) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[SimonGMT2014](#ref-SimonGMT2014)].

At a contact point a tangent filled cone contains the tangent half-space of $O$. The corresponding minimizing hypercone is therefore supported on one side of its boundary plane, and is that plane. One way to see the last assertion is to integrate the signed coordinate-height equation on its stationary spherical link; the height has one sign and its spherical Laplacian is a negative constant times itself. The singular set can be removed in this integration by its dimension and the perimeter bounds. Equivalently one can use the half-space cone theorem in (Simon 2014, chap. 7, Section 4, Theorem 4.5) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[SimonGMT2014](#ref-SimonGMT2014)]. The almost-minimizer regularity theorem now gives a $C^{1,\beta}$ graph at contact for some $\beta>0$; one may take $\beta=\gamma/4$. Estimate <a href="#var:quasiminimizing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:quasiminimizing">[var:quasiminimizing]</a>, differentiated along smooth flows, bounds its weak mean curvature. Difference quotients in the uniformly elliptic prescribed-mean-curvature graph equation give $W^{2,2}$ regularity. Written in nondivergence form, that equation has Hölder coefficients and bounded right-hand side, so local $W^{2,p}$ estimates give all finite $p$; see (Gilbarg and Trudinger 2001) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[GilbargTrudinger2001](#ref-GilbargTrudinger2001)].

If two minimizing frontiers meet at a free regular point of one of them, their union and intersection also minimize by <a href="#var:obstacle-submodularity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:obstacle-submodularity">[var:obstacle-submodularity]</a>, with equality in the sum of the perimeters. They are ordered relative to the given regular sheet. Their tangent cones at contact are one-sided relative to its tangent plane, and hence are planes. Regularity and the strong comparison principle for the minimal graph equation imply local coincidence. Strict compactness and regular convergence then give the asserted uniform graph neighborhoods: otherwise a sequence of failing frontiers would converge strictly to a minimizer through the point, where multiplicity-one regular convergence gives precisely the missing neighborhood. The difference of two distinct ordered graphs solves a linear uniformly elliptic equation. Positive-solution Harnack and interior derivative estimates imply, on smaller patches, that its gradient is bounded by a uniform constant times its pointwise separation.

Finally let $F_t$ minimize for $g_t$. Compactness, lower semicontinuity and comparison with a fixed $g_0$ minimizer show that every convergent subsequence at $t=0$ converges strictly in perimeter to an element of $\mathcal F_*$. Strict continuity for vector measures then gives convergence of the tangent-plane area measures (Ambrosio, Fusco, and Pallara 2000) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[AmbrosioFuscoPallara2000](#ref-AmbrosioFuscoPallara2000)]. The area Taylor expansion has a uniform remainder on the resulting perimeter-bounded family. Fixed minimizers give the upper bound in <a href="#var:envelope-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:envelope-derivative">[var:envelope-derivative]</a>; the varying minimizers give the lower bound. This also proves compactness of the minimizing family and continuity of $F\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}\OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}a'_F(h)$ in its strict topology. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-51"></a>
#### Strict directions and positive-measure separation

An active ray is a pair $(x,v)$ with $|v|_g\le1$ and $\mu_m(x)+J_m(x)(v)=0$. Under a metric variation $h$, transport the unit ball by the metric square root, so $v'=-h^\sharp v/2$. Denote by $\mathfrak C'$ the variation of $2(\mu_m+J_m(v))$ under that transport. Our test space consists of finite linear combinations of:

1.  arbitrary compact smooth variations $(h,p,\alpha',D_m')$, including support up to $S$, with $p=K'$ and both form variations closed;

2.  a metric end prototype $h=r^{2-n}\delta$, cut off toward the core;

3.  second-tensor end prototypes whose Euclidean trace reversal is <a id="var:momentum-prototype"></a>$$\Pi_{ij}=r^{-k}
       \bigl(a_i n_j^\delta+a_j n_i^\delta
                         -(a\cdot n^\delta)\delta_{ij}\bigr),
      \qquad n^\delta=x/r,
      \label{var:momentum-prototype}$$ with arbitrary constant $a\in\mathbb R^n$ and a cutoff toward the core;

4.  the strict conformal direction $(h,p)=(2\phi g,\phi K)$, with both forms fixed.

<a id="var:strict-tail"></a>
For the last direction choose $0<\beta<\min(1,q)$, put $\rho_0=r^{-n-\beta}$ with a positive smooth extension of the radial weight, and take <a id="var:strict-direction"></a>$$-\Delta\phi=B\sqrt{|\mathrm d\phi|^2+r^{-2k}}+\rho_0,
 \qquad \partial_\nu\phi=-1,
 \qquad \phi(\infty)=0,
 \label{var:strict-direction}$$ where $B\ge |K|$ is smooth and has simple symbol bounds $B=O_j(r^{-1-q})$. The strictification construction in Lemma <a href="#prep:strictification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prep:strictification">15</a> applies to this decaying drift: its radial barriers dominate the drift because $\beta<q$. It gives $\phi>0$, $\phi=O_2(r^{2-n})$ and finite gradient flux. In particular $$\frac{-\Delta\phi}{\rho_0}\longrightarrow1,
 \qquad
 \frac{\mathfrak C'_{\rm strict}}{\rho_0}>0
 \text{ on active rays},\qquad
 \frac{\mathfrak C'_{\rm strict}}{\rho_0}\longrightarrow2k,
 \qquad -\theta'_{\rm strict}=k.
 \label{var:strict-tail}$$ Here the extra algebraic electric term is nonnegative: at a purely electric active ray the conformal derivative of $\mathfrak C$ is <a id="var:conformal-active-derivative"></a>$$-2k\Delta\phi+2kK(\nabla\phi,v)
                  +4(k-1)c_n^2\phi|E|^2.
 \label{var:conformal-active-derivative}$$

The prototypes have zero flat linearized constraints on the end. For example $\Pi_{ij}=r^{-n}(a_ix_j+a_jx_i-(a\cdot x)\delta_{ij})$ satisfies $\partial_j\Pi_{ij}=0$ and $\Pi_{ij}n^j=r^{-k}a_i$. Its momentum derivative is $a_i/k$; the scalar prototype has energy derivative $(n-2)/2$. The background errors in the linearized constraints are $O(r^{-n-q})$. Thus the prototypes span the ADM flux derivatives and their normalized active-constraint values tend to zero.

<a id="stmt-var-multiplier-identity"></a>
<div id="var:multiplier-identity" class="proposition">

<a id="var:separation"></a>
**Proposition 43** (Multiplier identity). *There are a probability measure $\pi$ on $\mathcal F_*$, a nonnegative locally finite measure $\Lambda$ on the active rays, and a nonnegative finite measure $\zeta$ on $S$, such that $$2k\omega\mathcal B'-c\int_{\mathcal F_*}a'_F(h)\,\mathrm d\pi(F)
 =\int\mathfrak C'\,\mathrm d\Lambda-\int_S\theta'_+\,\mathrm d\zeta
 \label{var:separation}$$ for every compact test and every end prototype above. No surjectivity of a constraint map is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-multiplier-identity"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-multiplier-identity</summary>

*Proof.* Consider the linear image of the test space with components <a id="var:separation-map"></a>$$\left(\mathfrak C'/\rho_0,\ -\theta'_+,\
       \{c a'_F(h)-2k\omega\mathcal B'\}_{F\in\mathcal F_*}\right).
 \label{var:separation-map}$$ The constraint test space is the one-point compactification of the closed active-ray set, with one artificial infinity point added even if that set is bounded. At infinity assign $2k$ times the coefficient of the strict direction. The other two test spaces are $S$ and the compact minimizing family. The normalized functions are continuous by <a href="#var:strict-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:strict-tail">[var:strict-tail]</a> and Proposition <a href="#var:area-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:area-envelope">42</a>.

This image does not meet the open cone of functions strictly positive on the disjoint union of the three compact test spaces. Indeed such a direction has positive strict-direction coefficient because of the infinity test. Apply that positive conformal direction exponentially to the data after making the other variations additively. On compact subsets, strict first-order positivity on the active rays implies feasibility for all sufficiently small positive parameters: rays outside a fixed neighborhood of the active set have a positive zeroth-order gap. On the end the nonconformal linear errors are $O(r^{-n-q})$ and are dominated by $\rho_0$. After retaining the nonnegative background through the conformal formula, the quadratic errors are bounded by $Ct^2\rho_0$. The strict first-order term therefore gives $\mu_m-|J_m|\ge c_t\rho_0$, with $c_t>0$. The forms remain closed; a newly created magnetic form has fixed compact support. Trapping is strict, the ADM vector remains future timelike, and completeness, decay, integrability and existence and differentiability of the fluxes are preserved. These are precisely the hypotheses of Corollary <a href="#flux:magnetic-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:magnetic-extension">41</a>. The area infimum stays positive by uniform metric comparison on the compact core and the controlled tail. Equation <a href="#var:envelope-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:envelope-derivative">[var:envelope-derivative]</a> and strict positivity of the objective components would now violate the numerical inequality for small positive parameter.

Hahn–Banach separation of a linear subspace from an open convex cone gives a nonzero positive functional on the continuous functions on this compact disjoint union. The Riesz representation theorem supplies the three nonnegative measures. Its objective measure has positive mass. Otherwise testing the strict direction, uniformly positive on the two constraint test spaces, would force both remaining measures to vanish as well. Normalize the objective measure to have mass one. On finite active rays divide the constraint measure by $\rho_0$ to obtain $\Lambda$. This measure is finite over compact subsets, including the boundary. The possible infinity atom contributes nothing on compact tests or prototypes. Rearranging the annihilation identity gives <a href="#var:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:separation">[var:separation]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-52"></a>
#### Normal-motion tests and the sheets selected by the multiplier

<a id="var:moment-complementarity"></a>
Let $u$ and $X$ be the scalar and vector moments of $\Lambda$. Initially these are measures, using the fixed Riemannian volume-density convention; thus $u$ also denotes the scalar distribution defined by its moment measure. Positivity and the definition of the active set imply $$u\ge |X|_g,\qquad u\mu_m+J_m(X)=0
 \label{var:moment-complementarity}$$ as measure identities. In particular, electromagnetic or metric terms of the ray derivative require moments of order at most one.

<a id="stmt-var-trace-free-sheets"></a>
<div id="var:trace-free-sheets" class="lemma">

**Lemma 44** (Tangential trace on free sheets). *For $\pi$-almost every minimizing frontier, $\operatorname{tr}_{T\partial F}K=0$ on each of its free regular sheets. Every such frontier either equals $S$, or is wholly inside $\operatorname{int}\Omega$ and satisfies $H=\operatorname{tr}_{T\partial F}K=0$ on its regular part.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-trace-free-sheets"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-trace-free-sheets</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a smooth lapse test $s$ compactly supported in the open exterior, and choose a local Gaussian Lorentzian metric inducing $g,K$ at time zero. Its spatial metric and first normal jet are fixed, while its second normal metric jets are free. Choose a two-form whose initial electric and spatial components are $c_n E$ and zero. Its first normal derivatives can be chosen so that both Maxwell equations hold on the initial slice. In general dimension the tangential Maxwell constraints are $\mathrm dD_m=0$ and $\mathrm d\alpha=0$; the remaining equations prescribe the $n(n-1)/2$ magnetic and $n$ electric normal evolution components. The induced flux-form variations under normal motion with velocity $sN$ are compactly supported and closed, as follows also from Cartan’s formula applied to these initial Maxwell jets.

<a id="var:normal-jet-matter"></a>
Let $\mathsf S$ denote the electromagnetic stress tensor with the normalization in Definition <a href="#def:normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:normalization">2</a>. It is divergence-free at the initial slice. For each $\delta>0$, prescribe there the spatial components of Einstein minus $\mathsf S$ to be $$T^{(\delta)}_{ij}
       =\frac{(J_m)_i(J_m)_j}{\mu_m+\delta}.
 \label{var:normal-jet-matter}$$ Spatial trace reversal of the second normal metric jets is invertible, so these are legitimate smooth jet prescriptions. The normal and mixed components remain $\mu_m,J_m$ by Gauss–Codazzi. The resulting variation has $h=2sK$.

<a id="var:matter-jet-bounds"></a>
We check that $\mathfrak C'\to0$ uniformly on compact active test sets. The tensors in <a href="#var:normal-jet-matter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:normal-jet-matter">[var:normal-jet-matter]</a> converge spatially in $C^1$. On $\{\mu_m>0\}$ this is ordinary smooth convergence; near a zero of $\mu_m$, DEC gives $|J_m|\le\mu_m$ and smoothness gives $\mathrm d\mu_m=\nabla J_m=0$ at that zero. The bounds $$|T^{(\delta)}|\le\mu_m,
 \qquad
 |\nabla T^{(\delta)}|
       \le 2|\nabla J_m|+|\mathrm d\mu_m|
 \label{var:matter-jet-bounds}$$ make the convergence uniform across the zero set. At a positive-density active ray, the limiting spacetime tensor is $\gamma\otimes\gamma/\mu_m$, where $\gamma(N)=\mu_m$ and $\gamma|_{T\Omega}=J_m$. It is causal and annihilates the null vector $N+v$. Parallel-transport that null vector along spatial curves. Its contraction with the causal covector is nonnegative and vanishes at the base point; differentiation at the minimum therefore shows that all relevant spatial covariant derivatives, with last argument $N+v$, vanish. Bianchi and the initial Maxwell equations then give the same limiting vanishing for the normal derivative contracted with $N,N+v$. Changes of the first argument cost zero, and changes of the second cost zero because it remains null and $\gamma$ is proportional to its metric dual. At a matter zero, both the tensor and its spatial derivatives vanish by <a href="#var:matter-jet-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:matter-jet-bounds">[var:matter-jet-bounds]</a>, and the divergence equation again suffices. These observations prove the asserted uniform convergence of $\mathfrak C'$.

<a id="var:weighted-trace-zero"></a>
Apply <a href="#var:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:separation">[var:separation]</a> to these tests and pass to the limit. They have no boundary or ADM variation, so $$\int_{\mathcal F_*}\int_{\partial^*F}
       s\operatorname{tr}_{T\partial^*F}K\,\mathrm dA_g\,\mathrm d\pi(F)=0.
 \label{var:weighted-trace-zero}$$ At free regular points, Proposition <a href="#var:area-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:area-envelope">42</a> provides uniform noncrossing graph patches, and frontiers through a common point have the same tangent plane. Consequently the tangential trace is a single-valued continuous function on each such patch of the integrated surface measure. There is no cancellation between different tangent planes in <a href="#var:weighted-trace-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:weighted-trace-zero">[var:weighted-trace-zero]</a>. A countable cover and Fubini imply the first conclusion, initially almost everywhere and then everywhere on regular sheets by smoothness.

At contact, the $W^{2,p}$ graph and the smooth obstacle have the same second jets almost everywhere on their coincidence set. The frontier therefore satisfies $H+\operatorname{tr}_{\rm tan}K=0$ almost everywhere throughout the contact graph, including its free portion. Uniform ellipticity first improves its regularity and then bootstraps it to a smooth solution of the expansion equation. The strong comparison principle gives coincidence with $S$ near contact. The set of coincidence is open and closed along the connected $S$, so the whole $S$ is present. Its area is already the minimum $A$, leaving no area for another sheet. If there is no contact, the compact frontier is separated from $S$, and the stated equations hold on all its regular points. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-53"></a>
#### The moment equations and their initial regularity

<a id="stmt-var-moment-regularity"></a>
<div id="var:moment-regularity" class="proposition">

<a id="var:first-kid"></a>
<a id="var:hessian-measure"></a>
**Proposition 45** (Regularity of the measure moments). *In the open exterior the moments are functions satisfying the first Killing initial data (KID) equation $$\operatorname{sym}\nabla X=-uK,
 \label{var:first-kid}$$ and $$\operatorname{Hess}u
  =\mathcal L(u,X,\nabla X)
    +\frac c2\int_{\mathcal F_*}
       \nu_F^\flat\otimes\nu_F^\flat\,
                       \mathrm dA_F\,\mathrm d\pi(F).
 \label{var:hessian-measure}$$ The equations are distributional, and surface measures in the second equation are expressed relative to ambient volume. The operator $\mathcal L$ has smooth linear coefficients. It is specifically the lower-order operator from the metric adjoint in <a href="#var:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:separation">[var:separation]</a>, with the flux form fixed and the unit-ball rays transported as specified above. In particular, it is not an arbitrary operator with smooth coefficients. Locally $u$ is Lipschitz and $X$ is $C^{1,1}$. The complementarity relations <a href="#var:moment-complementarity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:moment-complementarity">[var:moment-complementarity]</a> hold pointwise.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-moment-regularity"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-moment-regularity</summary>

*Proof.* For a compactly supported interior variation $K'=p$, the coefficient after integration by parts is <a id="var:second-tensor-adjoint"></a>$$2\bigl[u(\tau g-K)-\operatorname{sym}\nabla X
                                  +(\operatorname{div}X)g\bigr]:p.
 \label{var:second-tensor-adjoint}$$ Its trace gives $\operatorname{div}X=-u\tau$, and its trace-free part then gives <a href="#var:first-kid" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:first-kid">[var:first-kid]</a>. For metric tests the scalar-curvature principal adjoint is $\operatorname{Hess}u-(\Delta u)g$. Connection variations in the momentum term give smooth linear expressions in $u,X,\nabla X$; fixed-flux field terms and the ray-transport term are algebraic in those moments. The area term has coefficient $-c(g-\nu_F^\flat\otimes\nu_F^\flat)/2$ on this side of <a href="#var:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:separation">[var:separation]</a>. Inverting trace reversal gives <a href="#var:hessian-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:hessian-measure">[var:hessian-measure]</a>, with the displayed positive normal-normal measure.

Differentiating <a href="#var:first-kid" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:first-kid">[var:first-kid]</a> and commuting covariant derivatives expresses every component of $\nabla^2X$ as a smooth linear combination of $\operatorname{Rm}*X$, $K*\mathrm du$ and $(\nabla K)*u$. Taking traces gives a strongly elliptic Laplace system for $(u,X)$ with smooth lower-order coefficients. Its source measure has local growth $O(r^{n-1})$, uniformly in the minimizer, by the local perimeter bound. A localized elliptic parametrix has order $-2$ and kernel bounds $C|x-y|^{2-n-j}$ for $j=0,1$. Such a potential of a measure with this growth is locally $C^\gamma$ for every $0<\gamma<1$: split the potential difference at twice the point separation, use the growth bound nearby and the first kernel derivative on successive outer annuli to get $O(r(1+|\log r|))$. Lower-order terms are included in the parametrix, and the homogeneous remainder is smooth. Thus $u,X$ are Hölder continuous.

The traced differentiated equation for $X$ now has the form of the divergence of a Hölder field plus a Hölder function; local elliptic estimates give $X\in C^{1,\gamma}$, after decreasing $\gamma$ if needed. The lower term in <a href="#var:hessian-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:hessian-measure">[var:hessian-measure]</a> is therefore continuous. Its positive measure term implies a local lower bound for the covariant Hessian in the distributional sense. Slice this inequality in smooth flow boxes whose longitudinal curves are unit-speed geodesics. Multiplying by the smooth volume Jacobian gives the one-dimensional distributional inequality $(u\circ\gamma)''\ge-C$ on almost every such curve. Continuity extends its integrated convexity inequality to every curve in a smaller box. This is local geodesic semiconvexity, which implies a local Lipschitz bound by bounding one-sided slopes on short geodesic segments with endpoints in a fixed larger ball. The differentiated equation for $X$ consequently has bounded right-hand side componentwise, so $\nabla^2X$ is locally bounded and $X\in C^{1,1}$. The measure inequalities now reduce to pointwise inequalities by continuity. ◻

</details>

<a id="var:electric-metric-adjoint"></a>
For clarity about the field coefficients in this adjoint, holding $\alpha=i_E\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g$ fixed gives $E'=-(\operatorname{tr}h)E/2$ and hence $$\delta\bigl(2u c_n^2|E|^2\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g\bigr)
   =u c_n^2\bigl(2E_iE_j-|E|^2g_{ij}\bigr)h^{ij}\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g
   =-u\mathsf S_{ij}h^{ij}\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g.
 \label{var:electric-metric-adjoint}$$ The ray transport contributes $-(J_m)_{(i}X_{j)}h^{ij}$. The added variation of volume in the full constraint pairing is zero by complementarity. These identities, together with the actual gravitational adjoint, will control null focusing in the next section. In particular, the singular term in <a href="#var:hessian-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:hessian-measure">[var:hessian-measure]</a> cannot be discarded by assuming regularity of a minimizing frontier.

<a id="var:singular-multipliers"></a>
### Eliminating interior area multipliers

We prove that the area multiplier obtained from separation is concentrated on the given boundary. The main issue is that an area minimizer can have a singular frontier in high dimension. Atomic transverse mass forces a regular sheet to be totally geodesic. For the remaining sheets, a common positive Jacobi field, null focusing, and a cone argument give a uniform curvature bound. A separate locality argument removes diffuse multiplier mass from sheets on which the lapse vanishes. All these arguments concern the original smooth data.

<a id="var:zero-sheet-expansions"></a>
Throughout this section, $k=n-1$, and $\mathcal F_*$ and $\pi$ are the compact family and probability measure of Proposition <a href="#var:area-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:area-envelope">42</a> and equation <a href="#var:separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:separation">[var:separation]</a>. We use the full-measure family supplied by Lemma <a href="#var:trace-free-sheets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:trace-free-sheets">44</a>, intersected with $\operatorname{supp}\pi$. A member of this family has frontier $S$, or has compact frontier wholly in the open exterior and satisfies $$\label{var:zero-sheet-expansions}
 H_\Sigma=0,\qquad \operatorname{tr}_\Sigma K=0$$ on every free regular sheet. We retain the local common-sheet, noncrossing, and graphical compactness conclusions of Proposition <a href="#var:area-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:area-envelope">42</a>. In particular, distinct nearby sheets are disjoint graphs, and on smaller patches the tilt between them is bounded by a constant times their separation.

<a id="var:singular-system"></a>
The moments of Proposition <a href="#var:moment-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:moment-regularity">45</a> satisfy $$\label{var:singular-system}
 \operatorname{sym}\nabla X=-uK,\qquad
 \nabla^2u=B(u,X,\nabla X)+\mathcal M,
 \qquad
 \mathcal M=\frac c2\int_{\mathcal F_*}
       \nu_D^\flat\otimes\nu_D^\flat\,dA_D\,d\pi(D)$$ in the open exterior. Here $c>0$, $u$ is locally Lipschitz, $X$ is locally $C^{1,1}$, and the last formula defines a tensor-valued measure relative to the smooth volume density. Its singular sets have zero sheet area. Here $B$ abbreviates the operator $\mathcal L$ of Proposition <a href="#var:moment-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:moment-regularity">45</a>. It is the precise lower-order metric-adjoint expression in equation <a href="#var:separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:separation">[var:separation]</a>, with the electric flux held fixed and the unit-ball rays transported isometrically. It has smooth coefficients and is linear in its displayed arguments. Its origin, and not merely its regularity, will be used in the focusing calculation. We also have <a id="var:singular-complementarity"></a>$$\label{var:singular-complementarity}
 u\geq |X|,\qquad \mu_m\geq |J_m|,\qquad
 u\mu_m+J_m(X)=0.$$

<a id="pm-h-55"></a>
#### Local leaf measures and atomic sheets

Fix a free regular incidence point $(D,p)$. The uniform graph conclusion of Proposition <a href="#var:area-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:area-envelope">42</a>, after shrinking its neighborhood, provides nested open spatial boxes $$p\in W\Subset V\Subset U,
 \qquad W=B_0\times I_0,$$ in coordinates $(y,t)$, and a larger base ball $B_1\supset\overline{B_0}$, with the following property. Every minimizing frontier that meets $V$ has in $U$ a unique selected graph $t=f_F(y)$ over $B_1$; all portions of that frontier in $W$ belong to this graph. Its graph over $B_1$ lies in the coordinate chart, and the graphs have uniform interior smooth bounds and are mutually disjoint unless they coincide. The boxes can be chosen with this property by first taking the uniform graph neighborhood and then choosing the middle and inner boxes with positive margin from its side and top faces.

Put $$\mathcal H_V=\{F\in\mathcal F_*:
                         \operatorname{frontier}(F)\cap V\ne\varnothing\}.$$ This is an open subset of the minimizing family. Indeed convergence in that compact family is strict perimeter convergence, hence its perimeter measures converge weakly. If the limit frontier meets the open set $V$, its perimeter measure is positive on a nonnegative compactly supported test in $V$, and the same holds for every sufficiently late member of the sequence. This proves openness. The uniform density bounds also give local Hausdorff convergence of the frontier supports: a putative boundary point staying a positive distance from the limit frontier would force positive volumes of both phases in a ball where the limit is constant, contradicting local $L^1$ convergence.

The graph map $$q_V:\mathcal H_V\longrightarrow C^\infty(B_1),\qquad q_V(F)=f_F,$$ is continuous for the topology of smooth convergence on compact subsets. To see this, apply regular multiplicity-one convergence at the limiting graph on each compact subball; the uniform graph bounds and uniqueness identify every subsequential limit. Define the finite Borel measure $$\lambda_V=(q_V)_*(\pi|_{\mathcal H_V})$$ on the entire separable metrizable function space $C^\infty(B_1)$. This definition identifies coincident local graphs without requiring the image of $q_V$ to be a Borel subset of that space. We write $\mathcal G$ for this leaf-parameter space and $\lambda$ for $\lambda_V$ when the box is fixed.

<a id="var:injective-leaf-evaluation"></a>
Every source contribution on $W$ is retained: a frontier meeting $W$ also meets $V$, and is therefore in $\mathcal H_V$. No arbitrary neighborhood of the reference minimizer is used to truncate this measure. Distinct realized graph parameters are disjoint, so for each $y\in B_1$ the map $$\label{var:injective-leaf-evaluation}
 \ell\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}\OriginalLongmapsto\EndAccSupp{}f_\ell(y)$$ is injective on the realized leaves. All integrals with respect to $\lambda$ mean the corresponding pushforward integrals over $\mathcal H_V$; the injectivity assertion concerns the realized graphs in those integrals.

<a id="stmt-var-atomic-leaves"></a>
<div id="var:atomic-leaves" class="lemma">

**Lemma 46** (Atomic leaves). *If $\lambda(\{\ell_0\})>0$, the corresponding regular sheet is totally geodesic on $\operatorname{graph}(f_{\ell_0})\cap W$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-atomic-leaves"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-atomic-leaves</summary>

<a id="var:atomic-normal-jump"></a>
*Proof.* Fix any point of $\operatorname{graph}(f_{\ell_0})\cap W$ and take signed normal-distance coordinates $(y,s)$ about that sheet on a smaller patch compactly contained in $W$, writing the sheet as $\Sigma=\{s=0\}$. Put $m_0=\lambda(\{\ell_0\})>0$. The $ss$ equation in <a href="#var:singular-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:singular-system">[var:singular-system]</a> is a one-dimensional second-derivative equation after slicing by almost every normal line. The bounded nonmeasure terms include $B$ and the coordinate connection terms. The atom at $s=0$ has coefficient $cm_0/2$: on the reference graph the unit conormal is $ds$ and the sheet and volume Jacobians agree at $s=0$. No other graph produces an atom there, by <a href="#var:injective-leaf-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:injective-leaf-evaluation">[var:injective-leaf-evaluation]</a>. Consequently $\partial_su$ has bounded one-sided traces $v^\pm(y)$ for almost every $y$, and $$\label{var:atomic-normal-jump}
 v^+(y)-v^-(y)=\frac c2m_0.$$ The existence and boundedness of these traces can also be read directly from local semiconvexity of $u$ along the normal lines.

<a id="var:tangential-hessian-splitting"></a>
We compare the tangential equations from the two sides. Let $A_s$ be the second fundamental form of the parallel sheet $\{s=\mathrm{constant}\}$, with normal $\partial_s$. With $A_s(V,W)=g(\nabla_V\partial_s,W)$, $$\label{var:tangential-hessian-splitting}
 (\nabla^2u)|_{T\Sigma_s}
   =\nabla^2_{\Sigma_s}(u|_{\Sigma_s})+A_s\partial_su.$$ Average this identity on each of $0<s<\varepsilon$ and $-\varepsilon<s<0$, pairing with any smooth compactly supported tangential tensor in the $y$ variables. The intrinsic Hessian terms have the same limit: integrate twice in $y$, then use uniform convergence of $u(y,s)$ to $u(y,0)$ and smooth convergence of the parallel metrics. The $B$ terms also have the same limit, since $u,X,\nabla X$ are continuous.

The averaged tangential components of the sheet measure tend to zero. Indeed the conormal of a nearby graph has tangential component bounded by $C|s|$ relative to $\Sigma$ at a point of height $s$. This is the graph-difference estimate recalled above, with the smooth change from graph coordinates to normal coordinates included. Thus in a slab of width $\varepsilon$ the tangential-tangential source is bounded in total variation by $C\varepsilon^2$ times the total sheet measure in a fixed box. Dividing by $\varepsilon$ still gives a quantity tending to zero. The atom on $\Sigma$ itself has no tangential component.

Subtracting the two limits in <a href="#var:tangential-hessian-splitting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:tangential-hessian-splitting">[var:tangential-hessian-splitting]</a> gives $A_0(v^+-v^-)=0$ as a tensor distribution on $\Sigma$. Equation <a href="#var:atomic-normal-jump" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:atomic-normal-jump">[var:atomic-normal-jump]</a> and $c m_0>0$ imply $A_0=0$. The sheet is smooth, so the conclusion holds at every point of the patch. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-var-common-jacobi"></a>
<div id="var:common-jacobi" class="lemma">

<a id="var:three-jacobi-equations"></a>
<a id="var:minimal-jacobi"></a>
**Lemma 47** (A common Jacobi field). *Let $D$ belong to the full-measure family above, and let $\Sigma$ be a connected component of its regular interior frontier. Suppose one of its graph patches is a nonatom in the support of the corresponding leaf measure. Then there is a smooth function $\varphi>0$ on $\Sigma$ such that $$\label{var:three-jacobi-equations}
 L_0\varphi=L_+\varphi=L_-\varphi=0.$$ Here $L_0$, $L_+$, and $L_-$ are the outward normal linearizations of $H$, $H+\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{tan}}K$, and $H-\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{tan}}K$, respectively, at $\Sigma$. All have principal part $-\Delta_\Sigma$, and $$\label{var:minimal-jacobi}
 L_0=-\Delta_\Sigma-
       \bigl(|A_\Sigma|^2+\operatorname{Ric}_g(\nu,\nu)\bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-common-jacobi"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-common-jacobi</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a base point $p$ in the specified patch. Since its graph parameter is a nonatom in the support of the leaf measure, choose distinct realized graphs converging smoothly on compact subpatches to the reference graph, with representatives $D_j$ in the full-measure family. Such representatives exist because every graph neighborhood has positive pushforward measure, whereas the reference fiber has measure zero. Compactness of $\mathcal F_*$ permits passing to a subsequence with $D_j\to D'$ for some minimizing filled set $D'$. We do not assert that $D'=D$ away from the selected component.

The limiting frontier of $D'$ contains the reference graph near $p$. Indeed points on the converging graphs are frontier points of $D_j$, and uniform density and local $L^1$ convergence put their limits on the frontier of $D'$. Set $$C=\Sigma\cap\operatorname{frontier}(D').$$ This set is nonempty and relatively closed in $\Sigma$. It is also relatively open: at each of its points the reference frontier is free and regular, and the common-sheet property of Proposition <a href="#var:area-envelope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:area-envelope">42</a> makes the frontier of $D'$ agree with it in a neighborhood. Connectedness gives $C=\Sigma$. In particular every point of $\Sigma$ is a regular point of the limiting frontier $D'$.

Choose a nested exhaustion of $\Sigma$ by connected precompact smooth domains $\Omega_m$ containing $p$, with $\overline{\Omega_m}\subset\Omega_{m+1}$; if $\Sigma$ is compact, use $\Sigma$ itself. Use the fixed normal of $\Sigma$ and its normal exponential map on a tubular neighborhood of each compact stage. Regular multiplicity-one convergence to $D'$ gives, for sufficiently large $j$ at that stage, a unique normal height $h_j$ over $\Omega_m$. A finite covering of the slightly larger compact stage by regular graph patches supplies this assertion uniformly on $\overline{\Omega_m}$. Uniqueness on each normal fiber makes the heights agree on overlaps and on successive exhaustion stages. Thus there is no choice of branch or monodromy in this construction.

The heights have a strict constant sign on each connected stage. If $h_j$ vanished at a point, the common-sheet property would make its zero set relatively open as well as closed there; connectedness would force coincidence at $p$, contrary to the distinct initial graphs. Passing to a subsequence fixes the sign at $p$, and hence on every exhaustion stage. Replace $h_j$ by $-h_j$ in the linear difference equations if that sign is negative, and normalize by $h_j(p)>0$. The equations $H=0$ and $\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{tan}}K=0$ are unaffected by choosing the fixed graph normal, even if a representative filled set induces the opposite orientation on its local graph. Subtract the three graph equations <a href="#var:zero-sheet-expansions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:zero-sheet-expansions">[var:zero-sheet-expansions]</a>. The integral of the linearization along the segment between the two graphs gives linear elliptic equations for $h_j$, whose coefficients converge smoothly on compact subsets to those of $L_0,L_+,L_-$. Harnack’s inequality and interior elliptic estimates applied to the first equation bound $h_j/h_j(p)$ above and below, with all derivatives, on each exhaustion stage. A diagonal subsequence converges smoothly to $\varphi$, with $\varphi(p)=1$. Harnack’s inequality gives positivity. Passing to the limit in all three equations proves <a href="#var:three-jacobi-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:three-jacobi-equations">[var:three-jacobi-equations]</a>. The same argument applies when the approach is from the other side, since all three linearized equations are homogeneous. ◻

</details>

The support qualification in this lemma loses no multiplier mass. On each graph box the complement of the support of $\lambda$ has zero $\lambda$-measure. A countable collection of such boxes suffices for the regular incidence set, as explained below.

<a id="pm-h-56"></a>
#### Focusing with a measure-valued Hessian

<a id="stmt-var-weak-focusing"></a>
<div id="var:weak-focusing" class="lemma">

<a id="var:Q-definitions"></a>
<a id="var:weak-focusing-inequalities"></a>
**Lemma 48** (Weak focusing). *On a free regular sheet satisfying <a href="#var:zero-sheet-expansions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:zero-sheet-expansions">[var:zero-sheet-expansions]</a>, define $$\label{var:Q-definitions}
 Q_+=u-g(X,\nu),\qquad Q_-=u+g(X,\nu).$$ These functions are nonnegative and locally Lipschitz. On the open part of the sheet where $u>0$, they satisfy the distributional inequalities $$\label{var:weak-focusing-inequalities}
 L_\pm Q_\pm\leq -u|A_\Sigma\pm K_{\mathrm{tan}}|^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-weak-focusing"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-weak-focusing</summary>

<a id="var:local-stationary-metric"></a>
*Proof.* We first identify the sign of the singular term in the stationary curvature calculation. For smooth positive $u$ and smooth $X$, set $$\label{var:local-stationary-metric}
 \mathbf g=-u^2 dz^2+
       g_{ij}(dx^i+X^i dz)(dx^j+X^j dz),\qquad
 \mathbf n=u^{-1}(\partial_z-X).$$ The second fundamental form of a stationary slice is $b=-\operatorname{sym}\nabla X/u$, with the convention for $K$ used in the theorem. Define $\mathsf B(P,Q)=P:Q-(\operatorname{tr}P)(\operatorname{tr}Q)$. Integrating the momentum term in the gravitational constraint pairing at fixed lapse and shift gives the algebraic expression <a id="var:stationary-action-identity"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
\label{var:stationary-action-identity}
