 u\bigl(R_g-\mathsf B(K,K)+2\mathsf B(K,b)\bigr)
 &=u\bigl(R_g+\mathsf B(b,b)
                    -\mathsf B(K-b,K-b)\bigr).
\end{aligned}$$ At $b=K$ its first metric variation agrees with that of the stationary scalar-curvature action, up to divergences. The spatial metric coefficient of that variation is $-u\mathbf G_{ij}$, where $\mathbf G$ is the Einstein tensor of <a href="#var:local-stationary-metric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:local-stationary-metric">[var:local-stationary-metric]</a>. This identity can equally be obtained by substituting $b=-\operatorname{sym}\nabla X/u$ and integrating the derivatives of a compactly supported metric variation; it uses no field equation.

<a id="var:stress-trace-reversal"></a>
Let $\mathsf S$ be the Maxwell stress of a two-form whose slice electric field is $c_nE$ and whose slice magnetic part is zero. Only these slice values are needed here. Holding the electric flux form fixed under $g'=h$ gives <a id="var:fixed-flux-stress"></a>$$\label{var:fixed-flux-stress}
 \delta\bigl(2u c_n^2|E|^2\,dV_g\bigr)
 =u c_n^2(2E_iE_j-|E|^2g_{ij})h^{ij}\,dV_g
 =-u\mathsf S_{ij}h^{ij}\,dV_g.$$ Adding the variation of the volume density in the full matter pairing does not alter the adjoint coefficient, by $u\mu_m+J_m(X)=0$. Transport of a unit-ball ray contributes $-(J_m)_{(i}X_{j)}$, where parentheses include the factor $1/2$. It follows from the actual metric-adjoint expression in <a href="#var:singular-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:singular-system">[var:singular-system]</a> that a Hessian source $M$ contributes $$\label{var:stress-trace-reversal}
 u(\mathbf G-\mathsf S)_{ij}
 =-(J_m)_{(i}X_{j)}+
       \bigl((\operatorname{tr}_g M)g-M\bigr)_{ij}.$$ For a positive semidefinite $M$, the last tensor is positive semidefinite: for a unit vector $v$, complete $v$ to an orthonormal basis and sum $M$ on the remaining basis vectors. In particular the sign is preserved by trace reversal in precisely this direction.

<a id="var:explicit-spatial-einstein"></a>
<a id="var:explicit-lower-hessian"></a>
The lower Hessian term can be read off explicitly. Write $\tau_b=\operatorname{tr}_g b$ and $(b^2)_{ij}=b_i{}^l b_{lj}$. The spatial stationary Einstein identity is $$\begin{aligned}
 u\mathbf G_{ij}
   &=(\Delta_g u)g_{ij}-(\nabla_g^2u)_{ij}
                      +(\mathcal C_b)_{ij},
 \label{var:explicit-spatial-einstein}\\
 \mathcal C_b
   &=u\left[\operatorname{Ric}_g+\tau_b b-2b^2
       -\tfrac12(R_g+\tau_b^2+|b|^2)g\right]
       -\mathcal L_Xb+X(\tau_b)g.
 \notag
\end{aligned}$$ At $b=K$, put $T=u\mathsf S-\operatorname{sym}(J_m\otimes X^\flat)
       -\mathcal C_K$. Equation <a href="#var:stress-trace-reversal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:stress-trace-reversal">[var:stress-trace-reversal]</a> then gives $$B(u,X,\nabla X)=\frac{\operatorname{tr}_gT}{n-1}g-T.
 \label{var:explicit-lower-hessian}$$ Thus $B$ has no $du$ term. All its coefficients are smooth fixed-data coefficients multiplying $u,X,\nabla X$. For the mollified pair, the same identity with $b=b_\epsilon$ shows that $C^1$ convergence of $b_\epsilon$ controls every non-Hessian spatial term.

<a id="var:b-mollifier-convergence"></a>
We justify the use of this calculation at the regularity of <a href="#var:singular-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:singular-system">[var:singular-system]</a>. Fix relatively compact coordinate patches $U'\Subset U\Subset\{u>0\}$, and choose $a>0$ with $u\geq a$ on $U$. All smoothing below is performed in $U$, then restricted to $U'$. Let $\rho_\epsilon$ be a nonnegative standard mollifier, and convolve the coordinate components of $u$ and $\beta=X^\flat$: $u_\epsilon=u*\rho_\epsilon$, $\beta_\epsilon=\beta*\rho_\epsilon$, $X_\epsilon=g^{-1}\beta_\epsilon$. The spatial metric is not changed. In components the first equation of <a href="#var:singular-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:singular-system">[var:singular-system]</a> reads $\partial_{(i}\beta_{j)}=\Gamma_{ij}^{l}\beta_l-uK_{ij}$. Hence the residual <a id="var:KID-mollifier-residual"></a>$$\label{var:KID-mollifier-residual}
 R_\epsilon:=\operatorname{sym}\nabla\beta_\epsilon+u_\epsilon K
 = (\Gamma\beta)*\rho_\epsilon-\Gamma\beta_\epsilon
       -(uK)*\rho_\epsilon+u_\epsilon K$$ tends to zero in $C^1(U')$. More explicitly, for a smooth coefficient $A$ and a Lipschitz function $f$, <a id="var:C1-commutator"></a>$$\label{var:C1-commutator}
 \|(Af)*\rho_\epsilon-A(f*\rho_\epsilon)\|_{C^1(U')}
 \leq C\epsilon\bigl(
       \|A\|_{C^2(U)}\|f\|_{L^\infty(U)}
       +\|A\|_{C^1(U)}\|Df\|_{L^\infty(U)}\bigr).$$ For its derivative, differentiate the product to obtain the sum of the commutator of $DA$ with $f$ and the commutator of $A$ with $Df$. Both are bounded by the first moment of $\rho_\epsilon$ times the displayed norms. No second derivative of $f$ is used. Since $u_\epsilon\geq a/2$ and $Du_\epsilon$ is uniformly bounded, $$\label{var:b-mollifier-convergence}
 b_\epsilon:=-\operatorname{sym}\nabla X_\epsilon/u_\epsilon
       =K-R_\epsilon/u_\epsilon\longrightarrow K
       \quad\hbox{in }C^1(U').$$

In the Hessian equation, first express the measure-valued components relative to coordinate Lebesgue measure, dividing by the smooth positive volume density. Positivity is unchanged. Convolve this already un-trace-reversed equation. It gives <a id="var:Hessian-mollifier"></a>$$\label{var:Hessian-mollifier}
 \nabla_g^2u_\epsilon
 =B_\epsilon+\mathcal M_\epsilon+
        (\Gamma\,du)*\rho_\epsilon-\Gamma\,du_\epsilon,
 \qquad B_\epsilon\longrightarrow B\quad\hbox{uniformly on }U'.$$ The connection commutator is $O(\epsilon)$ in $C^0$ because $du$ is bounded. For every $x$, $\mathcal M_\epsilon(x)$ is a positive semidefinite matrix. We trace this matrix using $g(x)$ only after convolution. Consequently <a id="var:exact-PSD-reversal"></a>$$\label{var:exact-PSD-reversal}
 P_\epsilon(x):=
       (\operatorname{tr}_{g(x)}\mathcal M_\epsilon(x))g(x)
                        -\mathcal M_\epsilon(x)\geq0$$ exactly, even if its norm diverges as $\epsilon\downarrow0$. There is no product commutator involving a variable metric times an unbounded singular Hessian.

<a id="var:mollified-null-Ricci"></a>
Apply the smooth stationary calculation to $u_\epsilon,X_\epsilon$ and $b_\epsilon$. Formula <a href="#var:b-mollifier-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:b-mollifier-convergence">[var:b-mollifier-convergence]</a> controls the induced constraint densities and all the non-Hessian spatial adjoint terms uniformly. Indeed <a href="#var:explicit-spatial-einstein" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:explicit-spatial-einstein">[var:explicit-spatial-einstein]</a> uses only $b_\epsilon,\nabla b_\epsilon,u_\epsilon,X_\epsilon,
\nabla X_\epsilon$ outside the Hessian term. Equation <a href="#var:explicit-lower-hessian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:explicit-lower-hessian">[var:explicit-lower-hessian]</a> shows that uniform convergence of $du_\epsilon$ is unnecessary. Writing $\mathbf T_\epsilon=\mathbf G_\epsilon-\mathsf S_\epsilon$, we obtain uniformly on $U'$ <a id="var:smoothed-stress-blocks"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
\label{var:smoothed-stress-blocks}
 \mathbf T_\epsilon(\mathbf n_\epsilon,\mathbf n_\epsilon)
     &=\mu_m+o(1),&
 \mathbf T_\epsilon(\mathbf n_\epsilon,\cdot)|_{TU'}
     &=J_m+o(1),\\
 u_\epsilon(\mathbf T_\epsilon)_{ij}
     &=-(J_m)_{(i}X_{j)}+(P_\epsilon)_{ij}+o(1).&&
 \notag
\end{aligned}$$ To see the null sign of the bounded terms, when $\mu_m>0$, <a href="#var:moment-complementarity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:moment-complementarity">[var:moment-complementarity]</a> forces $|X|=u$ and $J_m=-\mu_m X^\flat/u$. Their limiting block tensor is $\mu_m\ell\otimes\ell$, with $\ell(\mathbf n)=1$ and $\ell(V)=-g(X,V)/u$. When $\mu_m=0$, all its blocks vanish. This description, or the direct formula $u\mu_m(1\mp g(X,\nu)/u)^2$, proves nonnegativity on $\mathbf n\pm\nu$. Maxwell stress is also nonnegative on null vectors. The exact positivity of $P_\epsilon$ therefore gives $$\label{var:mollified-null-Ricci}
 u_\epsilon\operatorname{Ric}_{\mathbf g_\epsilon}
       (\mathbf n_\epsilon\pm\nu,
        \mathbf n_\epsilon\pm\nu)\geq-o(1).$$ This is a one-sided estimate; it requires no uniform bound on the full spacetime curvature.

<a id="var:smoothed-focusing-formula"></a>
For completeness, apply the null shape-operator equation to the outgoing null geodesics orthogonal to the fixed sheet in the smooth metric $\mathbf g_\epsilon$. Use nearby stationary slices as the parameter surfaces. At the initial slice the null velocity with $dz/d\tau=1$ is $u_\epsilon(\mathbf n_\epsilon+\nu)$. Subtracting the stationary translation $\partial_z$ leaves the spatial velocity $u_\epsilon\nu-X_\epsilon$, whose normal component is $u_\epsilon-g(X_\epsilon,\nu)$. The Riccati equation gives the expansion derivative $$\label{var:smoothed-focusing-formula}
 -u_\epsilon\bigl(
     |A_\Sigma+(b_\epsilon)_{\mathrm{tan}}|^2+
     \operatorname{Ric}_{\mathbf g_\epsilon}
        (\mathbf n_\epsilon+\nu,\mathbf n_\epsilon+\nu)\bigr),$$ up to a bounded renormalization factor times the initial expansion. Tangential transport contributes a bounded tangential velocity times the tangential derivative of that expansion. Both error terms tend to zero uniformly: $H+\operatorname{tr}_\Sigma K=0$ and $b_\epsilon\to K$ in $C^1$. The spatial expansion linearization has coefficients converging uniformly to those of $L_+$. Its principal part is always the fixed $-\Delta_\Sigma$; convergence of its action on $u_\epsilon-g(X_\epsilon,\nu)$ therefore holds in distributions, using uniform convergence of these functions and uniform bounds on their first derivatives. Combining <a href="#var:mollified-null-Ricci" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:mollified-null-Ricci">[var:mollified-null-Ricci]</a>–<a href="#var:smoothed-focusing-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:smoothed-focusing-formula">[var:smoothed-focusing-formula]</a> proves the plus inequality in <a href="#var:weak-focusing-inequalities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:weak-focusing-inequalities">[var:weak-focusing-inequalities]</a>. Replace the future normal by $-\mathbf n_\epsilon$, equivalently reverse stationary time and $X_\epsilon,b_\epsilon$, to obtain the minus inequality. Since $U'\Subset\{u>0\}$ was arbitrary, this proves the stated local result. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-57"></a>
#### Positive Jacobi fields at singular ends

We give the analytic details needed at singular points. Their purpose is stronger than removability of a bounded subsolution: a bounded reciprocal Jacobi field must tend to zero at every singular end.

<a id="stmt-var-capacity-submean"></a>
<div id="var:capacity-submean" class="lemma">

<a id="var:surface-submean"></a>
**Lemma 49** (Capacity and a local mean-value estimate). *Let $T$ be a multiplicity-one locally perimeter-minimizing hypersurface in a compact smooth ambient patch, of dimension $k\geq3$. Its singular set has zero surface $2$-capacity. A bounded nonnegative continuous function $w$ on the regular part, satisfying $\Delta_T w\geq-C_0$ there, extends weakly across the singular set and satisfies, for sufficiently small ambient balls centered on $\operatorname{supp}T$, $$\label{var:surface-submean}
 \sup_{T\cap B_{r/2}}w
 \leq C\left[
       \left(r^{-k}\int_{T\cap B_r}w^2\,dA\right)^{1/2}
          +C_0r^2\right].$$ The supremum is over regular points. Constants are uniform in a fixed compact patch, and under its sufficiently small rescalings. The assertion also holds for a function defined on one regular component and extended by zero on the other components.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-capacity-submean"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-capacity-submean</summary>

<a id="var:capacity-cutoffs"></a>
*Proof.* The singular dimension bound is $k-7$, and local perimeter bounds give $\mathop{\mathrm{Area}}(T\cap B_\rho)\leq C\rho^k$; see (Simon 2014) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[SimonGMT2014](#ref-SimonGMT2014)]. If the singular set is nonempty, cover a compact part of it by balls $B_{r_i}$ with $r_i$ arbitrarily small and $\sum_i r_i^{k-2}$ arbitrarily small. Choose cutoffs equal to one on $B_{r_i}$, supported on $B_{2r_i}$, with gradient bounded by $C/r_i$. The maximum of these cutoffs has Dirichlet energy at most $C\sum_i r_i^{k-2}$ and support of area tending to zero. Truncation and smooth approximation produce cutoffs $\chi_j$, vanishing near the singular set and tending to one almost everywhere, with $$\label{var:capacity-cutoffs}
 \int|\nabla_T\chi_j|^2\,dA\longrightarrow0.$$ The same construction applies on compact cone annuli and their spherical links, where the singular link dimension is at most $k-8$.

On the regular part, test the subsolution inequality by $\eta^2\chi_j^2w$, using smooth approximations or positive truncations if needed. Integration by parts and Young’s inequality bound the Dirichlet integral of $w$ on smaller patches by <a id="var:subsolution-Caccioppoli"></a>$$\label{var:subsolution-Caccioppoli}
 \int\eta^2\chi_j^2|\nabla_Tw|^2
 \leq C\|w\|_\infty^2
       \int\bigl(|\nabla_T\eta|^2+
                     \eta^2|\nabla_T\chi_j|^2\bigr)
       +C C_0\|w\|_\infty\int\eta^2.$$ Thus $w$ has locally square-integrable weak gradient across the singular set. In a bounded compact test, the extra terms containing $\nabla\chi_j$ tend to zero by Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#var:capacity-cutoffs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:capacity-cutoffs">[var:capacity-cutoffs]</a>. This proves the weak extension. The same proof on any union of regular components is valid, since its boundary within the support lies in the singular set.

The Michael–Simon surface Sobolev inequality (Michael and Simon 1973) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[MichaelSimon1973](#ref-MichaelSimon1973)], applied in coordinate or isometrically embedded compact patches, gives for compactly supported $v$ <a id="var:surface-Sobolev"></a>$$\label{var:surface-Sobolev}
 \left(\int|v|^{2k/(k-2)}\,dA\right)^{(k-2)/k}
 \leq C\int\bigl(|\nabla_Tv|^2+v^2\bigr)\,dA.$$ The ambient embedding contributes a uniformly bounded mean-curvature term. The inequality extends to the above weak functions by the same capacity cutoffs. After scaling a ball to radius one, apply the energy estimate to successive positive powers of $w+C_0r^2$ and nested radial cutoffs. The Sobolev exponent multiplies each power by $k/(k-2)$; the resulting geometric iteration gives the $L^2$-to-$L^\infty$ estimate <a href="#var:surface-submean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:surface-submean">[var:surface-submean]</a>. All constants depend only on the ambient patch, dimension, and perimeter bound. Their scaled versions remain bounded as $r\downarrow0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-var-cone-subsolution"></a>
<div id="var:cone-subsolution" class="lemma">

**Lemma 50** (A bounded cone subsolution vanishes). *Let $C\subset\mathbb R^{k+1}$ be a nonflat, multiplicity-one, perimeter-minimizing boundary cone. A bounded nonnegative continuous function $z$ on its entire regular part satisfying <a id="var:cone-subsolution-equation"></a>$$\label{var:cone-subsolution-equation}
 \Delta_C z\geq |A_C|^2z$$ in distributions is identically zero. The function may be zero on some regular components.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-cone-subsolution"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-cone-subsolution</summary>

<a id="var:log-radius-inequality"></a>
*Proof.* Only $k\geq7$ can occur. Let $L=C\cap\mathbb S^k$ be the link, let $s=\log r$, and put $V=|A_L|^2$. The inequality becomes $$\label{var:log-radius-inequality}
 z_{ss}+(k-2)z_s+\Delta_Lz\geq Vz.$$ We first justify testing across the singular link. On each compact axial slab, the Caccioppoli test $\eta^2\chi_j^2z$ gives local $W^{1,2}$ bounds and a bound for $\int V\eta^2\chi_j^2 z^2$; the axial first-order term is bounded by the same energy inequality. Passing through the capacity cutoffs of Lemma <a href="#var:capacity-submean" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:capacity-submean">49</a> proves the weak inequality across the singular set. Testing with a nonnegative function equal to one on a smaller slab also bounds $\int Vz$ there. Positivity allows Fatou’s lemma for this term. The terms involving $\nabla\chi_j$ tend to zero by the energy bound and <a href="#var:capacity-cutoffs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:capacity-cutoffs">[var:capacity-cutoffs]</a>. These observations justify integration of <a href="#var:log-radius-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:log-radius-inequality">[var:log-radius-inequality]</a> over the whole link against any nonnegative compactly supported axial test.

In particular the bounded nonnegative function $q(s)=\int_Lz(s,\theta)\,dA_L(\theta)$ satisfies <a id="var:log-radius-monotonicity"></a>$$\label{var:log-radius-monotonicity}
 q''+(k-2)q'\geq0$$ on the whole real line. Convolve in $s$. Each smooth bounded convolution satisfies the same inequality, so $e^{(k-2)s}q'(s)$ is nondecreasing for that convolution. If $q'(s_0)<0$, then for $s<s_0$ $q'(s)\leq q'(s_0)e^{(k-2)(s_0-s)}$. Integrating backward contradicts boundedness. Thus every convolution is nondecreasing, and the original $q$ has a nondecreasing representative. If $z$ is nonzero, its forward limit $q_\infty$ is strictly positive.

<a id="var:averaged-link-inequality"></a>
Choose $w\in C_c^\infty((1,2))$, $w\geq0$, with integral one, and put $w_T(s)=T^{-1}w(s/T)$. The functions <a id="var:forward-average"></a>$$\label{var:forward-average}
 \overline z_T(\theta)=\int_{\mathbb R}w_T(s)z(s,\theta)\,ds$$ are bounded uniformly, and their integrals tend to $q_\infty$. Take a weak-star convergent subsequence in $L^\infty(L)$ with limit $\overline z\geq0$. For a compactly supported nonnegative regular link test $\psi$, integration by parts in $s$ bounds the axial terms by $\|z\|_\infty\|\psi\|_{L^1(L)}(\|w_T''\|_1+(k-2)\|w_T'\|_1)$, which tends to zero. Therefore $$\label{var:averaged-link-inequality}
 \Delta_L\overline z\geq V\overline z,
 \qquad \int_L\overline z\,dA_L=q_\infty>0.$$ Bounded subsolution regularity and the capacity argument give the needed local weak derivatives. Test this inequality by $\chi_j^2\overline z$ and use Young’s inequality to obtain <a id="var:link-energy-vanishing"></a>$$\label{var:link-energy-vanishing}
 \frac12\int_L\chi_j^2|\nabla_L\overline z|^2
       +\int_LV\chi_j^2\overline z^2
 \leq2\|\overline z\|_\infty^2
                  \int_L|\nabla_L\chi_j|^2\longrightarrow0.$$ Thus $\overline z$ is constant on every connected regular component of $L$, and a positive constant can occur only on a totally geodesic component.

We show that a nonflat minimizing boundary cone has no such component. A connected totally geodesic regular component lies in an equator $Q\cong\mathbb S^{k-1}$. It is relatively open there; its relative boundary lies in the singular set, since at a regular boundary point the same smooth sheet continues. A closed set of Hausdorff dimension less than $(k-1)-1$ does not disconnect $Q$: in coordinate balls one connects points by polygonal paths whose vertices are chosen outside the measure-zero exceptional sets of segments meeting that set, and then concatenates such paths. Here the singular set has dimension at most $k-8$. It follows that this component contains $Q$ minus the singular set, and its closure contains the entire equator.

Any other regular component lies strictly in one open hemisphere determined by $Q$. It cannot meet $Q$ at a regular point without being part of the same regular sheet. On that component the signed coordinate height $h$ is strictly of one sign and satisfies $\Delta_Lh=-(k-1)h$. It and its gradient are bounded. Integrate against capacity cutoffs, extended by zero on other components. The boundary terms tend to zero by Cauchy–Schwarz; hence $\int h=0$, a contradiction. There are no other regular components. Regular points are dense in the support, and multiplicity one now says that $C$ is the plane through $Q$. The componentwise interpretation is also consistent with the decomposition of a multiplicity-one oriented minimizing boundary into minimizing regular components; see (Simon 2014, chap. 7, Section 5, Corollary 5.11 and Remark 5.12) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[SimonGMT2014](#ref-SimonGMT2014)]. This contradicts nonflatness. Therefore <a href="#var:averaged-link-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:averaged-link-inequality">[var:averaged-link-inequality]</a> is impossible and $z=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-var-reciprocal-jacobi"></a>
<div id="var:reciprocal-jacobi" class="lemma">

<a id="var:Jacobi-singular-behavior"></a>
**Lemma 51** (Reciprocal Jacobi decay). *Let $\Sigma$ be a connected regular component of a compact interior minimizing frontier, and let $\varphi>0$ solve <a href="#var:minimal-jacobi" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:minimal-jacobi">[var:minimal-jacobi]</a>. Then $$\label{var:Jacobi-singular-behavior}
 \inf_\Sigma\varphi>0,
 \qquad
 \varphi(x)\longrightarrow+\infty
 \quad\hbox{as }x\in\Sigma\hbox{ approaches the singular set}.$$ The second assertion is vacuous for a smooth compact component.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-reciprocal-jacobi"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-reciprocal-jacobi</summary>

*Proof.* The Ricci curvature is bounded on a compact neighborhood of the frontier; choose $C$ with $\operatorname{Ric}_g(\nu,\nu)\geq-C$. For $\delta>0$ put $w_\delta=(1-\varphi/\delta)_+$ on $\Sigma$, and extend it by zero on other regular components. Convex truncation of the Jacobi equation gives $\Delta w_\delta\geq-C$; on $\{\varphi<\delta\}$ this follows from $-\Delta\varphi/\delta=(|A|^2+\operatorname{Ric}(\nu,\nu))
\varphi/\delta\geq-C$, and the truncation contributes a nonnegative measure. Moreover $0\leq w_\delta\leq1$ and $w_\delta\to0$ almost everywhere as $\delta\downarrow0$.

Choose first a fixed radius $r>0$ so small that the $Cr^2$ term in <a href="#var:surface-submean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:surface-submean">[var:surface-submean]</a>, including its constant, is less than $1/4$. Cover the compact frontier by finitely many balls of radius $r/2$. Dominated convergence in their radius-$r$ enlargements then permits choosing $\delta>0$ so that the integral term is less than $1/4$ in every ball. Thus $w_\delta\leq1/2$ on $\Sigma$, and $\varphi\geq\delta/2$. This proves the first assertion with the required order of choices, $r$ before $\delta$.

<a id="var:reciprocal-inequality"></a>
Set $z=1/\varphi$ and extend it by zero to the other regular components. It is bounded and satisfies $$\label{var:reciprocal-inequality}
 \Delta_\Sigma z
   =\bigl(|A|^2+\operatorname{Ric}_g(\nu,\nu)\bigr)z
                  +2\varphi^{-3}|\nabla_\Sigma\varphi|^2
   \geq(|A|^2-C)z.$$ Fix a singular point $p$ and any sequence of scales $r_j\downarrow0$. Pass to a subsequence along which the rescaled frontiers converge to a multiplicity-one minimizing tangent cone $C_p$. The cone is nonflat: a multiplicity-one planar tangent cone would imply regularity of the frontier at $p$. These are the standard compactness and excess-regularity conclusions for minimizing boundaries (Simon 2014) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[SimonGMT2014](#ref-SimonGMT2014)].

On compact subsets of the regular cone the convergence is smooth. The rescaled $z$ have subsequences converging locally uniformly to a bounded nonnegative function $z_0$. Here is a useful precise way to obtain this convergence. On a connected regular patch, either $z$ tends to zero at one base point, in which case Harnack for the positive Jacobi function $\varphi$ forces $z\to0$ on smaller patches, or $z$ stays bounded below there along a subsequence. In the latter case the same Harnack inequality and interior estimates bound $\varphi$ and its derivatives, giving smooth convergence of $z$. The uniform upper bound for $z$ was already established. Branches on which the zero extension is used cause no difficulty; choose the branch in each regular patch and diagonalize. Thus one obtains $z_0$ on the whole regular cone, allowing zero components. Scaling <a href="#var:reciprocal-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:reciprocal-inequality">[var:reciprocal-inequality]</a> and passing to regular patches gives $\Delta_{C_p}z_0\geq|A_{C_p}|^2z_0$. Lemma <a href="#var:cone-subsolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:cone-subsolution">50</a> implies $z_0=0$.

This regular convergence implies vanishing of the normalized $L^2$ integral of $z$ on each fixed bounded rescaled ball. To check points near the singular cone, first discard a small neighborhood of that set and of the cone vertex. Their limiting area can be made arbitrarily small. Area convergence and the uniform bound for $z$ control the discarded part, while smooth regular convergence controls its complement. The scaled version of <a href="#var:surface-submean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:surface-submean">[var:surface-submean]</a>, with source bounded by $Cr_j^2\|z\|_\infty$, now gives uniform decay on smaller balls.

In particular, if $x_j\in\Sigma$ tends to $p$, choose scales $r_j$ comparable to $d_g(x_j,p)$. The preceding argument and the mean-value estimate show $z(x_j)\to0$; any contrary subsequence would have a tangent-cone subsequence of the type just treated. A sequence approaching the compact singular set has a subsequence tending to a particular singular point. Applying this fixed-point argument to that subsequence proves the uniform sequential assertion in <a href="#var:Jacobi-singular-behavior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:Jacobi-singular-behavior">[var:Jacobi-singular-behavior]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-58"></a>
#### Diffuse mass at a zero lapse and the final curvature bound

<a id="stmt-var-diffuse-zero-mass"></a>
<div id="var:diffuse-zero-mass" class="lemma">

**Lemma 52** (No diffuse leaf mass on the zero set). *In a uniform graph box, let $\lambda^{\mathrm{na}}$ denote the nonatomic part of its leaf measure. Let $$\mathcal Z_W=\{\ell:
       \operatorname{graph}(f_\ell)\cap W\ne\varnothing,
       \ u=0\text{ on }\operatorname{graph}(f_\ell)\cap W\}.$$ Then <a id="var:diffuse-zero-claim"></a>$$\label{var:diffuse-zero-claim}
 \lambda^{\mathrm{na}}(\mathcal Z_W)=0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-diffuse-zero-mass"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-diffuse-zero-mass</summary>

<a id="var:graph-disintegration"></a>
*Proof.* On $W=B_0\times I_0$, the coordinate $tt$ equation in <a href="#var:singular-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:singular-system">[var:singular-system]</a> is the following equality of measures: $$\label{var:graph-disintegration}
 \partial_t^2u
 =b(y,t)\,dy\,dt+
   dy\int_{\mathcal G}w(\ell,y)
       \mathbf1_{I_0}(f_\ell(y))
       \delta_{f_\ell(y)}(dt)\,d\lambda(\ell).$$ Here $y\in B_0$ and the left side and bounded-density term are restricted to $W$ as measures. The indicator records the restriction of the graph measure to the working $t$-interval; it is not a differentiation of a characteristic function. The density $b$ is bounded, and $0<w_0\leq w(\ell,y)\leq w_1$ for the contributing graphs. The weight is $c/2$ times the squared conormal on $\partial_t$, times the graph area Jacobian, divided by the volume Jacobian. All contributions on $W$ are included because their frontiers lie in $\mathcal H_V$. Pairing with product tests and using the graph area formula proves the display; measure approximation then gives its linewise disintegration.

<a id="var:BV-level-locality"></a>
For almost every $y$, $t\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}\OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}u(y,t)$ is Lipschitz and its derivative $v_y=\partial_tu(y,\cdot)$ is a one-dimensional BV function. This follows from the sliced equation, or from semiconvexity and Fubini. Write $Z_y=\{t:u(y,t)=0\}$. At any point of $Z_y$ where the two one-sided precise values of $v_y$ agree, their common value is zero: a nonzero common value would contradict the nonnegative minimum of the primitive $u(y,\cdot)$ at that point. The diffuse derivative of a one-dimensional BV function does not charge a fixed precise-value level. In the usual $D^av+D^cv$ notation this locality statement is $$\label{var:BV-level-locality}
 |D^av+D^cv|\bigl(\{\widetilde v=0\}\bigr)=0;$$ it follows from the BV chain rule, or directly from the one-dimensional coarea formula; see (Ambrosio, De Lellis, and Malý 2007, Proposition 8, equation (16)) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[AmbrosioDeLellisMaly2007](#ref-AmbrosioDeLellisMaly2007)]. Consequently the diffuse part of $Dv_y$ gives no mass to $Z_y$. The countably many jump points do not affect that assertion.

The bounded term $b$ is zero almost everywhere on $\{u=0\}$. Indeed $X=0$ there by $u\geq|X|$, and the first derivatives of $u$ and $X$ vanish almost everywhere on their respective zero sets. Thus the linear expression $B(u,X,\nabla X)$ and the connection term involving $du$ both vanish almost everywhere there. Fubini gives $b(y,\cdot)=0$ almost everywhere on $Z_y$ for almost every line.

<a id="var:linewise-zero-mass"></a>
Finally, by <a href="#var:injective-leaf-evaluation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:injective-leaf-evaluation">[var:injective-leaf-evaluation]</a> the pushforward of $w(\ell,y)d\lambda^{\mathrm{na}}(\ell)$ under $\ell\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}\OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}f_\ell(y)$ is atomless for every $y$. All line atoms of the area source in <a href="#var:graph-disintegration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:graph-disintegration">[var:graph-disintegration]</a> therefore come from transverse atoms of $\lambda$; there are no hidden atoms produced by the evaluation map. On almost every line, restrict the diffuse part of <a href="#var:graph-disintegration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:graph-disintegration">[var:graph-disintegration]</a> to $Z_y$ and use <a href="#var:BV-level-locality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:BV-level-locality">[var:BV-level-locality]</a> and the preceding vanishing of $b$. It follows that $$\label{var:linewise-zero-mass}
 \int_{\mathcal G}w(\ell,y)
       \mathbf1_{I_0}(f_\ell(y))
       \mathbf1_{\{u(y,f_\ell(y))=0\}}
                  \,d\lambda^{\mathrm{na}}(\ell)=0$$ for almost every $y$. The integrand is defined to be zero whenever $f_\ell(y)\notin I_0$, so $u$ is evaluated only inside $W$. For each leaf set $$m_W(\ell)=\int_{B_0}w(\ell,y)
                    \mathbf1_{I_0}(f_\ell(y))\,dy.$$ This quantity is strictly positive whenever its graph meets $W$: that intersection projects to a nonempty open subset of $B_0$, and $w\geq w_0>0$. A uniform positive lower bound for $m_W$ over all leaves is neither asserted nor needed. Integrate <a href="#var:linewise-zero-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:linewise-zero-mass">[var:linewise-zero-mass]</a> in $y$ and use Tonelli’s theorem. On $\mathcal Z_W$ the result is $$\int_{\mathcal Z_W}m_W(\ell)\,d\lambda^{\mathrm{na}}(\ell)=0.$$ Strict positivity of $m_W$ there gives $\lambda^{\mathrm{na}}(\mathcal Z_W)=0$, proving the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-var-area-elimination"></a>
<div id="var:area-elimination" class="proposition">

<a id="var:pi-boundary-only"></a>
**Proposition 53** (Elimination of the interior area multiplier). *Under the hypotheses above and the exclusion of a smooth interior enclosing frontier with zero future expansion in Definition <a href="#def:rigidity-class" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:rigidity-class">6</a>, the measure $\pi$ is concentrated on the given boundary: $$\label{var:pi-boundary-only}
 \pi=\delta_S,
 \qquad
 \int_{\mathcal F_*}a'_D(h)\,d\pi(D)=a'_S(h).$$ Here $\delta_S$ denotes the point mass at the filled minimizer whose frontier is $S$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-area-elimination"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-area-elimination</summary>

*Proof.* First consider a connected regular component $\Sigma$ with a nonatomic support patch. Choose the common field $\varphi$ of Lemma <a href="#var:common-jacobi" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:common-jacobi">47</a>. The functions $Q_\pm$ of <a href="#var:Q-definitions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:Q-definitions">[var:Q-definitions]</a> are bounded on the compact frontier. By Lemma <a href="#var:reciprocal-jacobi" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:reciprocal-jacobi">51</a>, $Q_\pm/\varphi$ tends to zero at every singular end. If one of these ratios is positive somewhere, it therefore attains a positive maximum at a regular interior point. This is also immediate when $\Sigma$ is smooth and compact.

<a id="var:ground-state-transform"></a>
Write $Q_\pm=\varphi v$. For an operator $L=-\Delta+b\cdot\nabla+d$ with $L\varphi=0$, direct product differentiation gives $$\label{var:ground-state-transform}
 L(\varphi v)=\varphi\left[
   -\Delta v+(b-2\nabla\log\varphi)\cdot\nabla v\right].$$ At any point where $Q_\pm>0$, one has $u>0$, so Lemma <a href="#var:weak-focusing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:weak-focusing">48</a> applies. The weak strong maximum principle for the locally Lipschitz $v$ and the smooth coefficients in <a href="#var:ground-state-transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:ground-state-transform">[var:ground-state-transform]</a> implies that $v$ is constant at its positive maximum on the connected component of $\{Q_\pm>0\}$ containing that point. This component is also closed in $\Sigma$: at a regular limit point, $Q_\pm=v\varphi>0$ by continuity. Since $\Sigma$ is connected, it is all of $\Sigma$. Substitution in <a href="#var:weak-focusing-inequalities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:weak-focusing-inequalities">[var:weak-focusing-inequalities]</a> then gives <a id="var:sheet-curvature-bound"></a>$$\label{var:sheet-curvature-bound}
 A_\Sigma\pm K_{\mathrm{tan}}=0,
 \qquad |A_\Sigma|\leq |K|.$$ The weak maximum principle used here is the ordinary local elliptic one, for example (Gilbarg and Trudinger 2001) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[GilbargTrudinger2001](#ref-GilbargTrudinger2001)]; no maximum principle on an unresolved singular boundary is used. If neither ratio is positive anywhere, $Q_+=Q_-=0$ on $\Sigma$, hence $u=0$ there and $X=0$ there by domination. Lemma <a href="#var:diffuse-zero-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:diffuse-zero-mass">52</a> excludes multiplier mass on this alternative in every nonatomic patch. In atomic patches Lemma <a href="#var:atomic-leaves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:atomic-leaves">46</a> already gives $A=0$.

We make the exceptional set independent of the regular incidence point. The open inner boxes $W$ constructed above cover all free regular points of all minimizing frontiers. Their union is an open subset of the second-countable ambient manifold, so choose a countable subcover $W_i$, retaining the corresponding middle boxes $V_i$ and graph maps $q_i$ on the open families $\mathcal H_{V_i}$. In particular the open sets $\mathcal H_{V_i}\times W_i$ cover the regular incidence set in the family-times-manifold topology.

For each $i$ let $\lambda_i=(q_i)_*(\pi|_{\mathcal H_{V_i}})$, let $\mathcal A_i$ be its countable set of atoms, and let $\mathcal Z_i=\mathcal Z_{W_i}$ be the zero-leaf set from Lemma <a href="#var:diffuse-zero-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:diffuse-zero-mass">52</a>. These are measurable sets. For the last assertion, the condition that a graph meets $W_i$ is open in the graph topology. The condition that $u$ vanishes on its intersection with $W_i$ can be checked at a countable dense set of base points: failure gives, by continuity, an open base set on which the graph stays inside $W_i$ and $u>0$. Thus it is a Borel condition.

<a id="var:uniform-frontier-curvature"></a>
The set $$\mathcal N_i=
    \bigl(C^\infty(B_{1,i})\setminus\operatorname{supp}\lambda_i\bigr)
         \cup(\mathcal Z_i\setminus\mathcal A_i)$$ has $\lambda_i$-measure zero, since the second term has $\lambda_i$-measure $\lambda_i^{\mathrm{na}}(\mathcal Z_i)=0$. Consequently $$\pi\bigl(\{F\in\mathcal H_{V_i}:q_i(F)\in\mathcal N_i\}\bigr)=0.$$ Remove the countable union of these pullbacks, together with the already excluded null set in the good family. For a remaining frontier and any of its regular points $p$, choose $i$ with $p\in W_i$. Its leaf is either an atom, giving $A=0$ locally, or a nonatom in the support of $\lambda_i$. In the latter case the common Jacobi argument applies on its entire connected regular component. If that component had $u=0$ everywhere, the leaf would belong to $\mathcal Z_i\setminus\mathcal A_i$, which was removed. Therefore the positive-ratio alternative gives $|A|\leq |K|$. This proves the curvature bound simultaneously at every regular point of every remaining frontier. In particular, $$\label{var:uniform-frontier-curvature}
 |A|\leq\sup_{\mathcal K}|K|,$$ where $\mathcal K$ is a common compact set containing the minimizing frontiers.

Fix such an interior frontier and any proposed singular point. Blow it up at scales tending to zero. The tangent cone is a multiplicity-one minimizing boundary cone, with smooth convergence on its regular part. The rescaled bound <a href="#var:uniform-frontier-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:uniform-frontier-curvature">[var:uniform-frontier-curvature]</a> tends to zero, so every regular component of the cone is totally geodesic. The equator and height argument in Lemma <a href="#var:cone-subsolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:cone-subsolution">50</a> shows that the cone is a plane. Multiplicity-one planar tangent regularity contradicts the point being singular. The entire compact frontier is therefore smooth.

This frontier is a genuine enclosing cut. In particular the minimizing filled set has no removable bounded open component of its complement: filling one would preserve the obstacle condition and strictly reduce its full perimeter. Its components carry the outward normals of that enclosing set, and <a href="#var:zero-sheet-expansions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:zero-sheet-expansions">[var:zero-sheet-expansions]</a> gives zero future expansion on every component. The assumed exclusion of a smooth interior enclosing marginal frontier rules it out. Consequently $\pi$-almost every frontier is $S$. The filled set with frontier $S$ and the prescribed obstacle is unique modulo null sets, and $\pi$ is a probability. This proves <a href="#var:pi-boundary-only" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:pi-boundary-only">[var:pi-boundary-only]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-59"></a>
#### Smooth stationarity and the boundary laws

We finish the original-data variation argument. The lapse $u$ and shift $X$ in the following proposition are the measure moments constructed above; they are unrelated to the lapse notation used locally in the filled scalar construction.

<a id="stmt-var-stationary-development"></a>
<div id="var:stationary-development" class="proposition">

<a id="var:lapse-shift-end"></a>
<a id="var:stationary-product"></a>
<a id="var:stationary-einstein"></a>
**Proposition 54** (Stationary data supplied by equality). *<span id="var:stationary-data" label="var:stationary-data"></span> There are smooth fields $u,X$ on $\Omega$, including $S$, with $u\ge |X|$, $u>0$ in the open exterior and <a id="var:smooth-kid-complementarity"></a>$$\operatorname{sym}\nabla X=-uK,\qquad
 u\mu_m+J_m(X)=0.
 \label{var:smooth-kid-complementarity}$$ For any sufficiently small decrease $q_0<q$ with $q_0>(n-2)/2$, their end behavior is $$(u,X)=(b^0,b^i)+O_2(r^{-q_0}).
 \label{var:lapse-shift-end}$$ On $\mathbb R_z\times\operatorname{int}\Omega$ set $$\mathbf g=-u^2\mathrm dz^2+
             g_{ij}(\mathrm dx^i+X^i\mathrm dz)(\mathrm dx^j+X^j\mathrm dz),
 \qquad \xi=\partial_z,\qquad
 \mathbf F=c_n\mathrm dz\wedge uE^\flat.
 \label{var:stationary-product}$$ The stationary fields satisfy Maxwell’s equations and $$\operatorname{Ein}_{\mathbf g}-\mathsf S
       =\mu_m u^{-2}\xi^\flat\otimes\xi^\flat,
 \qquad
 \mathsf S_{ab}=2\left(\mathbf F_{ac}\mathbf F_b{}^c
           -\tfrac14\mathbf F_{cd}\mathbf F^{cd}\mathbf g_{ab}\right).
 \label{var:stationary-einstein}$$ The extra term is zero unless $\xi$ is null. The original slice has future normal $N=(\partial_z-X)/u$ and induces exactly $(g,K,E)$, with $\mathbf F$ restricting to zero on it. There is a global smooth function $p$, including up to $S$, with <a id="var:potential-maxwell"></a>$$\mathrm dp=(k-1)uE^\flat,
 \qquad p(\infty)=0,
 \qquad p=O_2(r^{2-n}),
 \qquad \mathrm d(i_X\alpha)=0.
 \label{var:potential-maxwell}$$*

<a id="var:positive-lapse-boundary"></a>
<a id="var:zero-lapse-boundary"></a>
*Let $\kappa=c/2>0$. On $S$ the boundary laws are <a id="var:horizon-boundary-laws"></a>$$X=u\nu,\qquad
 \partial_\nu u+K(X,\nu)=\kappa,\qquad
 cH=2L_+^*u,
 \label{var:horizon-boundary-laws}$$ where $L_+$ is the outward normal linearization of $H+\operatorname{tr}_S K$, and its adjoint uses $\mathrm dA_g$. Either $u>0$ on all of $S$, or $u=0$ on all of $S$. The nonnegative function $W=u^2-|X|^2$ tends to one at infinity, vanishes on $S$, and is strictly positive in a deleted collar of $S$. If $u>0$ on $S$, then $$\mathrm dW=2\kappa X^\flat\quad\hbox{on }S.
 \label{var:positive-lapse-boundary}$$ If $u=0$ on $S$, writing $s$ for exterior distance from $S$, there are smooth fields $a_*,Y_*$ such that $$u=s a_*,\qquad X=s^2Y_*,\qquad a_*|_S=\kappa.
 \label{var:zero-lapse-boundary}$$ No assertion of a Lorentzian extension across the zero-lapse boundary is made at this stage.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-var-stationary-development"><summary>Proof of stmt-var-stationary-development</summary>

*Proof.* *Smoothness and boundary moments.* Proposition <a href="#var:area-elimination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:area-elimination">53</a> removes the interior surface measure from <a href="#var:hessian-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:hessian-measure">[var:hessian-measure]</a>. Together with the differentiated KID equation, the resulting equations express all second derivatives of $u,X$ as smooth linear expressions in their first jets. Ordinary elliptic regularity makes them smooth in the open exterior. The full first-jet system is closed; along the normals of a smooth collar it is a linear ODE with smooth coefficients. Starting on an interior collar face, solve that ODE to $S$. Smooth dependence on the tangential parameters gives smooth extension of all fields and their jets. This argument also shows that a zero full first jet at one interior point would force the solution to vanish throughout the connected open exterior.

There can still, at this point, be constraint moment measures supported on $S$. Integrate the smooth interior fields by parts in <a href="#var:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:separation">[var:separation]</a>. Take $K'=0$ and both form variations zero, and choose a metric test with value and first jet zero at $S$. The trace of its normal second derivatives in the tangential slots is arbitrary, so its scalar-curvature variation at $S$ is arbitrary. Its area and expansion variations, all integrated interior boundary terms, momentum variation and ray-transport terms at $S$ vanish. Equation <a href="#var:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:separation">[var:separation]</a> therefore kills the scalar boundary moment. Positivity and domination of the vector moment by the scalar moment kill the vector boundary moment as well. Henceforth the moments in that identity are just the smooth functions $u,X$ times volume.

<a id="var:end-jet-estimate"></a>
*The end constants and positive lapse.* The closed first-jet system gives, for $Y=(u,X)$ in end coordinates, $$|D^2Y|\le C r^{-1-q}|DY|+C r^{-2-q}|Y|.
 \label{var:end-jet-estimate}$$ Indeed curvature, $\nabla K$, $K^2$, the constraints and the electric stress multiply values, whereas $K$ and metric first derivatives multiply first derivatives. These orders require only the stated $O_2/O_1$ tails. Radial integration and Gronwall first give $Y=O(r)$ and $DY=O(1)$: write $|Y(r)|$ as its initial value plus the radial integral of $|DY|$ and use the integrability of $r^{-1-q}$. Equation <a href="#var:end-jet-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:end-jet-estimate">[var:end-jet-estimate]</a> now gives a limit of $DY$ on each ray with error $O(r^{-q})$. The limits agree on all rays, since integration of $D^2Y$ along a path of length $O(r)$ on a large sphere gives an $O(r^{-q})$ difference. Denote the common linear part by $\mathcal A$.

The scalar affine part cannot be nonzero: $u\ge0$ on all large spheres. Once that part is zero, $|X|\le u$ rules out every growing vector affine part as well. Thus $\mathcal A=0$. Since $n\ge4$ and $q>(n-2)/2$, we have $q>1$. Integration gives bounded $Y$, a common constant limit $Y_\infty$, and initially $Y-Y_\infty=O(r^{1-q})$. Reinserting boundedness in <a href="#var:end-jet-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:end-jet-estimate">[var:end-jet-estimate]</a> improves the Hessian to $O(r^{-2-q})$, and two integrations give $Y-Y_\infty=O_2(r^{-q})$. The stated weaker exponent $q_0$ is therefore available.

Apply <a href="#var:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:separation">[var:separation]</a> to the end prototypes and integrate by parts on large truncations. The interior adjoint equations and the compact boundary terms cancel. Only the constant scalar multiplying the linear scalar ADM flux, and twice the constant vector multiplying the linear momentum flux, remain. All other boundary terms vanish by <a href="#var:end-jet-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:end-jet-estimate">[var:end-jet-estimate]</a>, the prototype orders and the background decay. The surviving identity is <a id="var:prototype-normalization"></a>$$2k\omega\bigl(b^0\delta\mathcal E_{\mathrm{ADM}}
                     +b^i\delta(\mathbf P_{\mathrm{ADM}})_i\bigr)
 =2k\omega\bigl(u_\infty\delta\mathcal E_{\mathrm{ADM}}
                     +X_\infty^i\delta(\mathbf P_{\mathrm{ADM}})_i\bigr).
 \label{var:prototype-normalization}$$ The prototype fluxes span all entries, proving <a href="#var:lapse-shift-end" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:lapse-shift-end">[var:lapse-shift-end]</a>. If $u$ vanished at an interior point, smooth nonnegativity would give $\mathrm du=0$ there. Domination would give $X=0$ and $\nabla X=0$ there as well, since $|X|\le u=O(d^2)$. The full-jet uniqueness noted above would contradict $u_\infty=b^0>0$. Thus $u>0$ in the interior.

*The stationary Einstein equation.* The stationary metric in <a href="#var:stationary-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:stationary-product">[var:stationary-product]</a> has second fundamental form $b=-\operatorname{sym}\nabla X/u=K$ on each slice. We recall the metric-adjoint computation, now with no interior area source. Put $\mathcal Q(A,B)=A:B-(\operatorname{tr}A)(\operatorname{tr}B)$. Integration of the momentum pairing gives <a id="var:stationary-action"></a>$$u\bigl(R_g-\mathcal Q(K,K)+2\mathcal Q(K,b)\bigr)
 =u\bigl(R_g+\mathcal Q(b,b)-\mathcal Q(K-b,K-b)\bigr).
 \label{var:stationary-action}$$ At $b=K$ its metric first variation equals that of the stationary scalar action, at fixed lapse and shift, up to the integrated divergence. Its spatial coefficient is $-u\operatorname{Ein}_{ij}$. Equation <a href="#var:electric-metric-adjoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:electric-metric-adjoint">[var:electric-metric-adjoint]</a> supplies the electromagnetic coefficient. The variation of the constraint volume factor vanishes by complementarity, and isometric transport of the active unit-ball rays adds $-(J_m)_{(i}X_{j)}$. Thus <a id="var:spatial-einstein-adjoint"></a>$$u(\operatorname{Ein}-\mathsf S)_{ij}
                         =-(J_m)_{(i}X_{j)}.
 \label{var:spatial-einstein-adjoint}$$ Gauss–Codazzi gives the remaining normal and mixed components as $\mu_m,J_m$. Where $\mu_m=0$, DEC gives $J_m=0$. Where $\mu_m>0$, equality in $$-J_m(X)\le |J_m||X|\le\mu_m u$$ forces $|X|=u$ and $J_m=-\mu_m X^\flat/u$. Consequently all components combine to give <a href="#var:stationary-einstein" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:stationary-einstein">[var:stationary-einstein]</a>, and the extra matter can occur only at $W=0$.

<a id="var:electric-form-test"></a>
*Maxwell tests and a global potential.* Test <a href="#var:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:separation">[var:separation]</a> with the metric and second tensor fixed and a compactly supported closed variation of $\alpha$. The resulting identity, after dividing by the nonzero factor $4c_n^2$, is $$\int_\Omega uE^\flat\wedge\alpha'=0
 \quad\hbox{for every compactly supported closed }
                         (n-1)\hbox{-form }\alpha'.
 \label{var:electric-form-test}$$ Exact tests imply $\mathrm d(uE^\flat)=0$. All closed tests, not only exact ones, are available. Compact-support de Rham duality on the oriented open manifold $\operatorname{int}\Omega$ therefore makes $uE^\flat$ exact; see (Bott and Tu 1982) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[BottTu1982](#ref-BottTu1982)]. This step does not presume simple connectivity. Define $p$ by $\mathrm dp=(k-1)uE^\flat$. Since $u$ is bounded at infinity and $E=O_1(r^{-k})$, radial integration and integration along large spheres give one common limit of $p$ at infinity and $p-p_\infty=O_2(r^{2-n})$. Choose $p_\infty=0$. Integration in local collars extends $p$ smoothly to $S$.

<a id="var:magnetic-form-test"></a>
Independent closed compact two-form tests of $D_m$ give $$\int_\Omega D_m'\wedge i_X\alpha=0,
 \qquad\hbox{and hence}\qquad \mathrm d(i_X\alpha)=0.
 \label{var:magnetic-form-test}$$ The degree of $i_X\alpha$ is $n-2$, so this pairing and its differential have the required degrees in every dimension. With orientation $\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_{\mathbf g}=u\mathrm dz\wedge\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g$, a direct orthonormal-frame computation gives <a id="var:star-field"></a>$$*_\mathbf g\mathbf F=-c_n
          \bigl(\alpha+\mathrm dz\wedge i_X\alpha\bigr).
 \label{var:star-field}$$ Equations <a href="#var:electric-form-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:electric-form-test">[var:electric-form-test]</a>, <a href="#var:magnetic-form-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:magnetic-form-test">[var:magnetic-form-test]</a> and $\mathrm d\alpha=0$ therefore give both Maxwell equations. Contraction of $\mathbf F$ with $N=(\partial_z-X)/u$ and pullback to a slice yields $c_nE^\flat$; its spatial pullback is zero. This verifies all conventions in the claimed original-data realization.

*Boundary laws.* The outward integration normal of $\Omega$ at $S$ is $-\nu$. The boundary coefficient of a free second-tensor variation in <a href="#var:separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:separation">[var:separation]</a> is <a id="var:boundary-pairing-k"></a>$$-2\operatorname{sym}(X^\flat\otimes\nu^\flat)
       +2X_\nu g
       -\frac{\mathrm d\zeta}{\mathrm dA_g}
                         (g-\nu^\flat\otimes\nu^\flat).
 \label{var:boundary-pairing-k}$$ Interpreting this first as an equality of measures, its tangential and mixed entries show $X_{\rm tan}=0$ and $\mathrm d\zeta=2X_\nu\mathrm dA_g$. Now use compact scalar tests with $(h,K')=(2s g,sK)$ and the forms fixed. Their constraint differential terms are $-2ku\Delta s+2kK(X,\nabla s)$; the other terms cancel by the interior adjoint equation. Since $\theta_+=0$, their expansion derivative is $k\partial_\nu s$, while their area derivative is $k\int_Ss$. Thus <a id="var:boundary-conformal-pairing"></a>$$-kc\int_Ss\,\mathrm dA_g
 =2k\int_S\left[u\partial_\nu s
       -(\partial_\nu u+K(X,\nu))s\right]\mathrm dA_g
             -k\int_S\partial_\nu s\,\mathrm d\zeta.
 \label{var:boundary-conformal-pairing}$$ The boundary value and normal derivative of $s$ are independent. The derivative coefficient gives $u=X_\nu$, and the value coefficient gives $\partial_\nu u+K(X,\nu)=c/2$.

For the last boundary identity, take Lie variations of all the data along a vector field extending arbitrary $s\nu$ on $S$. Such tests are legitimate field variations on the fixed manifold: the jets may be defined by a smooth extension across $S$; no flow preserving the boundary is asserted. The active interior constraints have zero first variation by spatial transport at a minimum of their nonnegative contractions. The induced rotation of the transported unit ball contributes zero for the same reason. The area and expansion derivatives at $S$ are, respectively, $\int_SHs$ and $L_+s$. Hence $$c\int_SHs\,\mathrm dA_g=2\int_SuL_+s\,\mathrm dA_g,$$ which proves $cH=2L_+^*u$. One-sided outer area minimality gives $H\ge0$ by nonnegative outward tests. The strong minimum principle for this smooth elliptic equation and $u\ge0$ on connected $S$ gives the boundary dichotomy in the proposition.

The boundary laws give $W=0$ on $S$. If $u>0$ there, differentiate $W$ and use <a href="#var:first-kid" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:first-kid">[var:first-kid]</a>; tangential derivatives vanish and $$\partial_\nu W
   =2u\bigl(\partial_\nu u+uK(\nu,\nu)\bigr)
   =2\kappa u.$$ This is <a href="#var:positive-lapse-boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:positive-lapse-boundary">[var:positive-lapse-boundary]</a> and implies positivity in a deleted exterior collar. If $u=0$ on $S$, then $X=0$ there and $\partial_\nu u=\kappa$. All tangential derivatives of $X$ vanish at $S$; the normal-normal and mixed parts of the KID equation then give $\nabla_\nu X=0$ there. Smooth division by the boundary defining function gives <a href="#var:zero-lapse-boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:zero-lapse-boundary">[var:zero-lapse-boundary]</a>. In particular $W=\kappa^2s^2+O(s^3)>0$ in a deleted collar. Finally <a href="#var:lapse-shift-end" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="var:lapse-shift-end">[var:lapse-shift-end]</a> and $(b^0)^2-|b|^2=1$ imply $W\to1$. ◻

</details>

On the positive set of $W$ it will be useful to introduce <a id="var:orbit-metric"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 \lambda=\sqrt W,\qquad
 h_s=g+W^{-1}X^\flat\otimes X^\flat,\\
 C=h_s^{-1}=g^{-1}-u^{-2}X\otimes X,\qquad
 A_s=W^{-1}X^\flat.
\end{gathered}
 \label{var:orbit-metric}$$ The stationary metric is then $\mathbf g=-\lambda^2(\mathrm dz-A_s)^2+h_s$. These definitions are made only where $W>0$; the next section proves global staticity and rules out interior zeros, rather than imposing either conclusion here.

<a id="stat:section"></a>
### Electric transport and the complete vacuum static base

The stationary development supplied by the preceding section need not initially have a timelike Killing field everywhere. We first transport the electric alignment from the boundary, and then prove staticity without making any regularity assumption on the zero set of the Killing norm. The charged case is reduced to vacuum by an explicit change of potential. In the zero-charge case, a complete Riemannian circle extension excludes an additional interior zero set. No black-hole uniqueness theorem is used in this section.

<a id="stat:interface-fields"></a>
<a id="stat:remaining-matter"></a>
Throughout this section, $k=n-1$, $c_n^2=k(k-1)/2$, and all spatial norms, divergences, and volume forms without a subscript refer to the original metric $g$. Write $\Omega^\circ=\Omega\setminus S$. We use Proposition <a href="#var:stationary-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:stationary-data">[var:stationary-data]</a>, whose hypotheses are the connected nonextremal equality hypotheses of the main theorem. In particular, it supplies smooth functions and fields $u,X,p$ on the original exterior, with $$u>0\quad\hbox{on }\Omega^\circ,\qquad
 u\geq |X|,\qquad
 dp=(k-1)uE^\flat,\qquad
 d(i_X\alpha)=0,\qquad \alpha=i_E\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g.
 \label{stat:interface-fields}$$ The potential tends to zero at infinity. On $\mathbb R_z\times\Omega^\circ$ the stationary Lorentz metric and Faraday form are <a id="stat:stationary-metric"></a>$$\mathbf g=-u^2dz^2+g_{ij}(dx^i+X^i dz)(dx^j+X^j dz),\qquad
 F=c_n\,dz\wedge uE^\flat.
 \label{stat:stationary-metric}$$ The Killing field is $\xi=\partial_z$, and its future unit normal to a constant-$z$ slice is $\mathbf n=(\partial_z-X)/u$. Both Maxwell equations hold. If $\mathbf G$ is the Einstein tensor and $\mathsf S$ the electromagnetic stress tensor with the normalization fixed in the problem, the remaining matter tensor satisfies $$\mathbf G-\mathsf S
   =\mu_m u^{-2}\xi^\flat\otimes\xi^\flat,
 \qquad \mu_m=0\quad\hbox{where }u>|X|.
 \label{stat:remaining-matter}$$

<a id="stat:boundary-laws"></a>
<a id="stat:zero-lapse-factors"></a>
<a id="stat:end-exponent"></a>
<a id="stat:end-interface"></a>
Set <a id="stat:W-kappa"></a>$$W=u^2-|X|^2=-\mathbf g(\xi,\xi)\geq0,
 \qquad
 \kappa=\frac{k-1}{2r_h}
       \left(1-\frac{Q^2}{r_h^{2(k-1)}}\right)>0.
 \label{stat:W-kappa}$$ The boundary conclusions of Proposition <a href="#var:stationary-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="var:stationary-data">[var:stationary-data]</a> are $$X=u\nu,\qquad
 \partial_\nu u+K(X,\nu)=\kappa\quad\hbox{on }S,
 \qquad W|_S=0.
 \label{stat:boundary-laws}$$ Either $u>0$ on all of $S$, in which case $dW=2\kappa X^\flat$ there, or $u=0$ on all of $S$. In the latter case, in the original distance $s$ into the exterior from $S$, $$u=s a,\qquad X=s^2Y,\qquad a|_S=\kappa,
 \label{stat:zero-lapse-factors}$$ with smooth $a,Y$. Thus $W>0$ on a deleted boundary collar in either case. On the end, for a fixed $$\frac{n-2}{2}<q_0<\min(q,n-2),
 \label{stat:end-exponent}$$ we have $$(u,X)=(b^0,b)+O_2(r^{-q_0}),\qquad
 (b^0)^2-|b|_\delta^2=1,\qquad
 p=O_2(r^{2-n}).
 \label{stat:end-interface}$$ Decreasing $q_0$ while retaining <a href="#stat:end-exponent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:end-exponent">[stat:end-exponent]</a> is harmless. These are the only conclusions from the preceding section used below.

<a id="pm-h-61"></a>
#### Boundary alignment and electric transport

<a id="stmt-stat-electric-alignment"></a>
<div id="stat:electric-alignment" class="lemma">

<a id="stat:alignment"></a>
**Lemma 55** (Global electric alignment). *The potential $p$ is constant on $S$, and $$X\wedge E=0\quad\hbox{throughout }\Omega.
 \label{stat:alignment}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stat-electric-alignment"><summary>Proof of stmt-stat-electric-alignment</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose first that $u>0$ on $S$. The stationary metric is smooth and nondegenerate up to the hypersurface $\mathbb R\times S$. Equations <a href="#stat:boundary-laws" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:boundary-laws">[stat:boundary-laws]</a> imply that $\xi$ is a null normal to this hypersurface: it is null, is tangent to it, and is orthogonal to all its tangent vectors. Its null second fundamental form vanishes, since its flow preserves the metric induced on the transverse sections. The null expansion equation consequently gives $\operatorname{Ric}_{\mathbf g}(\xi,\xi)=0$. This assertion only uses the one-sided smooth jets on the hypersurface. The nonaffine normalization of its null generators contributes a multiple of the expansion, which is zero.

The extra tensor in <a href="#stat:remaining-matter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:remaining-matter">[stat:remaining-matter]</a> has zero contraction with $(\xi,\xi)$ there. Maxwell null positivity therefore gives $$0=2\lvert i_\xi F\rvert_{\mathbf g}^2.$$ A covector annihilating a null vector has nonnegative Lorentz norm, and its norm is zero precisely when it is proportional to that null vector’s metric dual. Thus $i_\xi F$ annihilates the tangent space of the horizon. Since $i_\xi F=c_nuE^\flat$, the tangential component of $E$ on $S$ is zero. Equations <a href="#stat:interface-fields" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:interface-fields">[stat:interface-fields]</a> and <a href="#stat:boundary-laws" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:boundary-laws">[stat:boundary-laws]</a> give $X\wedge E=0$ and $dp|_{TS}=0$. If $u=0$ on $S$, these two assertions follow immediately from $X=0$ and $dp=(k-1)uE^\flat$. Connectedness of $S$ gives a single boundary value, denoted by $p_b$.

<a id="stat:electric-bracket"></a>
The divergence-free constraint and Cartan’s formula give $$0=d(i_X\alpha)=\mathcal L_X(i_E\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g)
   =i_{[X,E]+(\operatorname{div}X)E}\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g.$$ In particular, $$+(\operatorname{div}X)E=0,
 \qquad \mathcal L_E(X\wedge E)=0.
 \label{stat:electric-bracket}$$ Alignment therefore propagates along every nonzero electric trajectory once it is known at one point of that trajectory.

Here is the measure argument ensuring sufficiently many boundary hits. Let $\Pi$ be a relatively compact regular level patch $p=p_0\ne0$ in the interior. Follow $V=\operatorname{sgn}(p_0)E$ from this patch until its first boundary hit, with maximal time $T(y)\in(0,\infty]$. Along each trajectory $|p|$ increases strictly, because $$dp(E)=(k-1)u|E|^2>0$$ where $E\ne0$. All these trajectories remain in a fixed compact subset of the original exterior: outside a sufficiently large coordinate sphere, $|p|<|p_0|$. The trajectory map $(t,y)\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}\OriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{}\Phi_t(y)$ is injective for $0<t<T(y)$, by uniqueness of the flow and strict monotonicity of $p$.

Let $d\mathfrak f$ be the positive transverse flux measure $i_V\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g$ on $\Pi$, oriented appropriately. Since $\operatorname{div}V=0$, the volume element in this flow tube is $dt\,d\mathfrak f$. Consequently <a id="stat:flow-time-volume"></a>$$\int_\Pi T(y)\,d\mathfrak f(y)
 \leq \operatorname{Vol}_g(K_\Pi)<\infty,
 \label{stat:flow-time-volume}$$ where $K_\Pi$ is the fixed compact region just described. Hence $T(y)<\infty$ for almost every $y$ in flux measure. A finite-time trajectory cannot terminate in the interior of that compact region. This follows by smoothly extending the vector field locally and using ordinary differential equation existence. Nor can it reach a zero of $E$ at a finite time, by uniqueness. It therefore hits $S$ with $E\ne0$.

Equation <a href="#stat:electric-bracket" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:electric-bracket">[stat:electric-bracket]</a> transports the boundary alignment back to almost every point of $\Pi$. The flux density is smooth and strictly positive on this regular patch, so continuity gives alignment on the whole patch. Varying the patch proves it at every point with $p\ne0$ and $E\ne0$. If $p=0$ and $E\ne0$ at an interior point, the derivative of $p$ along $E$ is nonzero there, so such a point is a limit of the preceding points. At zeros of $E$ alignment is automatic. This proves <a href="#stat:alignment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:alignment">[stat:alignment]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-62"></a>
#### Killing identities and a twist estimate through zero sets

<a id="stat:orbit-quantities"></a>
On the open set $\mathcal U=\{W>0\}$ define $$\begin{gathered}
 \lambda=\sqrt W,\qquad
 h_s=g+W^{-1}X^\flat\otimes X^\flat,\\
 C=h_s^{-1}=g^{-1}-u^{-2}X\otimes X,\qquad
 A_s=W^{-1}X^\flat,\qquad F_s=dA_s.
\end{gathered}
 \label{stat:orbit-quantities}$$ Here $C$ is written as a contravariant tensor on the original slice; it extends smoothly and is positive semidefinite everywhere in the interior. The stationary metric is <a id="stat:orbit-splitting"></a>$$\mathbf g=-\lambda^2(dz-A_s)^2+h_s
 \quad\hbox{on }\mathbb R\times\mathcal U.
 \label{stat:orbit-splitting}$$

<a id="stmt-stat-killing-identities"></a>
<div id="stat:killing-identities" class="lemma">

<a id="stat:ricci-eigenvalue"></a>
<a id="stat:killing-wave"></a>
<a id="stat:twist-current"></a>
**Lemma 56** (Killing curvature and flux identities). *Put $D_{ab}=\boldsymbol\nabla_a\xi_b$, where $\boldsymbol\nabla$ is the Lorentz connection. Then $$\operatorname{Ric}^{a}{}_{b}\xi^b
      =-(k-1)^2|E|^2\xi^a,
 \label{stat:ricci-eigenvalue}$$ and, throughout the original open exterior, $$u^{-1}\operatorname{div}(uC\,dW)
   =-2D_{ab}D^{ab}+2\operatorname{Ric}(\xi,\xi).
 \label{stat:killing-wave}$$ The smooth antisymmetric tensor $\widetilde Q^{ij}=u(d\xi^\flat)^{ij}$ satisfies $\nabla_i\widetilde Q^{ij}=0$. On $\mathcal U$ it is $$Q^{ij}=uW C^{ia}C^{jb}(F_s)_{ab}.
 \label{stat:twist-current}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stat-killing-identities"><summary>Proof of stmt-stat-killing-identities</summary>

<a id="stat:ricci-null-contraction"></a>
*Proof.* In spacetime dimension $k+2$, the electromagnetic trace reversal is $$\operatorname{Ric}_{ab}
   =2F_{ac}F_b{}^c-\frac1k(F_{cd}F^{cd})\mathbf g_{ab}$$ apart from the matter term in <a href="#stat:remaining-matter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:remaining-matter">[stat:remaining-matter]</a>, which is trace-free wherever it is nonzero. In an orthonormal frame whose timelike vector is $\mathbf n$, the electric magnitude is $c_n|E|$. The mixed Ricci eigenvalue on the plane spanned by $\mathbf n$ and $E$ is therefore $$-2c_n^2\left(1-\frac1k\right)|E|^2
   =-(k-1)^2|E|^2.$$ Lemma <a href="#stat:electric-alignment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stat:electric-alignment">55</a> places $\xi=u\mathbf n+X$ in this plane wherever $E\ne0$. The additional matter tensor annihilates $\xi$, since it is supported where $\mathbf g(\xi,\xi)=0$. The same formula holds at $E=0$. This proves <a href="#stat:ricci-eigenvalue" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:ricci-eigenvalue">[stat:ricci-eigenvalue]</a>, including $$\operatorname{Ric}(\xi,\xi)=(k-1)^2|E|^2W.
 \label{stat:ricci-null-contraction}$$

For a Killing field, $D$ is skew and $\boldsymbol\nabla_a(d\xi^\flat)^{ab}
=-2\operatorname{Ric}^{b}{}_{a}\xi^a$. Applying the Killing wave identity to $W=-\mathbf g(\xi,\xi)$ gives <a href="#stat:killing-wave" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:killing-wave">[stat:killing-wave]</a>: a stationary scalar has wave operator $u^{-1}\operatorname{div}(uC\,d\,\cdot)$ because the spatial inverse block of $\mathbf g$ is $C$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_{\mathbf g}=u\,dz\wedge\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_g$. The spatial components of the right side of the Killing divergence identity vanish by <a href="#stat:ricci-eigenvalue" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:ricci-eigenvalue">[stat:ricci-eigenvalue]</a>. Antisymmetry removes the remaining Christoffel term, and hence $\nabla_i\widetilde Q^{ij}=0$. Finally, $$\xi^\flat=-W(dz-A_s),\qquad
 d\xi^\flat=-dW\wedge(dz-A_s)+WF_s.$$ Raising both spatial indices eliminates the first summand and gives <a href="#stat:twist-current" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:twist-current">[stat:twist-current]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-stat-vanishing-twist"></a>
<div id="stat:vanishing-twist" class="lemma">

**Lemma 57** (Vanishing twist). *The two-form $F_s$ vanishes on every component of $\mathcal U$. No regularity of the interior set $\{W=0\}$ is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stat-vanishing-twist"><summary>Proof of stmt-stat-vanishing-twist</summary>

<a id="stat:glaeser"></a>
*Proof.* The interior zero set $Z_0=\{W=0\}\cap\Omega^\circ$ is compact, since $W\to1$ at infinity and is positive on a deleted boundary collar. On a fixed relatively compact neighborhood of $Z_0$, both $u$ and $|X|$ have positive lower bounds. Put $a=|X|$, $e=X/a$, and $T=e^\perp$ there. The eigenvalues of $C$ are one on $T$ and $W/u^2$ on $e$. Smooth nonnegativity of $W$ implies the local estimate $$|dW|^2\leq C_0W.
 \label{stat:glaeser}$$ For completeness, this follows by restricting to short coordinate line segments, using a bound for the Hessian, and minimizing the resulting quadratic Taylor upper bound at a point where the function is nonnegative. A finite cover gives a uniform constant on the chosen neighborhood.

<a id="stat:W-inequality"></a>
Use a slice-orthonormal frame $(e,e_A)$ and complete it by the future normal $\mathbf n$. Since $\xi=u\mathbf n+ae$, $$D_{i\mathbf n}=-\frac{W_i/2+aD_{ie}}u.$$ An explicit expansion of the Lorentz norm gives <a id="stat:D-expansion"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 -2D_{ab}D^{ab}
 ={}&\frac{|dW|^2}{u^2}
     +\frac{4a}{u^2}\sum_A W_A D_{Ae}
     -\frac{4W}{u^2}\sum_A D_{Ae}^2
     -4\sum_{A<B}D_{AB}^2.
 \label{stat:D-expansion}
\end{aligned}$$ The original fields and their derivatives are bounded on this fixed neighborhood. Equations <a href="#stat:glaeser" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:glaeser">[stat:glaeser]</a>, <a href="#stat:ricci-null-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:ricci-null-contraction">[stat:ricci-null-contraction]</a>, and <a href="#stat:killing-wave" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:killing-wave">[stat:killing-wave]</a> therefore imply $$u^{-1}\operatorname{div}(uC\,dW)
       \leq C_1\bigl(W+|d_TW|\bigr).
 \label{stat:W-inequality}$$

<a id="stat:log-energy"></a>
Choose a smooth compactly supported $\eta$ in this neighborhood. For $\delta>0$, test <a href="#stat:W-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:W-inequality">[stat:W-inequality]</a> with $\eta^2/(W+\delta)$. There is no singular integration here: $C,W$ are smooth and the denominator is positive. Integration by parts gives, with $L W=u^{-1}\operatorname{div}(uC\,dW)$, $$\begin{aligned}
 I_\delta
 &:=\int u\eta^2\frac{|dW|_C^2}{(W+\delta)^2}
  =\int\frac{u\eta^2LW}{W+\delta}
     +2\int\frac{u\eta\,C(d\eta,dW)}{W+\delta},\\
 |dW|_C^2&=|d_TW|^2+\frac W{u^2}\bigl(dW(e)\bigr)^2.
\end{aligned}$$ Since $0\leq C\leq g^{-1}$, Young’s inequality absorbs both the cutoff term and the $|d_TW|/(W+\delta)$ term. Explicitly, each is at most $I_\delta/4$ plus a constant times $\int u(\eta^2+|d\eta|^2)$. The remaining $W/(W+\delta)$ term is bounded. Consequently $$I_\delta\leq C_\eta\quad(0<\delta<1),\qquad
 \int_{\mathcal U}\eta^2|d_T\log W|^2<\infty.
 \label{stat:log-energy}$$ Take finitely many such cutoffs equal to one near $Z_0$.

<a id="stat:exact-twist-flux"></a>
We record the exact flux used at the zero-set cutoff. Contract <a href="#stat:twist-current" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:twist-current">[stat:twist-current]</a> with $A_s$, and lower the remaining index using $g$. Since $C X=(W/u^2)X$, direct substitution of $F_s=d(W^{-1}X^\flat)$ gives $$J^i=Q^{ij}(A_s)_j,\qquad
 J^\flat_i=\frac1u\left(
        X^j(dX^\flat)_{ij}-|X|^2(d_T\log W)_i\right).
 \label{stat:exact-twist-flux}$$ In particular $J$ is exactly orthogonal to $X$, and near $Z_0$ <a id="stat:twist-flux-bound"></a>$$|J|\leq C\bigl(1+|d_T\log W|\bigr).
 \label{stat:twist-flux-bound}$$

Choose a smooth cutoff $\chi:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]$, zero on $[0,1]$ and one on $[2,\infty)$. A zero-set cutoff $z_\delta$ can be chosen to equal $\chi(W/\delta)$ near $Z_0$ and one outside the fixed neighborhood. Indeed place the spatial localization transition on a compact set disjoint from $Z_0$; $W$ has a positive lower bound there, so for all sufficiently small $\delta$ no transition derivative remains. On the support of $dz_\delta$, transversality in <a href="#stat:exact-twist-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:exact-twist-flux">[stat:exact-twist-flux]</a> yields <a id="stat:zero-cutoff-error"></a>$$|J(dz_\delta)|
 \leq C\frac W\delta
       \left(|d_T\log W|+|d_T\log W|^2\right)
 \leq C\left(1+|d_T\log W|^2\right).
 \label{stat:zero-cutoff-error}$$ This is supported where $\delta<W<2\delta$. Its integral tends to zero by <a href="#stat:log-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:log-energy">[stat:log-energy]</a> and dominated convergence. In particular, an open portion of $Z_0$ causes no omitted contribution: all test fields used below vanish on a neighborhood of it.

Next choose a distance cutoff $\beta_\varepsilon$ at $S$, zero for $s\leq\varepsilon$ and one for $s\geq2\varepsilon$, with derivative bounded by $C/\varepsilon$. If $u>0$ on $S$, the boundary laws give $$W\asymp s,\qquad e=\nu_s+O(s),\qquad d_TW=O(s),$$ where $\nu_s=\nabla s$. Thus $J=O(1)$ and $J\cdot\nu_s=O(s)$ by its exact transversality. The resulting boundary error is $O(\varepsilon)$. If $u=0$ on $S$, write $$W=s^2w,\qquad w=a^2-s^2|Y|^2>0$$ using <a href="#stat:zero-lapse-factors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:zero-lapse-factors">[stat:zero-lapse-factors]</a>. Then $A_s=Y^\flat/w$, $F_s$, and $C$ are smooth up to $S$. Equation <a href="#stat:twist-current" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:twist-current">[stat:twist-current]</a> gives $Q=O(s^3)$ and $J=O(s^3)$, so the boundary error is $O(\varepsilon^3)$.

It remains to make the test field decay at infinity. In the end coordinates let $A_\infty$ be the constant limit of $A_s$. Choose a smooth function $$\psi(x)=\rho(r)(A_\infty)_ix^i,$$ where $\rho$ is zero near the compact core and one far out. Its support is chosen disjoint from the boundary and zero-set cutoff regions. Set $B=A_s-d\psi$ on $\mathcal U$. Then $dB=F_s$ and <a id="stat:twist-end-decay"></a>$$B=O_1(r^{-q_0}),\qquad Q=O(r^{-1-q_0}).
 \label{stat:twist-end-decay}$$ There is no bulk term from the transition of $\psi$, since $Q^{ij}\nabla_i\nabla_j\psi=0$ by antisymmetry. If $\theta_R$ is one for $r\leq R$, zero for $r\geq2R$, and $|d\theta_R|\leq C/R$, its error is bounded by <a id="stat:twist-infinity-error"></a>$$\left|\int Q^{ij}B_j\partial_i\theta_R\right|
       \leq C R^{n-2-2q_0}\longrightarrow0.
 \label{stat:twist-infinity-error}$$

<a id="stat:twist-test"></a>
Test $\nabla_i\widetilde Q^{ij}=0$ with $z_\delta\beta_\varepsilon\theta_R B_j$, extended by zero where necessary. This is a smooth compactly supported test in the interior. Antisymmetry gives <a id="stat:twist-density"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \int z_\delta\beta_\varepsilon\theta_R\,\mathcal T_{\mathrm{tw}}
   &=-\int Q^{ij}B_j
         \partial_i(z_\delta\beta_\varepsilon\theta_R),
 \label{stat:twist-test}\\
 \mathcal T_{\mathrm{tw}}
   &:=\frac12 Q^{ij}(F_s)_{ij}
    =\frac{uW}{2}C^{ia}C^{jb}(F_s)_{ij}(F_s)_{ab}\geq0.
 \label{stat:twist-density}
\end{aligned}$$ The three error estimates are independent of the other cutoffs, which lie between zero and one. Take $R\to\infty$, $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, and $\delta\downarrow0$, or use a diagonal sequence. Fatou’s lemma in <a href="#stat:twist-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:twist-test">[stat:twist-test]</a> gives $\int_{\mathcal U}\mathcal T_{\mathrm{tw}}=0$; its integrability need not be assumed beforehand. Since $C$ is positive definite on $\mathcal U$, $F_s=0$ there. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-63"></a>
#### The charged polynomial and the regular base attachment

<a id="stmt-stat-charged-polynomial"></a>
<div id="stat:charged-polynomial" class="lemma">

<a id="stat:W-polynomial"></a>
**Lemma 58** (A global polynomial when the charge is nonzero). *If $Q\ne0$, then $p_b\ne0$, $0<|p_b|<1$, and $$W=1+p^2+d_0p
   =\left(1-\frac p{p_b}\right)(1-p_bp),
 \qquad d_0=-\frac{1+p_b^2}{p_b}.
 \label{stat:W-polynomial}$$ Moreover $p$ lies strictly between $0$ and $p_b$ in the interior, and $W>0$ everywhere in $\Omega^\circ$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-stat-charged-polynomial"><summary>Proof of stmt-stat-charged-polynomial</summary>

<a id="stat:potential-equation"></a>
*Proof.* The Maxwell equation in <a href="#stat:interface-fields" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:interface-fields">[stat:interface-fields]</a> gives $$\operatorname{div}(u^{-1}\nabla p)=0.
 \label{stat:potential-equation}$$ Its coefficients are smooth and elliptic on every compact subset of the interior. A positive maximum above both boundary and end values, or a negative minimum below them, would be attained in such a subset. The strong maximum principle therefore applies without requiring a uniform boundary bound for $u^{-1}$. If $p_b=0$, it would give $p=0$ and then $E=0$, contrary to $Q\ne0$. Otherwise it gives the stated strict interval for $p$.

<a id="stat:global-Killing-flux"></a>
<a id="stat:global-flux-laws"></a>
There is a useful flux which is smooth even at interior zeros of $W$: $$\mathcal D^i=u(d\xi^\flat)^{iz}
  =\frac1u\left(\nabla^iW+X^j(dX^\flat)^i{}_j\right).
 \label{stat:global-Killing-flux}$$ The Killing divergence identity and <a href="#stat:ricci-eigenvalue" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:ricci-eigenvalue">[stat:ricci-eigenvalue]</a> imply $$\operatorname{div}\mathcal D=2u^{-1}|dp|^2,
 \qquad \mathcal D\cdot X=u^{-1}dW(X).
 \label{stat:global-flux-laws}$$ On $\mathcal U$, Lemma <a href="#stat:vanishing-twist" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="stat:vanishing-twist">57</a> and <a href="#stat:global-Killing-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:global-Killing-flux">[stat:global-Killing-flux]</a> also give <a id="stat:positive-Killing-flux"></a>$$\mathcal D=\frac uW C\,dW.
 \label{stat:positive-Killing-flux}$$

<a id="stat:polynomial-energy"></a>
Choose $d_0$ as in <a href="#stat:W-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:W-polynomial">[stat:W-polynomial]</a>, and put $$P=W-1-p^2-d_0p,\qquad
 j=\mathcal D-(2p+d_0)u^{-1}\nabla p.$$ Equations <a href="#stat:potential-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:potential-equation">[stat:potential-equation]</a> and <a href="#stat:global-flux-laws" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:global-flux-laws">[stat:global-flux-laws]</a> give $\operatorname{div}j=0$. On $\mathcal U$, alignment implies $C\,dp=(W/u^2)\nabla p$; hence $$j=\frac uW C\,dP,
 \qquad dP(j)\geq u^{-1}|dP|^2.
 \label{stat:polynomial-energy}$$ Indeed the axial eigenvalue of $(u/W)C$ is $1/u$, and its transverse eigenvalues are $u/W\geq1/u$. This also covers points where $X=0$. At an interior zero of $W$, $dW=0$ and $X\ne0$. Alignment makes $dp$ parallel to $X$, and <a href="#stat:global-flux-laws" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:global-flux-laws">[stat:global-flux-laws]</a> gives $\mathcal D\cdot dp=0$. Thus <a href="#stat:polynomial-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:polynomial-energy">[stat:polynomial-energy]</a> holds there as well, with equality in its second assertion.

The current $j$ is bounded up to $S$. This is immediate when $u>0$ there. In the other case $dW=O(s)$ and $X^j(dX^\flat)_{ij}=O(s^3)$, so the quotient in <a href="#stat:global-Killing-flux" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:global-Killing-flux">[stat:global-Killing-flux]</a> extends smoothly by $u=sa$. The other current is $u^{-1}\nabla p=(k-1)E$, already smooth. Since $P|_S=0$, a distance cutoff gives a boundary error tending to zero. At infinity, $$P=O_2(r^{-q_0}),\qquad j=O(r^{-1-q_0}),$$ so the cutoff error in integrating $P\operatorname{div}j$ is $O(R^{n-2-2q_0})$. Testing with the product of the boundary and end cutoffs used above, and using nonnegativity in <a href="#stat:polynomial-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:polynomial-energy">[stat:polynomial-energy]</a>, gives $$\int_{\Omega^\circ}u^{-1}|dP|^2=0.$$ Thus $P$ is constant, and its limit at infinity is zero. This proves the first equality in <a href="#stat:W-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="stat:W-polynomial">[stat:W-polynomial]</a>.
